    • Einstein’s Lost Legacy

    A CRITICAL REVIEW

    of

    CONTEMPORARY PHYSICS AND COSMOLOGY

    by

    N. Vivian (Viv) Pope and Anthony D. Osborne

    Affiliation:

    School of Computing and Mathematics, Keele University U.K.

    Any views expressed in this book are solely the views of the authors.

    Works by the same authors

    See website www.poams.org , section Seminal Publications

    WHY THIS BOOK?

    In March 1954, there took place a short and unique piece of private correspondence between the then seventy-five years old Albert Einstein, of the Institute of Advanced Study, Princeton University, USA, and Viv Pope, an ordinary twenty-three year old telephone lineman who was working in the hills and valleys of Wales. That letter from Einstein is now stored in the Archives Department of the Civic Centre, Swansea, UK, valued at several thousand pounds.

    For half a century this letter, with its significance for science, was virtually lost to the public where it rightfully belonged because, to her horror, the Swansea Archivist, when she was first alerted to its existence, found it languishing in a volume of papers kept lying around in an attic unprotected from the atmospheric rigours of so many summers and winters. On her urging it was promptly removed from the attic and deposited in the air-conditioned atmosphere of the Swansea County Archives Department, where it is now kept as a treasured item to be read only under staff supervision.

    What makes that letter so important? What was its ‘legacy’? It was that in grappling with Einstein’s argument the young telephone lineman discovered a whole new, radically different, much simpler and more philosophically coherent approach to relativity than the one which has since become standard in contemporary physics and cosmology. Unfortunately, that correspondence could not be sustained because Einstein’s health deteriorated and he died the following year. However, the problem Einstein’s letter had raised in the lineman’s mind led to his remaining half century’s dedicated and unrelenting effort to solve the problem that the letter had posed. This was in a more or less ‘backroom’ development which, in retrospect, may be seen as an unintended preparation for what more and more scientists and cosmologists, in view of certain failures in contemporary Physics and Astronomy, are now expressing as the urgent need for a ‘New Physics’. As some see it, our extant physics and cosmology have become even more bizarre than the Elephants and Turtle cosmology of the ancient Hindus, depicted on the cover of this book. For more than fifty years following that unique correspondence between Einstein and an un-academic telephone lineman, this alternative approach to relativistic physics and cosmology has been developed, honed, tried and tested both intra- and extramurally.

    What was it that transpired between these two very different men all those years ago that could now be worth that much money today? The answer lies in the following pages.

    ABOUT THE AUTHORS

    (Back cover)

    Neville Vivian (Viv) Pope, Born January 1931, worked for almost twenty years in the telecommunications industry as a telephone lineman. In 1965, aged 34, he left to become a mature-age student at the University of North Wales, Bangor. Graduating in Philosophy of Science, he then became a lecturer at the Further Education College in Burton-on-Trent, Staffordshire where he taught Liberal Studies and Philosophy to adult students. He was also Tutor/Counsellor for the Open University. He took early retirement from these posts in order to concentrate on his

    researches into conceptual problems of modern physics. He published in MENSA and corresponded, as a young man, with Albert Einstein, in 1954. He had extensive correspondence with philosophers and scientists such as Alfred Ayer, Karl Popper, Herman Bondi, Gilbert Ryle, and others. He lectured internationally and published numerous papers and books on the subject of conceptualisation in science, both on his own and in collaboration with Anthony Osborne. Viv Pope was married in 1953 and had three daughters and eight grandchildren. Throughout his married life, his wife, Alison Mary, was his constant support and research assistant.

    Anthony D. Osborne, Born January 1955, gained his Ph.D at City University, London for his thesis, Gravitation and Dynamical Systems. For more than thirty years, he has taught Relativity to Final Year Mathematics Students at Keele University, Staffordshire, where he is, currently, Senior Lecturer in Mathematics. His textbook for students, entitled Complex Variables and Their Applications, was published by Addison Wesley Longman in 1999. His collaboration with Viv Pope began in 1982, and their first joint paper on the Normal Realist

    Approach to Relativity, appeared in 1987. Together with Pope he has published books and papers on conceptual issues of science, both ancient and modern. Anthony is married with one son.

Amongst these influences were long discussions between Pope and the philosophical pragmatist, Karl Popper, as well as the following: the astrophysicist Hermann Bondi, P. M. Davidson and M. R. Hopkins, both of the Physics Department, Swansea University, Gareth Evans and J. R. Far from least among these influences was that of Pope’s father, telephone engineer, amateur astronomer and philosopher, Donald Edward Pope.

    November 2014

    INTRODUCTION

    Physics: Today’s ‘Tower of Babel’

    This book is a sequel to several publications on a single theme. The theme is that the time is now ripe for a radical change in thinking about contemporary physics, which, apart from having become too arcane, over-intellectualised and jargonised, is characterised by its philosophical rootlessness and theoretical anarchy.

    Modern Physics and Cosmology have parted company with Commonsense to the extent that they now scarcely share the same language. A fresh opportunity presents itself, however, of mending this situation. This is by reconnecting Physics to its roots in Natural Philosophy by means of the method of Linguistic Analysis developed by the commonsense philosophers at the first half of the 20th century in the tradition set by the physics philosopher, Ernst Mach and his follower, Ludwig Wittgenstein. What makes this possible is that recent developments have taken place in those Educationally disconnected disciplines of ‘Physics’ and ‘Philosophy’ which should now, logically, reunite them under the single, original heading of Natural Philosophy. For nigh on a century since this arbitrary bifurcation of disciplines was imposed, those two equally truncated thought-systems have been held apart by mutual ignorance of what was happening in each other’s territory. These separate developments might be distinguished, one might say, by the names of ‘Einstein’ on the one hand and ‘Wittgenstein’ on the other.

    The issue between these two factions is, basically, that of language. In the one faction is the more or less uncontrolled, centuries-long build-up of theoretical esotery and associated jargon, whereas in the other the main concern is to preserve the commonsense coherence of evolving language. This is by winnowing out of our developing scientific language the theoretical chaff, as it were, from the empirical wheat – in effect to offset the proliferation of theoretical jargon and bring Physics back under the jurisdiction of commonsense logic and literacy.

    This ‘Linguistic’, or logically paring-down movement in Philosophy begins with the observational positivism, or radical empiricism of Ernst Mach who, as a professional physics-philosopher stood with a foot in each camp. Mach’s approach to Physics was to discover and logically determine the most efficient, least elaborated interpretations of natural phenomena and render these in the plainest possible language.

    This observationist (or phenomenalist) approach of Mach became the inspiration for the observational relativism of his philosophical protégé, Einstein. However, the way in which Einstein sought to develop Mach’s phenomenalism was, to say the least, not approved by Mach, for whom any talk of hidden and inscrutable, ‘underlying mechanisms’ such as those of ‘atoms’ and light travelling between them in vacuo was anathema. For instance, his view of Einstein’s Theory of Relativity and the contemporary atomic theory is expressed as follows:

    ‘I gather from the publications which have reached me and especially from my correspondence, that I am becoming regarded as the forerunner of relativity… I must, however, as assuredly disclaim to be a forerunner of the Relativists as I personally reject the atomistic doctrine of the present-day school’ [1].

    The main aim of this book, therefore, is to review the relevance of Mach’s physics-philosophy to both modern Relativity and Quantum Theory, with particular relevance to the unresolved conflict between those two theories in the matter of instantaneous versus delayed action-at-a-distance. In so doing, we provide a candidate for what Bertrand Russell regarded as the missing, long-awaited ‘philosophy appropriate to quantum theory’.

    Although this book is in some ways a sequel to earlier works on the Neo-Machian physics-philosophy of Normal Realism, it is intended to be self-contained, and the reader should be able to follow the arguments without any previous knowledge of these earlier publications. Our subject is that of the nature of fundamental physical phenomena, including matter, space, time, motion and light. This is with special attention to the concept of ‘light-speed’ in the Second Postulate of Einstein’s Theory of Relativity, which asserts the constancy, for all observers, of the ‘speed of light in vacuo’. We also re-examine, from the same Neo-Machian standpoint, all concepts of in vacuo ‘field-forces’, of ‘gravitational,’ ‘electrostatic’, ‘magnetostatic’ and ‘nuclear’ conception.

    In short, then, the prescription for change offered by this book is to make the best of this rare opportunity of amalgamating the recent developments in Physics and Philosophy in a conceptual restructuring of our fractured Modern Physics along the philosophical lines of the quantised, or sense-datum relativism of Einstein’s philosophical predecessor, Mach. The reader will find that the text is liberally interspersed with explanatory end-notes and references to earlier publications on this theme. However, these should not halt readers in their tracks. They are there only to provide supplementary information where it might be felt necessary. Otherwise it is hoped that the flow of the argument may be followed with a minimum of interruption.

    CHAPTER 1

    The Primary Purpose of Physical Science

    It is generally accepted that the primary purpose of theoretical physics is to construct theories (models) which are capable of making predictions in agreement with current observations and which also make new predictions. It is regarded as obligatory within the profession to convince students not to think of any such theory as providing an absolutely true description of physical reality but rather to regard it as no more than a tool for explaining physical phenomena. To quote W. C. D. Whetham [2]:

    ‘The function of science is merely to construct a consistent set of phenomena. Whether that model corresponds with the ultimate reality behind phenomena, whether indeed there be anything answering to what we might think of as ultimate reality, is a question for metaphysics, not for Natural Science. An imaginary model of the whole of Nature would be too complex for the mind to grasp.’

    This was, originally, the view of the science-philosopher Sir Karl Popper: that a theory is only a ‘good’ theory if it is capable of making new predictions which are ultimately testable in observation and/or experiment. If such predictions turn out to be false, says Popper, then the theory fails and has to be eliminated as soon as possible, not held on to for reasons of sentimental attachment to tradition.

    It is also professionally assumed and taught that the only way to construct physical concepts is through observation involving quantitative measurement. The implication of this is that in science, questions which have no connection with direct observation and measurement are unanswerable and therefore pointless. It is also supposed that any physical theory is, at best, only transitory and may be replaced or amended whenever new phenomena are observed. A theory, it is claimed, can be proved false by finding even a single observation that disagrees with its predictions, although, more often, in fact, a new theory is ‘saved’ as an extension or amendment of such a falsified theory.

    A nice example of how scientific theories are proposed and developed on the basis of the observations available at the time, is that of the movements of the planets. The Greek philosopher and scientist, Aristotle, was one of the first, at around 340 BC, to argue that the Earth is spherical in shape rather than flat. His argument was based on observations concerning eclipses of the Moon, the position of the North Star and ships sailing over the horizon. At about the same time, another Greek scientist, Heracleides of Pontus, was the first to propose that the Earth is rotating on its axis. This proposal was based on his observation that day and night are caused by parts of the Earth facing alternately towards and away from the Sun.

    However, observation alone does not always suffice. There is also the matter of interpretation to consider. The Greeks believed that the Earth was at the centre of the known ‘universe’ and this belief was the basis of the cosmological model constructed by one of the most influential of Greek astronomers and geographers of his time, Claudius Ptolemy (circa 100 – 170 AD). In this Ptolemaic model of the solar system, the ‘heavenly bodies’ orbited our Earth in wheels-within-wheels of perfect circular motion. It was not until the early 16$^{th}$ century that this model was challenged. Copernicus was the first to claim that, in contrast to all appearances and the Ptolemaic highly complicated geocentric interpretation of these appearances, it was the Sun, not the Earth, that lay at the centre of the planetary system and that, consequently, the planets orbited the Sun in what Copernicus supposed were simple circles. This was not quite correct, as it did not explain the motions of the planets as precisely as were actually observed, and it was only with the work of Kepler and Galileo, a century later, that Copernicus’ Sun-centred model was taken seriously. Kepler, through more meticulous observation, found that the planets orbited the Sun in ellipses, not circles as Copernicus believed.

    In the 17$^{th}$ century, Newton proposed his own model of the solar system, based on his three ‘laws’ of motion and his (fourth) ‘law’ of ‘universal gravitation’ to explain the known orbits of the planets. His theory was based on an entirely novel idea of a universal in vacuo ‘gravitational force of attraction’ between bodies on account of their mass, and this was the first theory capable of producing an advanced mathematical model of planetary motion. However, it was later found that Mercury’s orbit did not follow exactly a Keplerian ellipse, thereby indicating that Newtonian theory, in its historical turn, was not quite correct.

    Newton’s theoretical ‘gravitational force’, therefore, was succeeded by Einstein’s General Theory of Relativity, which first appeared in 1915. In this theory, the natural paths of satellites and planets are explained, not by Newton’s ‘gravitational force’ but in terms of what Einstein conceived as peculiarities of space itself in the form of a space-time continuum. This dispenses altogether with Newton’s

    ‘gravitation’ in favour of a purely geometrical account of planetary motion in which those motions may be depicted simply as ‘warps’ in the grain of space-time due to the presence of mass objects, like the knots in a plank of wood. Unlike Newton’s theory, Einstein’s geometrodynamical theory deduces the correct motion of Mercury and has, potentially, vastly more predictive power. However, the advent of General Relativity did not mean that Newton’s ‘gravitational’ theory had to be scrapped. It is known still to provide, except for motions comparable with the ‘speed of light’, a very accurate, albeit no more than approximate, model of orbital motion. Indeed, despite being superseded by Einstein’s General Theory it is Newton’s much simpler and more intuitive ‘gravitational’ model that is employed by NASA’s scientists in calculating the trajectories of their space-probes – even though Newton’s concept of in vacuo ‘gravitation’ has been proved redundant.

    Nevertheless, it is widely agreed that Einstein’s General Relativity will have to be amended or replaced eventually since it is currently inconsistent with Quantum Theory, the study of physical phenomena on the micro-scale. Significantly, at this present time of writing, neither Standard Newtonian theory nor General Relativity can explain certain anomalies that have arisen both in NASA’s space programmes and in modern cosmology. For decades, these anomalies have resisted all attempts at a natural solution. This is to the extent that NASA’s spokesman, John Anderson, has even suggested the need for ‘a new physics’. As he says, ‘In the unlikely event that there is new physics, one does not want to miss it because one had the wrong mind-set.’ This might, of course, have been said at every stage in the history of Theoretical Physics, not excluding the present one.

    However, Popper’s pragmatical view that any theory not capable of making physical predictions is not truly scientific is unsatisfactory. This is not least because it leads to a major problem with modern physics; namely, that on pragmatical grounds many of its predictions are extremely expensive of time and effort – and, of course, money – to verify. A lot of expensive experimental physics has, in the past, provided practical applications which have offered benefits to the community at large. Nevertheless, at some stage, especially in the present economic climate, the line has to be drawn between sponsoring those experiments which have practical implications and those experiments aimed at supporting the more esoteric predictions of some fashionable theory. This is particularly true, not only of current astrophysics and cosmology but also, at the other extreme, on the micro-scale with the study of sub- atomic particles. For example, vast sums of money have already been spent in trying to detect ‘gravity waves’ or ‘gravitons’, ‘dark matter’, etc., on the basis of theoretical predictions which, over the years, have continually escaped verification.

    Nevertheless, Popper’s view is the one taken by the scientific community at large. Even if a theory provides an explanation of observed phenomena that is simpler than that of competing theories, it will not be seen as a viable alternative unless it is capable of making new predictions. However, there are other criteria which Popper’s view omits but which need to be considered when developing a physical theory. Some of these other criteria are every bit as important as that of purely pragmatical, predictive power. One of the most important of these is the principle commonly known as ‘Ockham’s Razor’, which emphasises the need for conceptual economy in the construction of a theory. This rules that one should not increase, beyond what is strictly necessary, the number of ideas required to explain a phenomenon. This principle was first formally introduced into science in the 14$^{th}$ century by William of Ockham, but the idea can be traced back many centuries to the ancient Greeks who showed a marked desire for simplicity of explanation $^{[3]}$. The general idea behind this economising on conception is that since nature does not have the guile to spin theories of itself, the less theory there is in our interpretations of physical phenomena, the closer we get to a picture of nature as it truly is.

    Plainly, this principle of Ockham’s Razor has been ignored by the constructors of some of the more bizarre modern physical theories that the media disseminate at this present time, such as the ‘Big Bang’, ‘Black Holes’, ‘wormholes in space’, ‘superstrings’, ‘gravitons’, ‘dark matter’, the ‘God Particle’, and so on. In our view, a scientific theory should, ideally, conform to Ockham’s Razor. This also accords with Mach’s positivistic criterion of the plainest and most unadorned descriptions of natural phenomena. However, it must be noted that sometimes, in doing so, some of the predictive value of a theory may be lost. Take, for example, the paradigm shift from the Ptolemaic to the Copernican cosmology. In the earlier days of that revolution, many of the meticulous observational records of the Ptolemaic astronomers, cast in terms of epicycles, equants and so on were laid aside in favour of the more intuitive Copernican interpretation. However, despite its conceptual superiority, the Copernican System, as already mentioned, was much less exact than its predecessor. The same could happen with any future theory that might do for Einstein, say, what Copernicus did for Ptolemy. This means that for pragmatic purposes, a new conceptual paradigm and an old one which is incommensurable with it might have to exist, confusingly, side-by-side for as long as it takes for the conflict to settle.

    In view of the chronic squandering of resources in attempts to verify predictions of modern over-intellectualised and purely fashionable physical theories, as mentioned above, in this book we suggest that Ockham’s Razor, originally a principle of conceptual economy, should now be extended to include financial economy also. As an example of the employment of Ockham’s Razor in our work, it is our opinion that what is traditionally reported as the ‘discovery of the speed of light in space’ by Olaus Römer in 1676 was, in truth, a misreporting and over-elaboration of the bare facts. As Professor Sir Hermann Bondi and others have since ascertained, the most economical interpretation of that discovery should have been that what was actually discovered was simply a constant numerical relation, $c$, between our customary measures of observational distance and time – as if someone had ‘discovered’ that there is a constant relation of British inches to the Continental metre. Founded on the Neo-Machian, observationist philosophy of Normal Realism [4], this key conceptual ‘flipover’ from interpreting $c$ as a ‘velocity’ towards regarding it as no more than a dimensional constant, forms the basis of our proposed, much simplified and more economical replacement of Einstein’s Theory of Relativity with a unified synthesis of Relativity and Quantum Theory.

    As another example of our employment of Ockham’s Razor, and in company with General Relativity, Newton’s idea of a ‘gravitational force’ acting in the vacuum of space is shown to be conceptually redundant. That idea was created by Newton’s invention of a ‘law’ that a body with no force acting upon it forever travels in a straight line. Then, to explain the fact that nowhere do freely-moving bodies travel in accordance with that law – as witnessed in the motions of satellites around the Earth and that of the Earth and other planets around the Sun, etc. – Newton conjured-up the concept of a universal tension, an invisible ‘gravitational force’, acting across the void between bodies so that they are attracted towards one another and prevented from travelling in their assumed straight lines. In our approach, known as the Pope-Osborne Angular Momentum Synthesis (POAMS), these two mutually negating and therefore cancelling Newtonian ‘Laws’, his first Law of rectilinear motion and his ‘Law of Universal Gravitation’, are replaced by the Aristotelian postulate that all continuous natural motion is essentially cyclic or orbital. This removes any need to assume that there is some invisible and intangible ‘gravitational force’ responsible for that orbital motion. We show that conservation of angular momentum, in itself, is sufficient to explain all that Newton’s formalism explains without any supporting esotery [5].

    Another important consideration when constructing a physical theory is the philosophy underlying that theory. It is this consideration which, for the most part, nowadays, is sadly ignored. A lack of underlying philosophy can lead to the most fanciful and bizarre ‘anything goes’ situations which may have little or nothing to do with direct observation. This point was at least appreciated by Bertrand Russell, who, in 1946, in his book, A History of Western Philosophy, wrote:

    ‘The philosophy appropriate to quantum theory…has not yet been adequately developed. I suspect that it will demand even more radical departures from the traditional doctrine of space and time than those demanded by the theory of relativity.’

    Now, over sixty years on, no such ‘philosophy appropriate to quantum theory’ has so far emerged. Theories have been presented in abundance, but no systematic philosophical analysis in keeping with this view of Russell’s has been seriously attempted – until now, that is.

    The basis of this New Philosophy of Normal Realism is Mach’s relativism, which has its roots in ‘English Empiricism’, or observational relativism in the tradition begun by the seventeenth century philosopher, John Locke. This empiricist way of thinking was developed during the eighteenth century by George Berkeley and David Hume. It was then taken up on the Continent by Immanuel Kant and followed, in the nineteenth century, by Mach. Early in the twentieth century, Mach’s ‘sense-datum’ (sense-quantum) philosophy became the basis of the next phase in the development of Empiricism. According to this Machian view, all sensory and instrumental information is quantised in terms of discrete amounts of what he called ‘sense-data’. The most prominent in this movement were the science-philosophers of what became known as the Vienna Circle, most notable among whom were Moritz Schlick, Alfred Ayer and especially, Ludwig Wittgenstein. In this so-called ‘Logical Positivist’ development, Mach’s ‘sense-data’ became ‘atomic’ statements about ‘incorrigible’ sensations, expressed in a highly structured ‘scientific’ language (metalanguage) of purely logical propositions. However, in both Ayer’s and Wittgenstein’s later work, this ‘Logical Positivism’ was abandoned and their empiricism proceeded more along the commonsense (Ordinary Language) lines developed throughout the twentieth century by G. E. Moore, John L. Austin and

    Gilbert Ryle. The influence of this particular tradition was carried through, eventually, to the University of Wales, Swansea, in the 1960s, by Wittgenstein’s ‘right-hand man’, Rush Rhees. This came to influence one of the authors of this book, the Swansea-born science-philosopher, Viv Pope. His espousal of this Linguistic method in the cause of correctly interpreting physical phenomena led to further critical developments in the ‘English Empiricist’ tradition of commonsense philosophy after Moore, et al. This philosophy now takes the form of Neo-Machian Normal Realism. As stated previously, its physics offshoot, the Pope-Osborne Angular Momentum Synthesis (POAMS), replaces Newton’s ‘gravitation’ with automatically paired and balanced angular momentum relations between freely orbiting bodies $^{[6]}$. So, although originally classified as ‘English’ Empiricism, some of the main influences in this movement, right up to this present time, have been German, Austrian, Scottish, Irish and, latterly, Welsh.

    Each phase in the development of this tradition has been marked by some rational criticism of its predecessor. This critical approach is continued in this book and in other publications by the authors and their supporters. During its developmental process, ‘English Empiricism’ has progressed along a course which is now almost entirely separate from what we think of as the standard orthodox physics which has evolved in the classical tradition of Democritus, Descartes, Galileo and Newton. Unfortunately, mostly confined, as it has so far been, to the cloisters of Academia (due to the modern Educational apartheid separating Arts and Science), this alternative empiricist approach to natural phenomena is virtually unknown amongst the physics community in which practically nothing is known of Mach beyond his ‘Mach Number’ in aeronautical engineering and ‘Mach’s Principle’ in astronomy and cosmology. Not even in view of the almost terminal mystification of modern physics has there been any true logical dialogue between the jealously divided, so-called ‘realists’ of physics and what they regard as the ‘airy-fairy philosophers’.

    In Normal Realism, however, we have what is probably the first serious attempt, since Mach, to combine Science and Philosophy in the interests of both, as an altogether new approach towards the so far insoluble problems and mysteries of modern physics. This book partakes of the Machian legacy, drawing it towards what is hoped will be the logical culmination of the ‘English Empiricist’ programme of scientific philosophy. This is to complete that Empiricist revolution by taking, as it were, our customary physics off its Democritan headstand and allowing it, perhaps for the first time, to stand more comfortably on its philosophical feet. In other words, this programme is to explore the possibility of a complete paradigm ‘flip-over’ from the classical Rationalist, or Theoretical-Fundamentalist tradition of physics, towards that of Objective Empiricism, or Phenomenalism. This has nothing to do with the psychologistic phenomenologie of ‘Continental Empiricism’ after Edmund Husserl (1859-1938), which is distinct from and incompatible with that of ‘English Empiricism’, particularly in the latter’s most recent form of Normal Realism. This Neo-Machian Normal Realism may be described as an observer-centred relativistic approach to physics in which Mach’s ‘sense-data’ become the sense-instrument data identified as the quanta of the physicist, Max Planck. In these terms, all the physical dimensions, properties and qualities of physical phenomena are relativistically projected by the observer out of directly perceived patterns and sequences of these quanta, similar to the way in which the length, breadth, depth and motion of a video scenario are projected by the viewer directly from the patterns and sequences of screen pixels. (In Normal Realism this is sometimes referred to as the ‘cinematic’ model of quantum interaction.)

    It was in grappling with Einstein’s reply to his letter in 1954 that Pope was convinced of two things: one was that there is no such thing as the ‘clock paradox’, as Einstein’s letter plainly demonstrated, and the other, after much head-scratching, was that Einstein’s well-known Second Postulate, that ‘light travels at a constant speed $c$ in vacuo’ is logically redundant, a fact which, it seems, more and more members of the science community are now beginning to recognise [7]. Eventually, Pope dispensed with that Second Postulate of Einstein’s in favour of a conclusion which, as it transpired, was concurrent with Featherstone’s [8] and Bondi’s view, namely, that $c$ need not be thought of as a ‘speed’ at all, but simply as what Bondi called a distance-time ‘conversion factor’ (in Pope’s description, a ‘dimensional constant’). Pope saw that this was tantamount to a discovery that Einstein’s philosophically rootless Theory of Relativity can, for its missing philosophical sustenance, be grafted onto the relativistic philosophical stem of his (Einstein’s) mentor and critic, Mach. In Pope’s correspondence with Bondi, Bondi said that it was sufficient for him that this change in the interpretation of $c$ made it much simpler and easy to teach Relativity to his students, and that he would leave it to Pope, with his blessing, to develop the philosophical implications of that change in conception of $c$.

    CHAPTER 2

    Atoms and Space: the Conceptual History

    2.1. The origin of Atomic Physics

    The initial development of what we know as ‘science’ as a discipline is generally attributed to the ancient Greeks [9]. Previous civilisations simply observed facts without proposing any underlying mechanisms or theories. It was the Greeks who first sought possible explanations for and connections between observed phenomena. They believed that all phenomena could be explained by reasoning of some sort. In doing so, they attempted to erase any ‘supernatural’ elements from their interpretations of the phenomena and replace them with the concept of rational proof. The Greek word logos, meaning ‘reason’ or ‘word’ encapsulates their approach to science. Theories were based on sets of postulates, which, in turn, were based on observation, and on deductions made from these postulates. The exception to this view was that of the ‘Sceptics’. These argued against analysing phenomena in this way, since they believed that observations inevitably involve the senses, which they regarded as unreliable as a source of any true knowledge of physical reality. For them, Reason was something apart from and superior to sensation, not only more reliable than observation but also, in some cases, self-sufficient and independent of anything to do with physical phenomena. In the long history of Philosophy, these champions of ‘Pure Reason’ are referred to as Rationalists, as opposed to the other, the phenomenalist faction, called Empiricists.

    The postulates proposed by certain schools of thought amongst these ancient Greeks appear very strange to us now. For example, the ‘four-element’ theorists believed that physical phenomena were determined by the four ‘elements’ of earth, air, fire and water. This school of thought was founded by Empedocles and further developed by Aristotle.

    The tradition of natural philosophy which pointed the way, right at the start, for what we nowadays call ‘physics’ was set by the Rationalist philosopher, Democritus (circa 460-370 B.C.). For him, the sole and primary reality was an unobserved and unobservable material ‘atomic substance’ of which, he reasoned, all things are made. Anything that is not matter or mechanical motion among atoms, he taught, is illusion – mere ideas, or ‘conventions’ as he called them. Like the Sceptics, he put sensory perception into that category of mere illusion.

    Plainly, then, the atomic materialism of Democritus on which our Western science is founded is not a product of sensation or observation but a product of purely theoretical or speculative Reason. This theoretical materialism of Democritus began with the Rationalist teachings of Parmenides (6$^{th}$ - 5$^{th}$ century B.C.), the Eleatic philosopher who reasoned that by its very definition ‘what is not’ (i.e., literally, nothing) cannot be (exist). In other words, the Eleatic philosophers thought that the notion of an identifiable ‘nothing’ actually existing was logically self-contradictory. From this they reasoned that since nothing, as such, is literally non-existent (i.e., ‘is not’), then everything that is, or exists, forms a permanent, indestructible and changeless Unity, or Whole, with no gaps or voids. This they called The One – what we would nowadays call the ‘Universe’. They regarded this holistic One, or ‘What Is,’ as the basic Substance, literally the absolute ‘standing under’ of everything, the logical continuum, or Substratum of all true existence. ‘What is not’, they concurred, was simply nothing, which it made no sense even to speak about as something that is. Regardless of how strange this sort of purely Rationalist argument may seem to us nowadays, it was what led to and still underlies the way in which our physical science, even today, is formulated. However, in order best to understand the character of our modern physics it is instructive, by means of logical analysis, to trace the history of that science right back to its logical roots.

    2.1.1. The meaning of ‘to exist’: the Eleatic legacy

    Now we all know what it means ‘to exist’. Or do we? We take the meaning of ‘existence’ so much for granted that it seems to us as something so completely obvious that it makes no sense even to talk about it. It is something we accept without being any more aware of it than fish are aware of water. The origins of the meaning of ‘existence’ have been lost in the mists of scientific history. So, if ever asked to explain what ‘existence’ means we are left floundering. The best we can say is that ‘existence’ means … well, ‘to exist’, which is a complete tautology.

    What, then, does it mean, to ‘exist’? Only at our peril can we ignore the fact that this is essentially a question purely of language, hence to think of it as having no relevance to practical science. The plain fact is that all concepts whatsoever, including those of physical science, are framed in language, whether spoken or unspoken. If our language is askew, then so are our thoughts and actions. This is every bit as non-trivial in physics contexts as our linguistic distinctions between ‘guilty’ and ‘innocent’ are in judiciary contexts, or between ‘left’ and ‘right’ in traffic directions. Imagine how disastrous it would be for a pharmacist to fail to distinguish between the bottles labelled ‘sodium chloride’ and ‘sodium chlorate’, or for a scientist not to distinguish between ‘platinum’ and ‘plutonium’ in a nuclear laboratory. Anyone who really thinks about it can therefore scarcely dismiss as ‘trivial’ the vital importance of language in framing our interpretations of physical phenomena, even in the most practical contexts of pure physics. Indeed, it is plain that without attention to the proper uses of language, we would be lucky to survive for more than a day, especially as drivers or pedestrians navigating roads and motorways.

    Obviously, then, there is no such thing as ‘existence’ on its own apart from language. However, as already stated, for Democritus, ‘existence’ was something he imagined as insensible and independent of language, and that is the way, almost by automatic reflex, we tend to think of ‘existence’ in physics today. Only fairly recently have philosophers realised that ‘existence’ is, in itself, essentially an item of language and that, like any other such item, it has its correct or incorrect function in that linguistic context.

    This linguistic function of ‘existence’ was not lost on the original Greek logicians. For them, ‘existence’ meant simply the ‘what is’, or ‘substance’ of things. Take, for instance, a statement such as ‘Socrates is short and stout.’ In language, the subject of that statement is, of course, ‘Socrates’ and the predicate, or predicate system is whatever is signified by the words ‘short and stout’, plus whatever else may be said about Socrates by definition or description, such as ‘male’, ‘bearded’ etc. Now just so long as the predicate (or predicates) of that or any other such statement consists of words with recognised meaning – such as, in this instance, ‘short’, ‘stout’, ‘male’, ‘married’, ‘Greek’, ‘stone-mason’ and whatever – was linked to that subject by some variant or other of the verb ‘to be’ (i.e., ‘is’, ‘was’, ‘will be’, ‘might be’, ‘might have been’, etc.), existence of that subject was assured for those Greeks. For example, to say ‘Socrates is (or was) short and stout’ was sufficient to assert the existence of Socrates as the subject, or substance of that statement. What may be difficult for us to realise, nowadays, is that in this basic way of thinking, any statement in which that copula ‘is’ is either explicit or implicit, is sufficient to bestow existence on that subject. This is what the philosopher Wittgenstein meant by his – some think puzzling – statement ‘The World is Language’. Even statements such as ‘there are no such things as ghosts,’ or ‘there can never be a good Fascist’, bestow existence on the subject of the statement by the variants of those words written in italics, such as ‘are’, ‘can never be’, etc., which automatically signify the existence of those subjects in the sense of pure language. After all, it scarcely makes sense to talk about the ‘existence’ of something about which nothing whatsoever can be said.

    There is, then, nothing intrinsically puzzling about what it means ‘to exist’. So long as we refrain from waxing metaphysical about it, the meaning of ‘to exist’ is perfectly definitive and clear. It is simply what is conferred on any named subject, for example, ‘Socrates’, by some variant or other of the verb ‘to be’, linking that subject to any named predicate or predicate system, such as ‘short’, ‘stout’, etc. But what does it mean to ‘wax metaphysical about it’? It means an insatiable urge for us nowadays, in practical-science terms, to look behind the obvious for something more tangible than mere ‘definitions in language’. This was a fundamental mistake that Democritus made in thinking of ‘existence’ as referring to some underlying and ineffable state of ‘being’, something ‘in itself’ abiding in some metaphysical domain of its own, behind, beyond and independent of all observation, language and thought – a pretty paradox if ever there was! But how did that metaphysic come about? The answer is as follows.

    There is something which philosophers and logicians call a category mistake. This is a failure to realise that in common language there is more than just one category of existence. The category of what we call the ‘physical’ is just one of these. There are others. For instance, Lewis Carroll’s Cheshire Cat exists as surely as do ‘electrons’ and ‘protons’. The only difference is that the Cheshire Cat exists in the language-category of ‘fiction’, whereas ‘electrons’ exist in the separate category of ‘matter’. Over the millennia, philosophers such as Aristotle, Immanuel Kant and moderns such as Gilbert Ryle, have been at pains to distinguish the most natural categorical divisions of language in the same way that naturalists like Darwin, et al. sought to classify, divide and categorise the various species of flora and fauna. In philosophical terms, failing to recognise these category distinctions is like some innocent wandering naked in a jungle unaware of the natural distinctions between such creatures as ‘predators’ and ‘prey’, or between what is ‘edible’ and ‘inedible’, or ‘poisonous’.

    The first and most monumental category mistake behind the thinking of Democritus was that of Parmenides, who moved from Ionia, in Asia Minor to found his Rationalist school of philosophy at Elea near the foot of Italy. Parmenides’ profound category mistake was somehow to convey to his followers the impression that there was only one true category of existence, or substance, which some of his more materialistically minded followers began to think of as matter. As Plato described it, these ‘materialists’ would point to every tactile object, ‘every particular stick or stone’, as manifesting existence, as opposed to abstractions such as the forms of things, ideas, concepts, etc., to which existence was arbitrarily denied. And it was this ‘concrete’, or tactile interpretation of ‘existence’ that gradually prevailed in what became known as materialism, or physis as the Greeks called it, as opposed to logos, or ‘language’ [10].

    2.1.2. The ancient Problem of Motion and Change

    This created a problem for the materialists, because without any spaces or gaps (nothings) into which matter can move or change position, that whole Eleatic unity had to be solid, permanent and unchanging. In other words, that Eleatic One had to be completely static and immobile. This assumed continuum of being was the archetype of permanent and indestructible ‘substance’ which became established from then on as matter. It implied a universal ‘motion-gridlock’, known as the ‘Eleatic stasis’, in which all existence was jammed solid, so that ‘what is not’ was nowhere.

    But, of course, this was nonsense, because motion and change are undeniable facts of nature. It led, in the materialist interpretation of ‘substance’, to Zeno’s notorious Paradoxes of Motion. Most well-known among these, perhaps, is the paradox of Achilles and the tortoise. As Aristotle described it, in a race, the quickest runner can never overtake the slowest. This is because – as it was reasoned – the pursuer must first reach the point from which the slowest started, which involves an infinite number of intermediate divisions of length which the pursuer needs to travel to catch up with the slowest runner. So this slowest runner must always stay ahead of the quickest [11]. These arguments of Zeno’s were meant to point the absurdity, as he saw it, of the idea of motion and thereby to confirm the Rationalist One-ness or absolute continuity or underlying gaplessness of all matter in the Eleatic way of thinking.

    2.1.3. Space, the reified ‘nothing’ of Democritus

    This materialist problem demanded a materialist solution. The search for that solution of the Eleatic problem of material immobility was tackled by Democritus. In order to accommodate the fact of motion and change, the nothing or void whose existence the Eleatics had denied somehow had to be reinstated. This Democritus did by introducing what has since become known as ‘space’, the physical vacuum or void. In this way, the ‘nothing’ became a ‘something’ which Democritus logically interposed between the ultimate bits of the Eleatic substance. These, he called ‘atoms’ (in Greek, meaning ‘indivisible’). In this way, the materially interpreted Eleatic One was shattered into space-separated ‘atomic’ fragments which moved and interacted mechanically with one another in the void (the reified nothing) to create the changing forms and constitutions of things that are witnessed on the level of ordinary physical phenomena [12].

    Note that, as has been discussed, all this is basically about language and the logical integrity thereof. In our preoccupations with the practicalities of everyday experience, especially in physics, this is something we all too easily forget. The same goes, of course, for the formalities of logic and mathematics, which are all integral elements in the function of language. Bearing this in mind, we see how this original Eleatic handling of language and its Democritan follow-on set the stage for the next two-and-a-half millennia of Physics as we know it. This was the tradition of atomic materialism, or ‘atomism’, which, around the turn of the 18th century was reinforced by the experimental findings of John Dalton. This established the ‘atomic’ basis upon which sciences, such as Physics and Chemistry, for example, were founded [13].

    In keeping with this mechanistic precept, it was supposed that our perceptions are caused by hidden mechanical processes among these inscrutable ‘atoms’, in collisions during which they cast off ‘atomic effluvia’, little images of themselves, (eidola in Greek and simulacra in Latin). These were the constituents of light which the Democritan physicists thought of as flying about everywhere, some of which ‘bits’ strike our eyes, and others collide with things in general – unless, of course, missing everything, they carry on forever. These theoretical light particles, the forerunners of what, after G. N. Lewis [14], Einstein eventually called ‘photons’, were conceived as the ultimate bits of sensory information that happen to impinge on our sense organs from an assumed mechanical and sub-phenomenal underworld of pure ‘matter’. Undoubtedly, this is why, nowadays, we find it so ‘natural’ to think of physical reality as residing in material ‘atoms’ and of our ideas or perceptions, by comparison, as no more than figmentary or illusory (that is, as ‘mere appearances’, or phenomena [15]). This was the metaphysic which, even now, either overtly or covertly – in any event, subliminally – militates against what we would otherwise plainly understand, according to its original meaning, as existence. It was this kind of ‘atomism’ which, in his notorious Preface, Mach spurned as ‘the atomistic doctrine of the present-day school’ [1].

    2.2. Atomism’s insoluble Knowledge Problem

    However, there is something very strange about this notion that the information on which all our knowledge of the world depends comes from atomic sources of which we have no knowledge whatsoever other than in terms of that very same information. In that case, how can we ever be sure that the world we assume is the source of all our knowledge really is, in itself, as we perceive and conceive it? Well, let’s face it, we can’t. This is a problem that has plagued academic philosophers for centuries in the name of the Epistemological Problem, or Problem of Knowledge. It is as though we had dealings with some person of repute, only to find that his reputation and all guarantees regarding it had been fabricated by that person himself. This is the situation with which our traditional science presents us, namely the question of how much, if at all, we can trust our senses and instrument systems to represent to us the physical world as it ‘really’ is behind the artificial ‘veil of perception’ that Democritus had put between ourselves and physical – i.e., material – reality. (It bears consideration that this ‘veil’ persists nowadays in what some physicists call ‘Einstein separation’.)

    2.3. The pragmatic solution of the Knowledge Problem

    In science, therefore, established as it now is so firmly on the Democritan tradition of atomic materialism, we are always suspended somewhere between trusting everything and trusting nothing that our senses and instruments tell us. However, physical science cannot operate at either of those extremes. Unable to obtain any direct, independent evidence of the reality of anything ‘behind the appearances’, our traditional physics is constrained to judge the validity of any one appearance only with reference to others, which is like a group of suspects in court, each vouching, on oath, for the innocence of all the others but with no independent evidence to back their story.

    This Problem of Knowledge, which has enervated generations of academic philosophers, has never been solved – not, that is, in the traditional language in which the Problem is couched. As for classical scientists, they have simply ignored it, regarding it as a problem for academic philosophy, having nothing to do with their own particular discipline which they quaintly regard as ‘realistic’. Scientific ‘realism’ proceeds, therefore, on the vague assumption that, by and large, everything is more or less ‘okay’, that whatever we observe, in our best, most direct and instrumental experiences of the world is ‘good enough’. It is taken for granted that the fund of sensory and instrumental data that has been accumulated throughout our scientific history provides a reliable conceptual template or paradigm of ‘realistic’ perception that matches the way things ‘really’ are in themselves behind the way they appear to us. This assumption that ‘seeing is believing’ persists even though there have been occasions when, spectacularly, our customary perceptions have been proved wrong. One thinks, for instance, of the time when people were confident that the Earth was flat, as it appeared to be; that the Sun goes around the Earth; that the whale is a fish and so on. These confidences, of course, did not last. Observations made by the ancient Greeks and, much later, with the voyages of exploration in the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries, showed that the Earth is round, not flat. Copernicus discovered that despite appearances to the contrary, the Sun and the other planets do not orbit the Earth but that our planet and all the other planets orbit the Sun. Biologists discovered that the whale is not a fish but a mammal … and so on. These and many other scientific revolutions have shown that, regardless of whatever confidence science may place in the ‘factual’ nature of its observations at any particular stage in its history – including right now, of course – that confidence, in itself, provides no guarantee that what appears to be so in the context of any contemporary paradigm or ‘standard model’ of perception, will remain so indefinitely. The simple fact is that in scientific perception and conception, nothing is ever absolutely immune to the possibility of radical revision or revolutionary change. In some cases, even the fundamental categories of existence may need to be revised and updated as, for instance, in the case of virus particles, which confound our category division between ‘animate’ and ‘inanimate’. The need for such radical revisions, from time to time means that the language of science is never absolutely fixed, not even at its very roots, but is continually undergoing evolutionary change, by the process of rational selection.

    2.4. A logical alternative to Democritan Atomism

    Such, then, is the predicament in which, inextricably, scientific so-called ‘realism’ finds itself. Without being able to place absolute trust in even our very best, most direct observations and the language we use to describe them, what price our knowledge of what we assume are the ultimate realities ‘underlying and inaccessible’ to those observations? The word ‘realism’ applied to that traditional scientific method is therefore, plainly, a misnomer. If we can never be absolutely sure of the truth of our perceptions, then how much less sure can we be of our ideas, or theories, of what underlies those perceptions? How can we be confident that such data as we may have of those ‘hidden atomic mechanisms’ and so on, are correctly interpreted when, in the last analysis they are no more than just ideas, or theories, framed in the language of our culture?

    This takes us right back to Democritus, the root source of all subsequent Physics. The point we make in this book is that there was, at that time, an alternative logical route which physical science – or Natural Philosophy as it then was – might have taken instead.

    Democritus had solved the Eleatic stasis problem by thinking of the primary reality as not just matter but as the conceptual trinity, atoms, void and mechanical motion. This, for him and his followers, was the ultimate reality that underlies all our perceptions of the world, all else, including phenomena, being, as he presented it, no more than just subjective ideas or ‘conventions’.

    This ‘atomising’ of matter was the inspiration which led not only to our Western science but also to the ‘materialist’ ethic of the society that has been established upon it. Like Democritus, we still tend to think of everything but brute matter as ‘abstractions’ – in Democritan terms, ‘conventions’, ‘ideas’ or just names. Any other view, which puts matter into the same category as that of ideas, or relative perceptions (observations) – as, indeed, the Theory of Relativity does – tends to be regarded as some kind of Subjective Idealism. This relativist or observationist theory is the very antithesis of Democritan atomism insofar as it reverses Democritus’ order of priority by putting relative perceptions first and matter (mass) as something secondary and derivative, a product of pure observation.

    Apart from its indisputable efficacy, then, as a fundamental theory of physics, Einstein’s Theory of Relativity has been, right from its appearance in 1905, a source of conceptual consternation. Its transmutation of the Democritan dimensions of space and time from absolute dimensions of brute matter into dimensions of observational perspective was, to say the very least, revolutionary [16]. Inevitably, from the point of view of traditional, or ‘Classical’, physics it was seen as a form of ‘idealism’, as opposed to the long-established belief in matter as something absolutely self-sufficient and substantial, something existing independently of how we observe it or describe it in language. This latter is the ‘absolutist’ or ‘fundamentalist’ view of matter, space and time which has opposed the Theory of Relativity, right from its very start. Those who oppose the Theory on these absolutist grounds are the so-called ‘Dissidents’, whose main protagonist was Herbert Dingle. Strangely enough, from being among the first to accept and advocate Einstein’s Special Theory of Relativity, Dingle switched to becoming one of the first to reject it and to contend vigorously against it on those very same absolutist grounds. In particular, his opposition was against the new, relativistic conception of time. Time, he insisted, has to be absolutely invariant, a ‘God’s Time’ or ‘cosmic GMT’ which cannot be altered, which holds throughout the universe as the time by which everything endures simultaneously and in parallel, as much in the void as in matter [17].

    2.4.1. Space in light, not light in space: inversion of classical atomism

    Such, then, is the – one might say, schizophrenic – scientific legacy of Democritus, as it now manifests itself in the form of modern physics, where the physical world still tends to be regarded as one thing and our observations of it, in the context of Relativity, as something else entirely. However, despite the difficulty, at this present stage in history, of looking at the world in any other way, the fact is that thinking in that absolutistic, ‘God’s-eye-view’ way about an underlying physical reality was never logically obligatory. For instance, some contemporary of Democritus might well have argued that what is primarily real is not these ‘atoms’ which, in the end-analysis, are no more than theoretical interpretations of observational information (ideas) but the information itself on which these ideas are based. This is in the form, mainly, of light, not as we customarily think of it as something travelling inscrutably in space, but as we actually and directly see it, both optically and instrumentally, in its full spectral range from the deepest infra-red to the farthest ultra-violet and gamma. From light, in this direct-action sense, both empirical science and commonsense project the external world in all categories of sensation and instrumentation. Since there is nothing that can be positively identified as more elemental, this light-information is undeniably primary, or a priori real. The customary idea that light is supplied by ‘atoms’ and ‘travels in space’ was never more than secondary and speculative (a posteriori); that is, essentially theoretical as opposed to directly observational, relative and empirical.

    To switch from thinking and speaking of matter as the basic ‘stuff’ of existence to thinking instead of information as that basic ‘stuff’ (substance) signifies a complete inversion of Democritan ‘realism’ and its associated language-system. If it could be managed on a public scale, this switch from one category structure, or paradigm, to another, in our present scientific language, would be truly revolutionary. In all probability it would be more psychologically and sociologically disturbing to contemplate – more heretical, in effect – than the Copernican revolution, Quantum Theory and the Theory of Relativity put together.

    One of the aims of this book is to bring to the fore this ‘pure-information’ interpretation of light for consideration as a candidate in a wholesale revision of our ailing modern physics with its exponential proliferation of bizarre theories and their associated pure jargon which makes a mockery of ordinary logical language. Much of this jargonising of contemporary physics stems from the current conception of ‘light’ as a space-travelling intermediary between objects and our perceptions of them. There are also some fallacies of a logical – or, rather, illogical – nature on which our modern physics and cosmology are based and which need to be pointed out. These rather flagrant misuses of reason will be dealt with in due course. Meanwhile, our dealings will be with the main offender, which is our current conceptualisation of what light is and does in space (in vacuo) between physical objects and the observer, particularly in the context of Relativity and Quantum Theory. The following chapter shows how that concept of light developed throughout our scientific history in the various attempts to understand light in all its different aspects. This puts the current ‘space-travelling’ interpretation of light in its proper logical perspective and thereby clears the way for whole new de-jargonised and progressive language of physics based on direct quantum information, the logical alternative to the current conception of ‘light-velocity’.

    We are reminded, here, of what Berkeley said about the physics of his time: ‘We kick up a dust and complain we cannot see.’ Since then, the ‘dust’ Berkeley talked about in those early days of nascent classical physics has become so dense as to be more like a conceptual ‘pea-soup smog’. The reader may be assured that the following discussion is aimed at achieving an atmospheric clean-up, as it were, of the centuries-long build-up of theoretical pollution in commonsense language. It will therefore be conducted in straightforward ordinary-language terms throughout, with the aim of systematically de-jargonising modern physics language. All that is required of the reader, therefore, is a modicum of competency in commonsense logic and the handling of the standard English language. No specialised knowledge of current physics or mathematical theories will be necessary. Indeed, the less committed to this specialised knowledge the intelligent reader may be, the better should be his grasp of the pure unadulterated logic of this discussion.

    CHAPTER 3

    Light as Pure Quantum Information

    At the end of the last chapter, we advocated, as a corrective to our ailing Modern Physics, a complete inversion from Democritan atomic ‘realism’, or absolutism, to the quantised relativism of Einstein’s mentor, Ernst Mach. Although he strenuously denied it, Mach was definitely the logical forerunner of modern Relativity Theory – and, indeed, a precursor of Quantum Theory.

    The essential difference between Mach’s original relativism and that of his follower, Einstein, apart from Mach’s relativism being quantised, was that while they were both centrally concerned with observation, for Einstein, observation was the end-product of light having travelled inscrutably in space (in vacuo) for perhaps æons. In contrast, for Mach, light was just pure observational-informational data – in effect, what we see or otherwise detect with our optical instruments, not something essentially unseen, underlying and prior to all observation.

    In this original Machian (or, now, Neo-Machian) physics, information, in the form of light – in its full spectral range – rather than matter, is the basic ‘stuff’ of existence. In this present chapter, we show that there are historical precedents going right back to the Middle Ages for this view of light as pure information, as opposed to something invisibly ‘travelling’ in the space between one atom and another. But, of course, in our Normal Realist approach to physics these ‘atoms’ and the ‘space’ between them are not those of classical (Democritan) conception. They are no more or less than relative projections from patterns and sequences of observation, that is, phenomena of one sort or another. However, from the evidence of ‘splitting the atom’, the creation of the ‘atomic bomb’, and so on we accept that these objects we call ‘atoms’ do exist in some conceptual sense or other (to be discussed in due course). Also, we accept that light consists of transactions of energy between these ‘atoms’, howsoever these may be conceived. What we do not accept is the customary view of light being composed of space-travelling ‘photons’. Instead, we think of light as consisting simply of disembodied and immediate (i.e., proper time-instantaneous) transfers of discrete packets or quanta of energy from one ‘atom’ to another, somewhat along the lines of the Wheeler and Feynman theory of direct particle interaction [18]. These instantaneous quantum transactions (see Appendix 1, section

    A1.2.) between bodies, as well as being bits of energy, are bits of information. In Normal Realism, it is this information which is transacted between observers in the form of quanta, the ultimate information from which we, as observers, project every physical property and quality – and indeed the very existence – of those bodies, including, of course, others like ourselves. In Normal Realism, this is what provides the objectivity of the world, as projected and described by an interactive community of observers, not by a single observer such as oneself.

    Basic to this Normal Realist agenda, right from the start, is that the constant $c$, traditionally interpreted as the ‘speed of light’ is reinterpreted in the Neo-Machian way as, simply, a conversion factor for converting units of observationally projected distance (metres) into units of observational time (seconds), which gives $c$ as 3.3 nanoseconds for every metre of distance in all the usual three dimensions. We begin this chapter, however, by providing a brief historical summary of theories concerning light and demonstrate that current theories are in apparent conflict simply because of an over-interpretation of the elementary empirical facts about light, as something travelling. By applying the principle of conceptual economy known as Ockham’s Razor, we shave off the hoary accretion of redundant hypotheses to reveal a logically clean-cut, Neo-Machian conception of light as directly perceived pure information.

    3.1. The history of light-theory

    The way we customarily think of light, nowadays, as travelling in space, was a fairly recent innovation in science. However, a study of the history of how that idea developed should disabuse us of any notion that this ‘space-travelling’ concept of light is logically obligatory.

    Before the time of the ancient Greeks, ‘light’, in the context of vision, was simply the opposite of ‘dark’. The ancient Greeks were the first to study the nature and properties of light and since that time, this study has remained a major topic in physics. The Greek scientists attempted to answer fundamental questions such as what does light consist of and by what mechanism can we see things? It is strange for us to appreciate today that these ancient scientists seriously contemplated the explanation of light as some kind of illumination projected by the eye itself. It was believed that the goddess Aphrodite lit the ‘fire in the eye’ which shone out to interact with physical objects. However, as early as the 5$^{th}$ century B.C., Empedocles postulated that there was an interaction between ‘rays of light’ from a light source such as the

    Sun and rays emanating from our eyes, since he clearly appreciated that we cannot see in the dark $^{[19]}$.

    This idea that light rays emanated from the eye was not, with a few notable exceptions, seriously challenged until the ‘Golden Age’ of Islamic science, by Arabian scientist al-Haytham, more popularly known in the West as Alhazen (circa 965 – 1040 A.D.). His most convincing arguments were based on his observations concerning pinhole cameras (camera obscuras) $^{[19]}$. Unfortunately, his work, like that of so many other Arab scholars, did not reach Europe until the end of the 12$^{th}$ century and was not generally known until it was published in the 16$^{th}$ century. On the basis of observations involving beams of light, Alhazen believed light to be a ‘streaming substance’ composed of tiny particles which travel in straight lines, similar to the modern idea of ‘photons’. He believed that we see an object by ‘rays’ of these streaming particles emanating from a source and bouncing off that object into our eyes. Although the ancient Greeks had not considered questions concerning what might be the actual speed of such ‘light rays’, it was Alhazen’s conviction that light travels in straight lines at a finite but very high speed. He was also one of the first scientists to propound the idea that light travels at different speeds in different media.

    It is all too easy to understand where the concept of light travelling as rays or beams originates since this appears to be the case in ordinary observation. For example, in dusty atmospheres, interactions with dust-particles form rays of illumination, as is often seen at Sunset, and shadows cast on walls strongly suggest that this illumination is travelling. Indeed, astronomers report that this same phenomenon is observed with interstellar gas-clouds, hazes around supernovae, and so on. However, it is important to appreciate that all the studies by Alhazen et al., were of light conducted by various materials. No study of the behaviour of light in a true vacuum was carried out at this time, although it had been observed that a ray of the brightest Sunlight directed through opposite holes in a barrel was completely invisible unless some dust or smoke was introduced into the barrel.

    In Europe the first major advance towards the current concept of light was not made until the beginning of the 17$^{th}$ century. This was with the work of Johannes Kepler. Kepler was the first scientist to give, among other things, a correct explanation of the working of the human eye $^{[19]}$. However, in contrast to Alhazen,

    Kepler believed the speed of light to be infinite. This was also the opinion of the French philosopher Descartes, who staked his whole philosophy on this belief$^{[20]}$.

    Just a few years after Kepler’s first publications, Galileo constructed a sophisticated telescope and, with it, was able to observe the four principal moons of Jupiter. In contrast to Kepler and Descartes, Galileo believed the speed of light to be finite and attempted to measure it by performing a crude experiment. This was essentially the first experiment of its kind but it was doomed to failure. The experiment was performed at night on two hilltops approximately three miles apart. He and his assistant were both equipped with lanterns which could be covered by a pail. The idea was that when Galileo uncovered his lantern, his assistant would see the light and immediately uncover his lantern. Galileo measured the time it took him from uncovering his lantern to seeing the light from his assistant’s lantern. Knowing the distance ‘travelled’ by the light and the time taken, he could, in principle, calculate its ‘speed’. But every time, a different result was obtained since the distance used was far too small to allow for human reaction time. This, of course, is scarcely surprising in view of the ‘speed’ nowadays ascribed to light, which is approximately 3.3 nano-seconds for every metre (in fact, $2.99792458 \times 10^8$ metres per second).

    During the 17$^{th}$ century, ideas concerning how light is transmitted in space continued to flourish. In the 1660s, Pierre Gassendi reiterated Alhazen’s postulate that light is composed of ‘streaming particles’, thereby laying the foundations of what was to become known as the ‘particulate’, or ‘corpuscular’ – sometimes called the ‘ballistic’ – theory of light. Newton was a supporter of this particle theory and developed Gassendi’s ideas further. He produced what he supposed was experimental evidence for the theory, involving the colour spectra produced by triangular prisms. Once again, it must be appreciated that the observations on which Newton based his theory were all concerned with the conduction of light in various media, not in space as such (i.e., the vacuum)$^{[21]}$.

    This view that light is a ‘streaming substance’ consisting of tiny particles is a development of the view of the ancient Greek atomists after Democritus, that light consists of atomic cast-offs, or ‘effluvia’. However, there now developed a different approach – that of explaining light in terms of travelling waves. Of course, it was plain that light reaches the Earth from the Sun and other stars through the vacuum of space. On the other hand, it was believed that light requires some sort of medium in which to ‘travel’, as is necessary for ‘waves’ of any kind – because, of course, for there to be a wave, there has to be something which waves – that is to say, there cannot, obviously, be a wave travelling on its own in nothing.

    In contrast to Gassendi and Newton, Descartes, following on from the idea of the Greek four-element theorists, believed that space is filled with a rigid plenum, which transmits ‘light pressure’ instantly from the source to the eye. Huygens and Hooke espoused his ‘plenum’ idea and produced the ‘wave theory’ of light in which it was proposed that light is transmitted as waves through the plenum, in much the same way that waves are propagated in water. There followed a prolonged and bitter argument between Hooke and Newton, concerning whether it was the particle theory or the wave theory which provided the correct description of light $^{[19] [22]}$. During the 18$^{th}$ century, Newton appeared to be the victor in this dispute, which prompted the majority of scientists to accept the particle theory.

    These two competing theories of light had one thing in common – it was almost universally accepted that light ‘travels’ in some sense. Historically, the Danish astronomer Römer has been credited with the first successful determination of the ‘speed of light in space’. Whilst observing the orbit of Io, one of the moons of Jupiter which had been discovered by Galileo, Römer found that the time between successive eclipses of that satellite by its parent planet got shorter as distance between the Earth and Jupiter lessened, and longer as that distance increased. Since the actual period of the satellite’s orbit could not, mechanically, vary in this way, he concluded that this extra time taken between those eclipses represented the time taken for the Sunlight reflected off Io to ‘travel’ the extra distance between the two planets as they orbited the Sun. Knowing the time taken over that estimated distance, Römer was able to calculate the ‘speed’ of light. Unfortunately, the accepted value for the diameter of Earth’s orbit around the Sun at that time was incorrect, so that Römer’s data produced too low a value for that ‘speed’. Nevertheless, Römer’s observations were taken as definite proof that the ‘speed of light in space’ is finite, contrary to Descartes’ view that it is infinite.

    The value of this supposed ‘speed of light in space’ was later labelled as $c$. However, it is important to appreciate that what Römer actually discovered, without any speculative embroidery, was simply a constant ratio, $c$, between conventional measures of observational distance and time. Logically, however, although $c$ has the

    dimensions of speed, it need not necessarily have been interpreted as a speed $^{[23]}$. The idea, therefore, that light ‘travels’ is, again, pure supposition, since we never actually observe light leave an object and ‘travel’ towards us in order for us to see the object. Nor do we ever see light actually ‘travelling’ between one object and another in the pure vacuum of space.

    Gradually, the value of this distance-time ratio $c$ was refined in other astronomical experiments such as those of the Astronomer Royal, James Bradley, in 1725. Bradley’s method was by means of the so-called ‘aberration of light’, which was like judging the speed of a man walking through vertically falling rain by the forward slope of his umbrella (for ‘umbrella’, in Bradley’s sense, read ‘telescope’ and for ‘rain’ read ‘light-ray’).

    The first successful terrestrial experiments were performed by Armand Fizeau in 1849. These led to a succession of experiments of increasing accuracy performed by A. A. Michelson in 1878 and culminating in his experiments of 1926 $^{[24]}$. These experiments employed an apparatus consisting of a beam of light, directed onto rotating many-sided mirrors, and then reflected back from a distant plane mirror, about 35 kilometres away. Michelson obtained from this a result, for $c$, of approximately 300,000 kilometres per second. Although many different experiments have been performed since 1926, this remains the accepted approximate value of the constant $c$.

    In stark contrast to the 18$^{th}$ century, it was the wave theory of light that gained widespread support during the 19$^{th}$ century. Arguably, increasing support for the wave theory began with the experiments of Thomas Young, performed around the turn of the 19$^{th}$ century. In Young’s experiments, light was shone through a screen with two slits. This produced an interference pattern of alternating light and dark fringes on a plate behind a screen $^{[19]}$. The fact that this could not be explained if light had particle-like characteristics, led Young to deduce that light ‘travels’ in waves. Young was also able successfully to explain the results of Newton’s experiments in optics using a wave theory. In 1850, Foucault performed an experiment which showed that the (alleged) ‘speed of light’ is slower in water than in air (pace Alhazen, see above). This demonstrated, crucially, that Newton’s particle theory could not be correct, so that the alternative wave theory of light now seemed firmly established. It was earlier in the

    19$^{th}$ century, within the wave theory of light, that the hypothetical carrier of light waves through the vacuum of space had become known as the ‘luminiferous æther’.

    3.2. The ‘electromagnetic wave’ theory of light

    During the early years of the 19$^{th}$ century, another important branch of physics appeared on the scene, introducing a whole new scientific language. This was the language of ‘electromagnetism’. Before this time, it seemed that what had been dubbed ‘electricity’ and ‘magnetism’ referred to completely separate physical phenomena. Indeed, William Gilbert, who coined the Latin term ‘electricus’ (which, in 1646, Thomas Browne rendered in English as ‘electricity’) distinguished between what he called ‘magnetism’ and ‘static’. However, the experiments of Oersted, Biot, Savart and Faraday demonstrated that these phenomena were not separate but were intimately connected. In 1846, in an endeavour to show that there was no need for assuming the presence of an æther, Faraday proposed that an ‘electromagnetic field’ could provide the medium by which light waves are propagated $^{[19]}$. However, it is to be noted that there is no electromagnetic field present in a true vacuum and so this proposal does not really explain how we receive light from, for example, the distant stars and galaxies, since electric ‘fields’ cannot possibly extend that far.

    In 1856, the ratio of the unit of charge in electrostatic units to that in electromagnetic units was determined by Weber and Kohlrausch. They found that this ratio was remarkably close to the known value of the constant $c$ and possessed the same dimensions, ostensibly, as those of a speed $^{[25]}$. However, it is to be noted that the intention of the Weber and Kohlrausch experiment had certainly not been to determine the value of the ‘speed of light’, so that the similarity between the ratio of units of charge to those of electromagnetism and the ‘speed of light’ was, at that stage, almost coincidental.
