    However, between 1862 and 1865, James Maxwell built on Faraday’s ideas to derive a set of four equations which became known as Maxwell’s Equations. The constant ratio, $c$, determined a few years earlier by Weber and Kohlrausch, appears in these equations as a ratio which has the same dimensions and the same numerical value as the ‘speed of light’. On this basis, Maxwell assumed that the constant $c$ in his equations could not have been coincidental but that his ‘electrodynamical constant’ $c$ actually was the ‘speed of light’, although, logically, he need not have interpreted this constant as a ‘speed’ at all $^{[26]}$.

    Solutions of Maxwell's equations are certainly wave-like in character, so that it seemed natural for Maxwell to infer from this that light is propagated in waves through the electromagnetic field, thereby supporting the wave theory of light. Subsequently, it became generally accepted that Maxwell had provided the explicit link between the new electromagnetism and the nature of light. In a paper written in 1864 Maxwell states:

    'This velocity is so nearly that of light that it seems we have strong reason to conclude that light itself ... is an electromagnetic disturbance in the form of waves propagated through the electromagnetic field according to electromagnetic laws [27].'

    By 'this velocity' Maxwell meant, of course, the constant c.

    From this time on, light came to mean not just visible light but the whole of the electromagnetic spectrum – that is, light of every kind, both visible and invisible, all of which propagates at the same distance-time rate c. Maxwell's equations represent physical laws pertaining to matter and as such, could not depend, in the thinking of that time, on any particular observer. Light waves were regarded as 'stresses' propagated through an electromagnetic, 'luminiferous æther', so that the constant c in Maxwell's equations was generally interpreted as the speed of light relative, not to the observer but to the æther. Logically however, as explained above, this is an over-interpretation of the true facts. This was discovered in the famous experiments performed by Michelson and Morley during the 1880s, which implied that even if an æther existed, it was undetectable by any experiment, hence was conceptually redundant.

    The logical conclusion from this failure to find an æther is that there is no 'absolute state of rest' to serve as a reference-zero for measuring motion. This means that, conceived as a 'speed', c becomes, altogether bafflingly, a constant with the same value for all observers in all states of motion relative to one another. Since there is no sort of 'speed', truly so called, that can ever behave in that way, what these experiments prove, in effect, is that c is a constant of pure observation, not a 'speed' in any sense.

    The lack of an absolute reference-frame, of course, supports the Machian view of observational relativism as opposed to that of fundamentalist Absolutism, or Realism. However, despite the intrinsic meaninglessness of this 'light-speed' interpretation of c, Einstein, in 1905 embraced it and went ahead with it in his theory. This was in the form of his second postulate of Relativity, stating that 'the speed of light in vacuo’ is the same for all observers regardless of their states of relative motion or rest. This has been, from the start, the sticking point of Commonsense in its efforts to understand Einstein’s Special Theory of Relativity.

    Logically, however, Einstein could have stated simply that the constant c is the same for all observers, in the same way that the constant ratio of conventional yards to conventional metres or between conventional pounds and conventional kilograms is, unproblematically, the same for all observers – needless to say, regardless of whether these observers are relatively moving or stationary [28].

    In any case, the whole idea of light having ‘a constant speed’ in the vacuum is, logically speaking, pure nonsense. In the vacuum (qua void), with regard to what reference-datum can that ‘speed’ be constant or otherwise? And if we think of light in vacuo as a sequence of waves, then what is there to ‘wave’ in a vacuum? Moreover, if we think of light as a stream of travelling particles then how shall we account for what those particles are doing in the vast reaches of the vacuum between the stars and galaxies? What time-frame are they supposed to be in when observed by no-one and interacting with nothing? Furthermore, as Relativity directs, at the speed those ‘particles’ (allegedly) ‘travel’, even the tiniest bit of intrinsic mass they might possess becomes relativistically infinite [29], and if they start out with no mass at all, then how can they be conceived as ‘particles’? Any existence light might have in vacuo, whether as ‘waves’ or ‘particles’, is therefore, on all counts, inconceivable.

    The implication of all this is that in Einstein’s Theory of Relativity, his Second Postulate of light-speed in vacuo being the same for all observers is not only meaningless but is also unnecessary for deducing the practical consequences of that theory. As Bondi and Pope have concurred (see Chapter 1), these same consequences can be deduced directly from Pythagoras, simply by extending Pythagoras’ Theorem in the usual three dimensions to include a fourth (the simple mathematical details of this are given in Appendix 1). This means that the whole language register of words, phrases, etc., that has grown out of the historical rigmarole regarding how light is supposed to behave in those experiments conducted by Michelson and Morley et al. can be dispensed with as surplus to requirements. Catastrophic though this would undoubtedly be for established convention, from a purist standpoint the plain truth of the matter is that it leaves room for another logical interpretation of the nature of light which rejects the notion of ‘light-speed’ together with all the language-culture which has gathered around it. This logical alternative is explored in the following chapter. It is only fair to warn the reader, however, that seriously to contemplate such a radical revision, not only of scientific but also of much of settled language in general, is not for the faint-hearted. Be that as it may, either to demand or offer any apologies for this prospective ‘vandalising’ of established convention would be as grotesque from the point of view of true Philosophy as it would be mandatory for modern reasons of ‘political correctness’. It has to be confessed, therefore, that the ideas presented in this book have been described as ‘immoral’ and even ‘dangerous’ for these same socio-political reasons. With that caveat in mind, we now continue with this economising of current scientific conceptions of the nature of light in relation to physical phenomena.

    CHAPTER 4

    The ‘Holographic’ Character of Illumination

    The historical account of light as a ‘travelling’ intermediary between the observer and the object observed, given in the preceding chapter, says little about the fact that there has always been, logically speaking, an entirely different way of thinking about light which has, so far, been only partly explored and whose relevance to Physics has been almost entirely neglected. During the Middle Ages, there were thinkers, such as the cleric and mathematician Grosseteste, and others such as Bacon, who regarded light, not as something travelling but simply as something that is directly observed in informational patterns of luminous data containing the very essence (the ‘soul’ or character, as they put it) of external objects. This ‘soul’ of light is what modern perception psychologists call a ‘character-complex’ or Gestalt. These luminous data, were named ‘simulacra’, ‘eidola’, etc., and any question as to how they were physically conveyed was subordinate to the question of what these character complexes, or information-complexes, betokened in themselves. Grosseteste in particular was intrigued with the geometrical characteristics of the optical patterns which we know as parallax and perspective. There was much discussion at the time as to the physiological connection between these geometrical patterns as they occurred in physical objects and as they occur directly in our eyes and thence conveyed by our nerves to our brains. This was apart from the speculations of Alhazen, et al. regarding the alleged ‘velocity of light’ in space. It was all about informational projection from directly perceived patterns and sequences of ‘images’, which is reminiscent of the way in which the ancient Greeks thought about light, as some kind of illumination projected by the eye itself (see Chapter 3).

    All this, in whichever way it was conceived and described, tacitly presupposed the existence of an external world defined in terms of dimensions, properties and qualities of its own, essentially similar in kind to those of our perceptions. The 17$^{th}$ century philosopher René Descartes was mainly responsible for creating the radical separation between ‘physical reality’ and our perceptions of it, as we think of it today. This duplication of realities was thenceforward known as Cartesian dualism $^{[30]}$. The undeniable fact that none of the normal dimensions, of parallax and perspective, can exist other than in relation to some specified point of observation and that they are different for every different observer, i.e., they are all relative to observation, was then, as now, not generally appreciated. Yet there were some thinkers, such as Berkeley, Hume and Kant, who were well aware of this essential observer-centredness, or relativity, of physical dimensions. However, at that time the science community was not ready for such a radical shift in thinking, from Cartesian dualism to observational relativism. That would come eventually, with Mach’s phenomenalism and its quantisation of sensation in terms of ‘sense-data’ leading to the subsequent appearance of Einstein’s Theory of Relativity, based as this was on Mach’s relativist philosophy. Meanwhile, there were other developments in physics which support Mach’s quantised relativism as opposed to Einstein’s continuist, Cartesian or dualistic version.

    4.1. The quantum light-pixel

    A revolution in the study of the nature of light occurred at the beginning of the 20$^{th}$ century. This was the discovery that light information consists of discrete packets (quanta) of physical energy. Four years prior to the publication of Einstein’s Special Theory of Relativity, the quantisation of physical energy was first proposed by Max Planck in order to explain the apparently paradoxical results of the Rayleigh and Jeans study of ‘black body radiation’ [31]. These two researchers had supposed that radiant energy was absorbed and re-emitted by a black body in a continuous way. Planck, on the other hand, proposed that this radiant energy could be emitted and absorbed only in the form of discrete, indivisible ‘packets’, which he called quanta, thus precluding any chance of radiant energy being released in a single flash, picturesquely named the ‘ultraviolet catastrophe’.

    In 1905, the same year as he published his Special Theory of Relativity, Einstein employed Planck’s ideas to explain the phenomenon of the ‘photoelectric effect’. This phenomenon, first discovered by Hertz in 1887, concerns the way in which electrons are ejected in discrete amounts from metal surfaces exposed to light of certain frequencies [32]. According to the wave theory of light, the number of electrons ejected cannot depend on the frequency of the light. Unfortunately for that theory, experiments clearly demonstrated that it did. As a solution to this problem, Einstein suggested that not only is energy exchanged between matter and radiation, in discrete packets, as proposed by Planck, but also that electromagnetic radiation (including visible light) is actually composed of discrete ‘particles’ which are, essentially, energy quanta. These quanta he called photons, which was a name coined by Gilbert Lewis to describe the smallest unit of radiant energy. Hence, at the beginning of the 20$^{th}$ century, it appeared that physics had turned full circle in finally supporting the particle theory of light, although this had been shown earlier to be contradicted by various experiments.

    The upshot of all this was that, in at least partially explaining the photoelectric effect, Einstein had created a paradox. A particle is not a wave and a wave is not a particle, any more than a horse is a cow or a cow is a horse. So how can light have both wave-like and particle-like characteristics? With its increasingly cavalier disdain for commonsense logic and language, the answer to this dilemma was proposed on grounds of ‘complementarity’. This was supplied by Einstein and de Broglie on the purely ad hoc grounds that although light cannot possibly have both of these characteristics, it nevertheless does! This counter-intuitive proposal came to be known as the particle-wave duality theory of light and is summarised by Baierlein $^{[27]}$ as:

    ‘Light travels as a wave but departs and arrives as a particle.’

    This is yet another portent of what would eventually become the flagrant departure from commonsense that now characterises Modern Physics and Cosmology.

    During the 1920s, Bohr and his collaborators developed what was known as the Copenhagen interpretation of Quantum Mechanics. This approach to quantum theory proposed what was called the ‘collapse of the wave function’ as a possible explanation for the particle-wave duality of light. This proposal, in essence, is that as soon as we attempt to observe a spherical light wave, it collapses at that point of observation similar to the way in which a pricked soap-bubble collapses into a blob or particle at the point of contact. Young’s two-slit experiment is often quoted as supporting this ‘fact’ $^{[33]}$.

    From both a logical and philosophical point of view, however, this explanation makes no sense. How can the alleged light-wave remain inscrutable until it is observed at a point of collapse? How can the mere presence of an observer possibly affect the character of light in that way? For example, is it only a human observer who can prick the ‘light-bubble’ or do just any – or all – physical objects perform that act? And what is the character of that wave in a region where there are no objects at all, when the bubble is whole and unpricked? The Copenhagen interpretation creates many more problems than the one it attempts to solve. In the words of Feynman, originally stated in 1964:

    'I think I can safely say that nobody understands quantum mechanics.'

    There is no doubt that the wave theory of light is a very successful practical tool for studying certain aspects of light. Likewise, in its own right, the corpuscular or ballistic theory is equally successful when studying other aspects. Very few physicists would argue against the fact that Quantum Mechanics is very useful when studying phenomena at the micro-level and for making physical predictions at that level. However, practicalities aside, no-one can pretend that these various approaches have provided anything like a successful understanding of how light behaves on its own, in vacuo. Nor has anyone provided any explanation of the mechanism whereby a wave could collapse into a particle, especially the so-called 'photon', which has no mass or any other physical property or quality to speak of$^{[34]}$.

    4.2. Light, the foundation of all knowledge of nature

    Most, if not all, the problems arising from attempts to explain the character of light can be traced right back to the notion of its being a 'streaming substance', that is, a substance which travels. To interpret it as a fact, that light travels relatively to a chosen observer and hence that it possesses a speed relative to that observer in the same way that a body in motion does is plainly absurd, especially when it is said that this 'speed' is the same for all different observers, no matter what. No 'velocity', properly so called can be described in that manner. Yet experiments have conclusively revealed that, despite the protests of a vestigial minority of conceptual Dingle-ites (see Chapter 2, section 2.4), the constant $c$ really is the same for all observers. To say the least, this makes it extremely difficult to justify the interpretation of $c$ as a speed in any sense, since then it would be, uniquely, the only speed that is absolute – that is the same with respect to everything – whereas from the time of Galileo, scientists have unanimously agreed that any speed must be relative to something, something definite which is not a vacuum. Moreover, if light does travel, then how can it behave as a travelling wave in some circumstances and mysteriously turn into a travelling 'particle' in others? The whole conception is wrapped in confusion.

    In the Normal Realist approach to physics, to be described more fully in the following chapter, the constant $c$ is interpreted as just that, a constant, no more or less. The empirical fact is simply that there is a constant ratio of time units to distance units for any observer measuring space. In other words, Einstein’s second postulate of Special Relativity, i.e., that the ‘speed of light in vacuo’ is the same for all observers, is replaced with the simple, unproblematic statement:

    ‘Observational distance and time have a constant ratio of units, c, for all observers sharing that same conventional choice of units’$^{[35]}$.

    However, from a commonsense point of view it certainly seems as though light is propagated through empty space with a constant speed – that is, until we try to construct in our minds any intuitive idea as to just how that motion takes place. We have seen that there are many reasons why the constant c cannot be interpreted as a speed (ten logical reasons for this will be discussed in Chapter 6). More importantly, if the constant c appearing in Maxwell’s equations is interpreted as no more than a dimensional constant of observation, then there is no implication of light being a wave travelling in the vacuum, for, as we say, what can possibly ‘wave’ in a vacuum? Likewise, any inference that light travels as a stream of ‘particles’ is also erased. This removes that whole nest of brain–racking problems created by the apparent wave-particle duality of light.

    The principle of Ockham’s Razor, then, makes it more conceptually economical and logical, simply to interpret c as a universal conversion factor for converting optical distances measured, say, in units of metres, into units of light-seconds, taking c as 300,000 kilometres to the second. This idea of interpreting c as simply a conversion factor was proposed by Bondi, as early as 1964, in his book Assumption and Myth in Physical Theory $^{[36]}$. As already mentioned, Pope had proposed this same interpretation, independently, ten years earlier and afterwards corresponded with Bondi concerning their mutual idea $^{[37]}$. Bondi concurred with Pope that interpreting c purely as a conversion factor made Special Relativity very much simpler $^{[38]}$. Indeed, the only difference between Bondi and Pope on this point was, as it transpired, that whereas Bondi found that jettisoning the ‘speed’ interpretation of c made it far easier to teach Relativity to his students, Pope found that it made Relativity not only much more logical and comprehensible but also, with its ‘speed’ axiom removed, that the theory slotted perfectly into the relativistic philosophy of Einstein’s mentor and critic, Mach $^{[39]}$. So it seems that if, instead of departing from Mach’s relativism by his unnecessary Realist interpretation of c as a ‘speed’, Einstein had followed truly in Mach’s phenomenalist footsteps, he would also have adopted the alternative logical interpretation of $c$ as simply a dimensional scale-constant, or ‘conversion factor’. Not only would this have massively simplified and de-mystified his theory but also it would have forestalled the notorious conflict between Relativity and Quantum Theory over the so-called EPR paradox by showing that the relativistic finite ‘speed $c$’ and quantum (proper time) instantaneity are complementary aspects of the same relativistic time-formula [40]. If that cooperation between Mach and Einstein had taken place in its natural, logical way, then Mach would truly have been hailed as the forerunner of Relativity. Mach’s failure to deal effectively with the interpretation of $c$ would have been made good and phenomenalism would have come into its own as a clear and combined relativistic and quantised alternative to classical absolutism. The conflict between Democritan materialism and the equally truncated Subjective Idealism of Berkeley, et al., would have become things of the past. In this way, the world of direct observation and the world of physics that Descartes had theoretically divorced from each other would have been reconciled within a common understanding based on a de-intellectualised and de-jargonised language-system applicable throughout both science and commonsense.

    So much, then, for what light is not. Let us now see what light is.

    4.3. What, really, is light?

    If light does not ‘travel’ in the accepted sense, either in waves or as ‘particles’, then the obvious question is, how does one think of light in this new, Normal Realist context? This takes us back to what was proposed at the end of Chapter 2, namely, to think of light as pure information, that is, naïvely, ‘what we see’. Of course, this is not the same as saying that light is to be thought of simply as the opposite of ‘dark’ as in times prior to the ancient Greeks. The root source of this information is physical phenomena, those time-sequences of three-dimensional forms we commonly experience in the union of all our sensory inputs as the World. This, of course, includes communicating and interacting with instruments of any shape and kind at all levels of the spectral range. It means also that things are communicating/interacting with one another continually, even in what is, to us, complete darkness, or when we are asleep, unconscious, unborn or even dead. There is therefore nothing in the least bit subjective about this conception of the ultimate reality of physical phenomena, as critics of phenomenalism have traditionally supposed. These phenomena are simply those character complexes (patterns, or Gestalten) of quantum information which are traded between everyone and everything in terms of quantum interactions on the ultimate micro-level. Examples of these phenomena are trees, grass and animals, the sky, the sea, the Sun, Moon and stars, the galaxies, other people, objects seen directly or in air or water, reflected in mirrors, seen in microscopes and telescopes, in readings from voltmeters and Geiger counters, phenomena such as heat, air-pressure, steam-pressure, radioactivity – in fact, all the phenomena known and named in the language of ordinary commonsense and practical science. This profusion of phenomena, which is immensely larger than any human individual’s perception of it, is, as we say, simply the World of ordinary reality in the view that is named Normal Realism. In short, Normal Realism is no more nor less than the default position adopted by just about any sane and sensible human observer who is not conditioned or bedazzled by any purely theoretical and jargonised belief system. This is more fully explained in the following section.

    4.3.1. Light as direct quantum information

    According to this Neo-Machian, Normal Realist philosophy, all physical phenomena, when analysed right down to their micro-phenomenal limit, are ultimately discrete and irreducible ‘data’ in the way Mach envisaged. Merging this together with the work of Planck and Einstein means that light (as Grosseteste, Bacon, et al. understood it) is ‘atomised’ in ultimately discrete and irreducible ‘packets’, i.e. quanta, of energy-cum-information. From this it will be readily seen that in the Normal Realist approach to physics, it is by no means implied that these quanta are to be thought of as ‘particles’ in the Democritan, materialist sense, far less as space-travelling particles, or ‘photons’.

    To expand on this idea, light analyses out, eventually, into tiny luminous bits, or pixels of light energy. These pixels are of one pure colour or another, according to their spectral frequency – that is, their energy divided by a constant. This follows the discovery, in the early 20$^{th}$ century, that there are energy-states in light such that the product of the energy and time (that is, the quantity of energy, called action) in a light pixel cannot be reduced below a certain amount, that is, a quantum. Thus, while at that fundamental level, the energy component of that quantity may be reduced more or less indefinitely, the effect of that reduction is to increase the time component, and vice versa in a see-saw-like way, so that the product of the two measures remains the same, no matter what. Since the reciprocal of time is frequency, this means that increasing the energy also increases the frequency in a constant relation whereby the frequency (the colour) of the light is directly related to its energy. Between the energy component and the time-component of the quantum light pixel, this constant reciprocal relation is known as Planck's constant, signified by h, where h has the value $6.6260755(40) \times 10^{-34}$ J s $^{[41]}$ . Once that limit of micro-physical reduction is reached, that is as far as we can ever go and ever need to go in the reductive analysis of physical phenomena. In Normal Realism, as in Mach's quantised 'sense-datum' approach to physics, all questions of substantial reality 'bottom-out' at that level. There is no other reality beneath or beyond it that it makes sense to speak or even to think about.

    These quanta of light energy occur in optical/instrumental observation like the pixels on a video screen out of informational patterns and sequences of which the viewer projects the video scenario. In Normal Realism, those patterns and sequences of quantum ‘blips’ are the ultimate information from which, as observers, we project the distances of objects from ourselves and from one another in the ordinary observational three-dimensional form we know as space. And from the way those objects and their distances change, as measured by our clocks and other processes, we extrapolate, at the observational centre, the passage of time. The masses of those objects can then be measured by the way in which, over time, they are seen to behave in relation to one another, in the same way that the masses of the Sun and planets are measured without actually handling them. Ultimately, every physical property and quality that defines a physical object can be measured by these same optical methods. Hence, in this Neo-Machian approach, light is not something that is just incidental to matter; it is basic to matter’s very existence. In this complete paradigm ‘flipover’ from Democritan to Machian realism, the ‘light-in-space’ of the standard model becomes the space-in-light of this new informational model. This new model of physical phenomena may be conceived as somewhat in the nature of a hologram but, of course, a far more fine-grained and extensive, interactive ‘hologram’ than anything man has ever constructed or is ever likely to construct – or even conceive $[42]$ .

    Times are long gone since ‘information’ was regarded as something purely abstract in comparison with measurable physical quantities such as mass, length and time. Not only is Planck’s constant h the basic irreducible quantum of physical energy

    – or, rather, action which is the product of energy and time – it is also, and essentially, the basic irreducible quantum of information. Since Planck’s discovery of the quantum, corroborated by Einstein, and the institution of modern Information Technology after Shannon and Weaver, information is known to be as quantifiable as any other physical property or quality $^{[43]}$. This does not mean that information can exist independently, as something floating about on its own, unread and undeciphered. The concept of information necessarily involves an encoder (sender) and a decoder (receiver). These three, the encoder, the decoder and the direct energetic link between them, are integrated and inseparable, so that without any one of those components, ‘information’ cannot signify as such.

    For information to be information, then, it has to be a double-ended, reciprocally connected and completely consummated affair. This means that the light quantum cannot be conceived as an independently space-travelling particle, as in the customary interpretation of the quantum as a ‘photon’. As such, this imaginary ‘light-particle’ is a misconception, as Einstein himself eventually came to realise. Since the so-called ‘photon’ is supposed to be an irreducible quantum of energy (action), it can have no portion of that energy to spare in manifesting itself in any way whatsoever between its source and sink, regardless of how far apart these may be $^{[44]}$. Any attempt to detect it ‘in flight’ would be immediately to annihilate it by absorbing the whole of it in a single quantum flash. This, of course, is why to talk about a ‘photon’ as ‘travelling’ in space is to say something for which there can be no evidence whatsoever $^{[45]}$. Moreover, to think of the ‘photon’ as ‘travelling’ like a projectile (the ‘ballistic’ concept) suggests a transmitted quantum of energy consigned on emission to a limbo of uncertainty where there is no assured goal for its completion. This suggestion, which leaves open the possibility of the ‘photon’ travelling on forever, unabsorbed, runs counter not only to the law of the conservation of energy but also the consummation of information just described.

    A quantum of light-energy, therefore, cannot be conceived as both integral and one-ended in that way. This makes the supposed space-travelling ‘photon’ no more logical to contemplate than a one-ended piece of string. Besides, experiments such as Young’s two-slit experiment reveal that the integrity of the quantum is never split in that way but that it always manifests itself as a complete and consummate inter-resonance between the source and the receptor $^{[46]}$.

    It is to be borne in mind, then, that every quantum of basic light-information that signifies as such immediately signals, in energy terms, the existence of both an ‘emitter’ and a ‘receptor’, or absorber – in informational terms, an encoder and a decoder. The integrity of the quantum makes it, in effect, a single event, common to both the sender and the receiver, so that on the quantum level the world is not severed, in the way Democritus and Descartes envisaged, between the material ‘thing-in-itself’ and the remote observer. On that fundamental level, those ‘two worlds’ become one world. This is the world of the observer in which all space, matter, motion and other physical phenomena are projections from whole matrices of quantum information as directly read and interpreted by the proper use of language. There is no other, independent underlying ‘space’ or ‘void’ through which that information can be thought of as ‘travelling’ between the encoder and the decoder, far less at the ‘finite speed $c$’. The sender, the receiver, the information, the energy and whatever it signifies are all part and parcel of the complete phenomenological package. In this context there is, obviously, no place for the ‘photon’ concept of the light-quantum as something cast out at random into the void to ‘seek its fortune’, as it were $^{[47]}$. These light-quanta are the ultimate informational ‘bits’ into which our common experience of the physical world ultimately analyses $^{[48] [49]}$. They are, to observation, what the pixels on a video screen are to the viewer. In other words, it is from the patterns and sequences of these quantum events – but, of course, in an immensely more prolific and fine-grained way – that all the defining dimensions, properties and qualities of physical phenomena, such as distance, time, mass, motion, etc., are observationally projected. Even the phenomena associated with what is customarily interpreted as ‘the speed of light’, in experiments with the same, are all part of that relatively projected scenario.

    It is to be stressed that the sources and sinks of this quantum information may be things both like and unlike ourselves, simple or complex, human or non-human, animal, vegetable, mineral or anything capable of interacting in quantum-energy units with anything else. These things range from objects on the micro-scale, to those on the macro-scale, and include such things as rocks, plants, animals, stars and even galaxies. In fact, all things that are capable of swapping quanta in any spectral range are sources of information for one another and for the human observer.

    CHAPTER 5

    Phenomena: the Stuff of all Physical Science

    5.1. Language and the logical interpretation of physical phenomena

    In Chapter 2, section 2.1.1, we saw how intimately the common idea of existence is bound-up with the use of language. Indeed what we think of as existence and language are inseparable. The same applies to our interpretations of physical phenomena. The realisation of this intimate connection between language and perception has led to what is generally called Linguistic Philosophy. All those things we think of as existing are always, in the last analysis, linguistic interpretations of phenomena, which prove to be good or bad, as expanding experience reveals.

    Now these phenomena are no ‘pale shades’ of underlying and directly unknowable Democritan existents, or noumena, as Kant called them $^{[50]}$. They are the primary fount of all physicality. The one and only real existence of those entities which are the sources of our perceptions, therefore, lies in the correctness of our interpretations of them in common language as subjects of observational predications. When we get that right as, for the most part, our animal and human natures are designed to do, the reality of those entities, as things in themselves, is simply what they are, right there in front of us – assuming, of course, that there is no distorting medium in between – which is all part of the same phenomenal package, anyway. And so long as we are not mistaken, lying or deluded, they are as we describe them in common language. For instance, as J. L. Austin would say, when we see a duck we are not seeing an image or impression of some metaphysical duck existing behind, beyond or beneath what we see and are familiar with as a duck in the language of our culture. As he puts it, ‘If that’s not a duck then I don’t know what is.’ $^{[51]}$

    On a clear day, then, in standard instances of direct perception, what can we say is more real than the duck that we see before us? Are its ‘atoms’ more real? Are its ‘electrons’ and ‘protons’ more real than that duck? Is the duck more real when conceived as an observational collapsing of a cosmic ‘wave-function’, or as just one manifestation of the duck as it exists in many ‘parallel universes’? Compared with the stark reality of the thing that is actually there, which swims, quacks and walks like a duck, tastes like a duck and which everyone calls a duck, it is those theoretical entities which we call ‘electrons’, ‘protons’, ‘quarks’(!) etc. that are the ‘pale shades’ of the real thing, not the other way around, as traditional Realism would have it.

    But, of course, a dead duck may be carved, dissected and analysed right down to the level of those things we classify and distinguish as ‘molecules’, ‘atoms’, ‘electrons’, ‘protons’ and so on. However, the same interpretational and linguistic conditions apply to those things, in turn, as they do to the duck – except, of course, that in some of these cases, the entities so perceived and described are more speculative and theoretical, hence somewhat less certain and real, than the existence of those things which Austin lists, such as ducks, pigs, trees, churches, barns, etc.

    So, what can we regard as the absolutely unconditional benchmark of what is truly real, independently of human perception and language? This question is addressed in the following section on Normal Realism. In the meantime, let us deal with an all-too-common misunderstanding of what ‘phenomenalism’ means. There is a general tendency, inculcated by the Democritan tradition, to regard any discussion of this present kind as ‘Subjective Idealism’, the view that everything we perceive and conceive is no more than a figment of our own habitual or conventionalised imaginings [52]. There is, however, no way in which these objects that we ordinarily perceive as existing externally to and independently of us can be identified entirely with our own subjective perceptions. They are definitely things other than oneself, but not in the radical, Democritan sense that leads to the classical ‘Problem of Knowledge’ (see Chapter 2, section 2.2). Insofar as things interact with other things, they do so in terms of quanta, which, as we say, are as much units of energy as of information, and are decoded by their recipients according to their capacities for interpretation, no matter how rudimentary and limited in some cases these capacities might be. Obviously, a pebble, an atom, a porpoise and an astronomer have different capacities in that respect. Nevertheless, distinct though they may be from ourselves and one another, these inter-agents can be quite sensibly thought of as residing all in one and the same communicational network, matrix or ‘hologram’, in which oneself is no more than just one finite point-perspective among countless others, all distributed with respect to one another in the way that the sciences of geometry and geography – and commonsense – describe. As Alfred Ayer neatly puts it,

    ‘Places are places in a visual field and the times those that furnish the temporal order of experiences…We can identify a sense-field by reference not only to its own character but also that of its neighbours, and if necessary to that of their neighbours and so on’ [53].

    What Ayer was describing here is, of course, the essentially communal nature of observational perspective. Indeed, any completely isolated self-sufficient personal and private perspective is impossible to imagine [54]. As perception psychologists know very well, all perception takes place in the context of one’s knowledge of other people and objects, how they respond to the world and to ourselves. Without that communicational connection, as in a life-long sensory-deprivation situation, say, there can be no perception, far less language, as we know it. Recognising this dispenses with any notion of ‘Subjective Idealism’ by the simple process of systematically comparing and contrasting, in verbal and behavioural communication, the various individual sensory and instrumental perspectives provided by external sources, that is to say, other things, other animals and other people. As modern philosophers, such as Austin [55], Ryle [56], Wittgenstein [57], et al. have shown, the way we perceive and conceive is inseparable from common language [58]. Language, in all those inter-translatable forms known to mankind, is our ultimate connection with the logical language of natural reality, or logos, as the Greeks called it [59].

    It is to be noted, then, that in our analysis, what the Greeks sought to express by their word ‘logos’ and what we nowadays call ‘information’ – as in Information Technology – are an exact match. This Neo-Machian quantum-relativistic conception of the World as a digital-informational, multi-interactive ‘hologram’ is the natural logical alternative to the model which Democritus imposed on us at the inception of what has since become our modern-day Physics, namely, the model of an infinitely extended, self-sufficient, matter-sprinkled void or vacuum. Which of these models is the more difficult to comprehend, the idea of nature, including ourselves and others like us, being either a Great Interactive Hologram or a Great Mindless Machine? Undoubtedly, our ‘flipside’, Neo-Machian view of physics is very difficult to contemplate from the standpoint of our traditional mind-set. It seems to us nonsensical, for instance, to think of an atom in a far-off galaxy being in immediate and proper time-instantaneous contact with another atom in the eye of a terrestrial observer or in the emulsion of a photographic plate. Even more difficult is it to accept the logical consequence of this, that the loss of energy by the donor atom and the gain in energy by the recipient atom over that vast distance are one and the same event, a quantum pixel shared by the object and the observer in an observer-projected ‘video’ scenario. However, unless we are prepared to jettison Relativity altogether, we have to accept that the intrinsic, or proper time of the quantum interaction at the ‘speed’ $c$ is zero (see Chapter 7, section 7.3.) This means that neither space nor time exist at that quantum-informational level and that it is only in relative time – i.e., observer time, or viewer time – that this interaction is split into the two events separated by ordinary distance and time.

    Yet this consequence of the basic relativistic time formula cannot be denied, however incredible it may seem as applied on the vastest possible scale of the most distant galaxies. But is the stretch of imagination required for this ‘flip-over’ in understanding, from Democritan Materialism to Machian Phenomenalism, any more difficult than, say, the flip-over from a flat-Earth to a round-Earth view of the world, or from a pre-Copernican to a post-Copernican cosmology? Conceptual upheavals of this kind are to be expected as ‘par for the course’ in the development of empirical science and are not to be dismissed on grounds of scepticism alone. As NASA’s John Anderson warns, we shouldn’t miss these things just because we might have the ‘wrong mind-set’.

    Is this flipside, Neo-Machian paradigm, then, seriously to be considered as the natural ‘New Physics’ that Anderson talks about and which Russell anticipates as the ‘philosophy appropriate to Quantum Theory’? Unfortunately, if Normal Realism is to be that ‘New Physics’, then its concept-structure is incommensurable with that of present Physics; that is to say, it is no mere add-on to existing Physics. This means that in what Wittgenstein called ‘language games’, the language in which this ‘New Physics’ is couched would obey rules as different from those of present Physics as those of, for example, rugby from table-tennis or Ludo from cricket. It is doubtful, therefore, whether Anderson, et al. were aware of just how widely different those ‘mind-sets’ might have to be. (In fact, despite what Anderson warns against ‘the wrong mind-set’, all attempts to discuss this suggestion of the required ‘New Physics’ with NASA officials, including Anderson himself, have been monolithically ignored.)

    In any case, whether in the Democritan or the Neo-Machian approach to physics, the World is, for the most part, way beyond our capacity to comprehend. But how else should it be for finite creatures like ourselves, faced with an infinitely extended universe? On the required scale of thinking, any claim to comprehend or mentally encompass the Whole, as so many of our contemporary mathematicians and cosmologists presume to do, is a claim to comprehend the incomprehensible, hence to be automatically dismissed as illusion. Physics, therefore, has to stop short of metaphysics in remaining empirical – that is to say, heuristic or open-ended in its approach to nature. Only in closed minds can the universe be imagined closed.

    However, with our modern experience of digitalisation in the communications industry, with the binary digit or bit, and even in Biology with its DNA molecule and the genome, it is surely, far less difficult now than at any previous stage in its history for our Physics to embrace the concept of the World as being more along the lines of a Universal information-transactional ‘Hologram’ than a Universal ‘Machine’.

    5.2. From Ernst Mach’s approach to physics: Normal Realism

    As already discussed in the previous chapter, in philosophical circles this global norm of objective commonsense empiricism has been called Normal Realism. This is a continuation of that historical skein of commonsense philosophy which was woven by those empiricists, Mach, Moore, Austin, Ryle, Wittgenstein, et al., in the tradition of George Berkeley. Indeed, it claims to be the most up-to-date beneficiary of that whole philosophical legacy [60]. The way in which this intrinsic realism differs from classical Theoretical Realism is that in Normal Realism it is never even suggested that the world we observe in everyday life may not be the same world as that occupied by objects themselves and their various parts. The realism which we know as normal is as described in our best employment of commonsense language, which Wittgenstein called ‘proper usage’. The duck, for instance, has what are commonly called legs, wings, feathers, beak, bones, blood, DNA and so on, all of which go to define and describe the real duck in a suitably precise commonly understood language. Animals don’t make metaphysical projections as to the existence of any world radically beyond and different from that of their ordinary experience. Nor would we but for the peculiar quirk in our social history that has so artificially severed physical reality from ordinary observation. This dualistic metaphysic, arbitrarily introduced by Democritus and established in Western science by René Descartes (see earlier), makes us think of ‘physical reality’ as lying always inaccessibly behind and beyond the normal everyday appearances of things, in dark esoteric ‘fields’, ‘waves’, ‘æther’ and so on. Both Democritus and Descartes thought of perception/observation as a characteristic purely of ‘mind’, which they regarded as synonymous with ‘illusion’ and human subjectivity in general. This was as opposed to matter, which they thought of as existing self-sufficiently and permanently, relative to nothing but itself. Einstein’s Theory of Relativity, based as it was on Mach’s phenomenalism, should have disposed entirely of that artificial partition between the observer and matter. A consistent relativism, with its merging of matter, space and time with observation – as, indeed, Einsteinian Relativity already does – would surely have removed the last remnants of that dualistic divide if it had not been for Einstein’s idiosyncratic, separatist, interpretation of the fundamental constant, c, as the ‘speed of light’. This has perpetuated in Modern Physics the dualism which, logically speaking, Relativity should have completely eradicated.

    Mach was not the only one to challenge Democritan atomism and its Cartesian spin-off, mind-matter dualism. The 18th century phenomenalists, Berkeley, Hume and Kant sought to oppose this dualism on the grounds that both the ‘two realities’ and the divide between them existed nowhere but in the minds of Descartes and his followers, making their physics, in effect, split-minded or schizophrenic. In one part of the mind they put their ideas of matter and, in another part of that same mind, their ideas of – well, ideas. This created for future philosophers the peculiar problem (Wittgenstein called it the Scheinprobleme) of how to link-up those two artificially severed thought-systems. Unfortunately, in their reactionary efforts to avoid this dualism, Berkeley et al. fell right into the trap of Subjective Idealism, the very opposite of Democritan materialism. They virtually cancelled the ‘matter’ side of the duality to leave only ‘mind’, instead of cancelling the dichotomy altogether. Figuratively speaking, in the tug-of-war between these Realists and Idealists, the rope had broken and both disputants had ended-up on their backs [61].

    Regrettably, despite its logical ineptitude, in purely pragmatical terms, the separatist ‘light-speed’, or ‘travelling photon’, interpretation of the distance-time constant c has prevailed. This is to the extent of being now virtually ingrained in our language and our psyche, ‘imbibed with our mother’s milk’, as it were. This makes us think, automatically and by default, in the Democritan/Cartesian way, according to which we never observe things as they actually are but only as they were, due to the time taken for the light from those objects to reach us (3.3 nanoseconds for every metre). By contrast, in Normal Realism, whatever something is, what it is composed of, what it does, what processes it is involved in, etc., are all observationally ‘up front’ in the informational matrix with which we are directly presented (pace Grosseteste, et al.) One has only to think about it to realise that any processes conceived as preceding, underlying and ‘leading to’ these ‘up-front’ presentations can be formulated, in the first place (that is, a priori) only as projections made from information as presented in direct perception. This means that the assumed time taken ‘for light to reach us from the source’, as a ‘process underlying and leading to’ that perception can be no more than a backwards extrapolation or inference from the information supplied in the very first instance by the light itself as we actually see it. The common conception of the Sun as being ‘eight minutes away’ and of our nearest star as ‘four light-years away’ is empirically unverifiable, because there is no way whatsoever in which we can see the light leaving the star and then time it over the intervening distance, as we can with the sound of a thunderclap after seeing the lightning or the sound of the axe after seeing it fall on a log in the far distance. This means that the definition of the ‘light-year’ as a measure of astronomical distance is logically circular, like measuring the speed of a road vehicle by timing it over a distance measured by the vehicle’s speed itself which, of course, gets us nowhere.

    Dispensing, then, with traditional dualism means that all the defining properties, qualities, dimensions and positions of things are there to be discovered by direct inspection in ordinary macro- and micro-phenomenal experience, using the appropriate tools and the best tried and tested practical scientific methods in standard instances of perception. All the things we can claim to know about, by means of microscopes, telescopes, Geiger-counters, galvanometers or whatever, analyse-out to those informational basics. But, of course, such ‘direct inspection’ of anything does not automatically guarantee its reality. All observations, no matter how meticulously ‘scientific’ they might be, have to be analysed for logical coherence and consistency of interpretation in the context of the developing structure of language as a whole. When this Linguistic Analysis is carried out to exhaustion, having satisfied all the logical and observational criteria, there remains, as we say, no sensible question as to what might lie ‘behind’, ‘beyond’ or ‘beneath’ that ultimate informational level in the way of hidden ‘mechanisms’ or ‘causes’ responsible for what there is. As Austin has argued, it scarcely ever makes sense (excluding clinical ophthalmology, of course) to declare that what we are seeing in paradigm cases of perception is an impression or an image of such an object – unless, of course, we are thinking of that object as seen through a lens, in a mirror, in a painting, a photograph, a movie or suchlike. Besides, he says, we are scarcely, if ever, really fooled by natural mirages or illusions. Such misperceptions are always, in the end-analysis, especially in consultation with others in the community, judged illusory or delusory with reference to the normal or paradigm instances of perception [62]. By that same token, the only causes or mechanisms it makes sense to contemplate are those that can be directly examined and logically analysed on ordinary standard levels of engineering, chemistry, agriculture, medicine, geology, practical physics etc. These causes are the ordinary pushes and pulls, warmings, freezings and so on, which are accountable in terms of the textbook rules of mechanics, dynamics and thermodynamics. Theoretical speculations as to what underlies that terminal of logical analysis as the hidden ‘causes’ of physical phenomena in general is not physics but metaphysics. As soon as we are satisfied that our perceptions make good logical and practical sense, such speculations as to what lies ‘beyond’ those perceptions are pointless, like a motor mechanic over-tuning a racing engine or a musician confusing an orchestra by seeking to tune his violin beyond perfect pitch.

    It is in practical physics, of course, that we plumb the depths of micro-physical reduction. Unfortunately, as we dig deeper into that micro-level the phenomena we encounter are nothing like as distinctive and assured as Austin’s duck, or the pistons, wheels, bearings and so on of mechanical engineering. The same goes for phenomena at the opposite end of the scale, that is, at great astronomical distances. There we may be dependent entirely on the sparsest data supplied by our instruments, hence on almost pure guesswork as to what those instruments are telling us. At those two extremes of expanding experience, our language at its developmental edges is under constant stress, being prone to the necessity of radical revision in many cases. The knock-on effects of these changes may, in some instances, even affect common language, throughout. For instance, the common language we are familiar with today, in talking about ‘telephones’, ‘astronauts’, ‘computers’ – even such commonplace words as ‘car’, ‘train’ and ‘aeroplane’ – would be entirely foreign to even the most educated savant of the Middle Ages. This makes it unlikely, to say the least, that the integrity of human language can ever be secured for all time purely by scholarly edict, in the form of the idealised, set-for-all-time Ordinary Language projected by the Linguistic Philosophers [63]. Ordinary language can never be so finished and static as these disparagingly named ‘Language Police’ imagined. In the face of expanding experience, language is always evolving and restructuring itself, not only at its outermost scientific edges but also, sometimes at the very root-level of ordinary commonsense.

    Nevertheless, despite these changes, the integrity of truly ‘ordinary language’, at its logical core, has to be maintained if human knowledge is not to disintegrate into a veritable ‘Tower of Babel’ of pure jargon – assuming, of course, that it has not done so already. For that reason, Normal Realism rejects the Cartesian duplication of common language as applied, on the one hand, to things as we perceive them – the phenomena – and, on the other hand, to purely theoretical concepts of what things are supposed to be ‘in themselves’ underlying those phenomena and existing independently of all perception and language. This means that entities of a purely theoretical and intrinsically inscrutable character, such as the space-travelling ‘photons’ of conventional light-theory, the in vacuo ‘force-fields’ of classical ‘gravitation’, ‘electrodynamics’, ‘nucleonics’, the ‘Big Bang’ and so on, do not figure in Normal Realism. Such abstractions are regarded as not only surplus to logical requirements but also intrinsically meaningless, hence inimical to any prospect of a natural understanding of the physical world [64].

    5.3. The Neo-Machian pure-information interpretation of light

    To summarise, then, thus far; in our Normal Realist approach to physics we jettison altogether the traditional ‘travelling photon’ interpretation of light in favour of a completely new interpretation in which light is conceived as energetic pixels of observed information. That light-information tells us, without any theoretical embroidery, that in the observational projection of distance, the distance is related to time in the constant ratio of conventional units $c$. In this ‘flipside’ view of $c$, light does not travel in the usual sense of the word. It consists of a matrix of interacting point-perspectives, as described in section 4.3 of the previous chapter. In Neo-Machian Normal Realism, therefore, there is no problem of reconciling quantum immediacy with our ordinary ideas of distance-separation. To the question of how a pair of atoms can be separated by astronomical distances and yet be in direct contact, the answer is that on the quantum level of that interaction there is no such thing as distance. We recall that at that level, what we ordinarily regard as the astronomical distance between those two atoms does not exist; that by the law of the Identity of Indiscernibles, what we regard as those two distance-separated events are actually one and the same event. Distance is no more a function of those single events than the distance of the ‘faraway hills’ in a video scenario is the function of single screen pixels. As we say, distance is a function of whole informational patterns and sequences of those single, discrete and indeterminate events.

    5.3.1. The phenomenon of physical causality

    Bearing in mind the fundamental distinction between quantum interaction and ordinary energetic, or causal interaction over distance, there is no hint of mystery in that conceptual area. The fact that this distinction between micro-physical quantum interaction and macro-physical action is not realised in contemporary physics is responsible for its chronic confusion over ‘instantaneous and delayed’ interaction. There simply cannot be any cause and effect at the level of single quanta, because energetic or causal interaction takes place only on the macro-physical level, involving statistical numbers of these discrete and indeterminate, instantaneous events. This is in accordance with the statistical Second Law of Thermodynamics, or Law of Entropy which governs all mechanical-causal processes. This Law of Entropy is, in effect, the law according to which any order must, in time, dissipate towards disorder, or randomness. An example of this is the steam engine in which a confinement of steam molecules in the boiler dissipates through the various pipes and pistons into the surrounding atmosphere to power the locomotive. Another example is the way in which the Sun warms our skin rather than cools it. It is also the reason why water boils on a stove and freezes in the fridge, rather than the other way around. Plainly, then, this statistical law of causality which applies, essentially, to large numbers of particles cannot apply to just one single particle.

    From this it follows that the Greek-based word ‘photon’, which Einstein used to describe the light quantum, as already discussed, is singularly inappropriate for our present purposes, due to its unwanted ‘space-travelling-particle’ and hard ‘bumpety-bump’-type causal connotations. For this reason, we prefer the Latinised version, photon to describe the Planck quantum (quantum pixel) insofar as it carries no mechanical overtones of the usual ballistic kind [65].

    All causality, then, takes place on the macro-physical level, in accordance with the statistical Law of Entropy, which is the law governing distributions of order and disorder. We have seen how this law applies to molecules in the boilers of steam engines. Now let us see how that same law applies to events in the context of pure disembodied information.

    In terms of modern Information Theory, an ordered collection of objects or events is information. Suppose you are walking along a flat and featureless stretch of sand after the tide has receded. You happen upon some unusual distribution of sand grains, in a lump, say, or a scratch on the surface. This tells you something. It may signify the remains of a sandcastle or a message of some kind scratched with a stick. Similarly, if in a radio receiver after a long period of uninterrupted hiss (called ‘white noise’) there is some change in the sound which catches your attention, this is information, as in Information Technology. On a scale of nought to one, nought signifies maximum randomness, or disorder, and one signifies complete order, or certainty. In between are the various measures of probability or improbability in the series or sequence of events. Heat transfer lies at the maximum-disorder (i.e., maximum entropy) end of the scale, and communication lies towards the minimum extremity of the entropy scale (maximum order or information).

    Where does light sit on this scale? The light quantum or photom, being the same in the object as in the eye or other optical instrument, is at the extreme of complete certainty – complete information in Information-Theoretical terms. Now, like light, heat is radiated in quanta. In fact, light is a form of heat, so that they both obey the same Thermodynamical law. This, of course, is why Sunlight warms and sometimes burns us. The only difference between heat and light is the distribution of the quanta. Light consists of those informational patterns of quanta which make things visible to us – though for some animals, such as snakes, heat makes things visible in what is to us complete darkness. The information which is contained in light, in all its spectral frequencies from infrared to far ultraviolet, lies somewhere towards the zero end of the scale of entropy, hence is more of negentropy (negative entropy, or order) than of entropy (disorder).

    All this, of course, has to do with radiated heat and light. But heat is dissipated by other means, namely, by conduction, convection – in air or water, say – and reflection in mirrors or other reflecting surfaces. Light is similarly dissipated, in conduction, reflection, diffraction, etc., as well as in the form of heat-energy. However, having said that light does not travel, it is recognised that most scientists would maintain that light does travel in a medium such as water, glass or air since they claim it can be ‘observed’ doing so. For example, light beams appear to shine through glass prisms. They are reflected in mirrors and appear to be bent in water. That is to say, they are reflected, refracted, diffracted, dispersed and so on, in all those ways well known to students of optics. Such processes are subject to entropy, in which the mediated quanta lose their directness and therefore the certainty of connection which there is in each single quantum (photum). This mediation places a ‘veil of uncertainty’ between the object and the observer, which does not exist at the level of single quanta.

    These appearances of motion in rays of light that are reflected, refracted and so on, are no more than phenomena consisting of sequences or relayings, throughout the medium, of discrete atomic illuminations, giving the impression of mechanical motion, like the advertisement signs that are lit by on-off sequences of flashing light-bulbs – what perception-psychologists call the ‘phi phenomenon’. Thus it is the interaction of the light with the atoms of these media which produces these phenomena. If there is no glass, water or any other medium present, that is, in a true vacuum, then by the very definition of a vacuum, no such relaying process can take place. In Normal Realism, this mediated ‘speed of light’ is conceived in the cinematic or sequential sense as the progression of energy in direct quantum jumps from one atom to another throughout the medium. It is the time dilation (to be discussed in Chapter 7, section 7.3) of these atomic interactions due to the normal heat-movement of these relaying atoms, which is characteristic of the energy-density of the particular medium, that explains the ‘slowing’ of the light in what is customarily called refraction. This is proved by the fact that when motion is added to the medium, as in the rapid flow of water in Fizeau’s U-tube experiment, the refraction is increased relatively to the observer in precisely the way it should be according to the relativistic time dilation formula [66].

    5.4. The quantum light-pixel again

    Light, then, is both energy and information, in patterns and sequences of quantum ‘bits’ which, in themselves, are not causally connected. Like the pixels on a television screen, they are basically random and featureless – as when the aerial is disconnected and the picture turns to ‘snow’. In our Normal Realist, space-in-light approach to physics, these quantum ‘pixels’ may be regarded as the true atomic fragments of the Eleatic One which the Greeks required to solve the problem of immobility described in section 2.1.2. It must be stressed, however, that in Normal Realism, these quantum ‘fragments’, are not bits of matter (mass), as Democritus assumed. As we say, they are bits of information, analogous to the ‘binary digit’ or ‘bit’ in modern computers and other digital apparatuses, but with the physical dimensions of action, namely, the product of energy and time in the constant relation $h$. To think of these bits of action as anything like bits of mass, or matter, is what we have described as a category mistake, a prescription for utter confusion (see Chapter 2, section 2.1.1).

    To contemplate this conceptual alternative to Democritus presents us with an intellectual challenge, which is to think of ‘atomism’ in terms of these transient informational energy-blips rather than permanent bits of solid matter. To achieve this conceptual ‘flip-over’ and bear it in mind, firmly and consistently without conventionalist interference, whilst working towards its full logical conclusion, is no mean feat. It requires us to invert, in our minds, the whole tradition of Democritan atomic materialism and see that long-established tradition as the conceptual ‘photo-negative’, as it were, of our true experience of nature. That is to say, it means seeing information as the primary substance, and matter as no more than secondary and derivative, a projection out of pure quantum information, and sticking to that idea consistently throughout. As already stated, this fits well with modern relativistic physics, according to which mass, length and time are as relative to observation as form, colour and perspective. In addition to that, it also fits perfectly with the phenomenalism of Mach, with whom, from the groundwork laid by Berkeley, et al., the whole idea of relativity originates.

    Logically speaking, this lateral-thinking alternative to Democritan atomism is at least as valid as its traditional opposite and, under different historical circumstances, might well have become the current paradigm. This would have offered some distinct and demonstrable advantages for our modern understanding of relativistic and quantum physics. For instance, as we say, instead of having to think of light as something travelling inscrutably in space, we might now be thinking of all space and its contents as being in the light; that is, as video-like projections out of dimensional relations between patterns and sequences of quantum light-pixels [67].

    Consequently, instead of being something merely incidental to the scheme of things, as in traditional physics, in our lateral-thinking alternative, light becomes the informational basis of everything there is, the very World, in effect. So, rather than being, as it now is, an alien and abstruse, counter-intuitive add-on to classical physics, Relativity is merged with Machian relativism to constitute the very core of a combined commonsense, de-jargonised New Physics. Being also quantised, or discretised, it places relativistic physics on a more appropriate digital or informational basis, instead of the classical analog or mechanistic basis provided by Democritus.

    This upgrade of physics from analog to digital also reflects the general Informational trend that is taking place throughout commerce, industry and the public media.

    In the following chapters our aim will be to explore the feasibility of this logically inverted, inside-out approach to physical phenomena. This, we propose, is a prime candidate for the ‘New Physics’ that NASA’s spokesman suggests may be needed to replace the current physics which now throws up so many inexplicable paradoxes and anomalies. Specifically, this new move is to replace what Mach rejected as the ‘atomistic doctrine of the present-day school’ with his phenomenalist approach to physics in the Radical Empiricist tradition of Locke, Berkeley, Hume and Kant. Normal Realism is the most systematic and up-to-date encapsulation of that same phenomenalist legacy as a critical reappraisal of our modern-day understanding of physical phenomena.

    First, however, we have to remind ourselves that the recommended paradigm revolution hinges vitally on the question of whether or not light has a ‘velocity in vacuo’, as Einstein claimed. Logically, of course, light cannot both have and not have a ‘velocity’. To achieve a decisive answer to this question, therefore, it is not sufficient simply to affirm the one alternative to refute the other, which is a well-known classic fallacy. The proper logical course in making the correct decision is to confirm the one alternative by refuting the other, thereby decisively removing the ambiguity. In the next chapter we proceed towards a decisive conclusion by refuting every possibility that light may have what Einstein called a ‘velocity in vacuo’.

    CHAPTER 6

    Proof of the Non-Velocity of Light

    It should be plain from what has been said so far about the history of the various interpretations of the phenomenon of light that there has never been any truly satisfactory conception of what, in fact, light is. At the end of Chapter 1 we saw that it is a fallacy to assume that because c is a ratio of distance units to time units, it is necessarily a speed, hence that c need not be thought of as a speed. In this present short chapter we go further, by demonstrating that whatever light is, it cannot possibly be something travelling in space. Here, for example, are ten short arguments that were presented by Pope in his talk at the Alternative Natural Philosophy Association (ANPA), in Cambridge, UK, 2006 [68]. These were aimed at proving that in the case of light, c cannot, logically, be interpreted as a speed. Here is the list of these arguments as presented to the Association.

    6.1. Ten logical reasons why the constant c cannot be a velocity

    1. The undeniable fact that c has the dimensions of distance divided by time explains all that is known about the times taken for communications over distance. But the fact that all speeds are distances divided by time by no means entails that all distances divided by time are speeds, which would be as absurd as saying that because all dogs are quadrupeds, all quadrupeds are dogs.
    2. Herman Bondi says:

      'Any attempt to measure the velocity of light is...not an attempt at measuring the velocity of light but an attempt at ascertaining the length of the standard metre in Paris in terms of time-units'[69].

      Also, it has been shown that all the practical consequences of Einstein's Special Theory of Relativity can be deduced much more simply by adopting Bondi's and Pope's interpretation of c as a pure 'conversion factor' for interconverting measures in metres into time-measures in seconds [70].

      The aim of these two arguments was to show that c need not necessarily be a 'speed' of anything. The following eight arguments contend that c cannot, logically, be a speed of anything.

    3. For light to be seen, photographed or detected in any possible way, it has to shine on something. In a vacuum there is, by definition, nothing on which it can shine. So, logically, light cannot be seen, photographed or in any other way be detected in the vacuum of space, which signifies a reduction to absurdity of experiments claiming to have photographed 'light travelling in vacuo'.
    4. To be seen or otherwise detected travelling in a vacuum, light would have to give off light. And that secondary light would have to give off light; and that tertiary light would also have to give off light ... and so on, ad infinitum, in a logical regress to absurdity.
    5. If c is interpreted as a 'velocity in the vacuum of space' (as Einstein's Second Postulate states), then in a vacuum to what can that 'velocity' possibly refer, constant or otherwise [71]? So the concept of light as having a 'velocity in space' is just another absurdity.
    6. Light is quantised in units of Planck's constant h. These quanta have been interpreted as 'flying photons', claimed to have been photographed 'in flight' by Nils Abramson [72].

      However, since the 'photon' is defined as a single, irreducible light-quantum, it has no energy to spare in manifesting itself anywhere between its point of emission and point of absorption. A quantum interaction between a pair of atoms therefore has to be instantly consummated, with there being no sensible question either as to where it is or what it does between its source and sink. There are simply no parameters to describe that alleged 'motion'. Any attempt to photograph or otherwise detect that quantum absorbs its whole packet of energy at that point, so that there can be no question of how it exists or travels when undetected, in vacuo.

    7. In order to conform to the law of conservation of energy, the alleged 'photon' cannot just hang around unconsummated in limbo, waiting to be absorbed. As Tom Phipps (Jr.) put it, 'the 'photon' sure don't have a holding pattern!'[73] So, what is a 'photon' when it is supposed to be travelling, say, between galaxies or, as it might be, en route to nowhere? [74] The whole concept is meaningless.
    8. Can light be scattered by light, as some experimenters have claimed? If a powerful laser-beam is shone across the path of another, do their 'photons' collide or their 'waves' interfere? In a simple experiment devised and carried out at Brunel university, in 1980 [75], two powerful lasers were beamed across each other's paths and also shone head-on at each other. No blocking or interference whatsoever was detected. If any such interference were to take place, then that light would suffer dispersion. Considering the amount of light that is allegedly 'criss-crossing' around, it would be amazing if visual acuity were possible over the length of a single metre. All the light that is allegedly shooting around in all directions would present as much a barrier to vision as the densest fog that can be imagined. The fact, then, that there are photographs of the farthest galaxies that display awesome clarity negates the validity of any such experimentalist claim.
    9. All velocities, properly so called, obey the rule of the composition of velocities, according to which the velocity of an object is different relative to differently moving observers. But c is, eminently, the same for all relatively moving observers, as Einstein's Relativity states and experiment confirms. Therefore, logically, c cannot be a velocity.
    10. For a velocity to be a velocity it has to be the velocity of something that is physically identifiable. In physics, both ancient and modern, there is nothing that can be physically identified as light travelling in vacuo, especially in view of Heisenberg's Indeterminacy Principle, which makes the 'track' of an alleged 'photon' absolutely indeterminate. If we think of what 'travels in vacuo' as 'waves', then what can possibly 'wave' in a vacuum? And if we think of what 'travels' as 'photons', then if those 'photons' travel at the 'speed of light', their mass has to be relativistically infinite at that 'speed'. The mass of a single photon would be as great as that of the whole universe. To escape this consequence by assuming that the 'stationary mass' of the photon is zero – as some physicists have claimed [76] – is untenable, for how can that 'zero mass' be conceived as a 'particle'? Besides, when is a photon ever regarded as stationary, since its alleged 'velocity' is c in all observational frames, bar none?

    It is pertinent to mention, in passing, the phenomenon of the windmill-like motion of the Crookes radiometer. The apparatus, sometimes called the 'light-mill', consists of an airtight glass bulb, enclosing an almost complete vacuum in which an array of vanes rotates freely in the horizontal plane when exposed to bright light. The vanes, silver on one side and black on the other, rotate at a rate depending on the intensity of the light.

    The facile interpretation of this phenomenon is that the mill rotates due to the pressure of light-corpuscles ('photons') striking and bouncing off the vanes. In fact, the phenomenon is proof of the very opposite. If it

    The Crookes radiometer really were the pressure of ballistic ‘photons’, then the vanes would be spun around by those particles striking their hard-reflecting silvered surfaces, whereas the dark sides of those vanes would absorb the kinetic energy of the particles like slack strings on an old tennis racquet. What really happens, however, is that the dark sides of the vanes are heated by the absorption of the light-energy which, by contact with those surfaces warms the thin residue of air-molecules inside the bulb. It is therefore the heat kinetic energy of the molecules against those warm, dark surfaces which, by reaction, rotates the vanes in a direction opposite to what it should if that rotation were caused by light-particles striking the ‘hard’ silvered surfaces. So, unlike the wind against the vanes of an anemometer or the sails of a windmill, the vanes of the radiometer are, as it were, ‘sucked around’ from inside the apparatus, not pushed around by the light from outside. At the very least, this should dispel any notion that the Crookes, so-called ‘radiometer’ is proof of a ballistic velocity on the part of light.

    So far as the Cambridge talk was concerned, there were no really challenging responses to these ten points of argument, from which it may be concluded that they were accepted, by default. However, one response, by a professor from SLAC (the Stanford Linear Accelerator Centre) in California, USA, was to say that experiments had proved that light can be scattered by light. From this he inferred that light had to be something travelling in vacuo in the way Einstein had claimed. However, another professor from that same institution countered this argument on the basis of experiments which he and a colleague had conducted. After some discussion, the claim that light scatters light was withdrawn, leaving what seemed to be a consensus, on all ten counts, that to conceive of light as having a ‘velocity in vacuo’, as Einstein stated, is logically untenable. Eventually, the Principal member of the Association (the late) Professor Clive Kilmister, wrote to Pope saying that he had now accepted Pope’s long-standing and long-rejected contention that there can be no such thing as the travelling ‘photon’. It was also declared, by another member, Prof. James Lindesey, at a meeting of the Association, in 2004, that he and a colleague of his at SLAC had experimentally confirmed Pope’s contention.
