When the outer ring is put into a regular swing (by an electromagnetic 'kicker' in the base) the porpoises remain stationary at first and then begin, sporadically, to rock around their axis. While the input to the device (the battery kicker) remains constant, there are occasions when the swing of those porpoises turns into a full and rapid rotation. When this happens the swing amplitude of the main, that is the outer, ring is observed to decrease. Continued observation reveals that an increase in rotation of either of the two main parts of the apparatus is obtained at the expense of the other. This, of course, is in perfect accord with the law of

    A complex angular momentum system conservation of angular momentum which is the same for this toy as for whirlpools, planetary systems and galaxies. This table-top apparatus includes in itself all forms of angular momentum, including orbital, spin – and swing – as observation reveals.

    10.8. Angular momentum and inertia

    This overall integrity of angular momentum has profound implications for what we customarily think of as momentum and inertia. When we are riding in a bus and the driver applies his brakes, what is it that throws us forward? Customarily, we think of it as the force of 'momentum', or 'inertia'. However, this not a force. There is no 'force' throwing us forward. The real force is what is holding us back. It is the force exerted by the seat belt, our muscles on the hanging-strap and whatever, which is preventing our bodies from moving smoothly forward in the way they did before the driver braked.

    It is the same if we are orbiting freely inside a space vehicle. When the pilot operates the rocket-motor to accelerate the ship we are thrown towards the frame of the ship in whichever direction is opposite to that of the motor's thrust. This is where some surface or other stops our bodies from orbiting freely with respect to the Earth, the Sun, the stars and so on in the way they did before the thrust was applied. It is only in such situations that we can, so to speak, ‘feel’ the presence of those distant bodies. As Ernst Mach, is reported to have said, ‘When the subway train jerks it is the fixed stars that throw you down’ [108].

    This integrated angular momentum connection with distant bodies is instantaneous in the sense that nothing has to travel at any kind of speed between one’s body and those distant bodies to connect the application of the brakes and one’s body being ‘thrown down’. The two events are absolutely, on-the-spot simultaneous. The same applies to those distant bodies in relation to one another. Any jolt, or change in the angular momentum that occurs to any one of these bodies instantly affects its balance with others in the overall angular momentum nexus of which their correlated orbital motions are an inseparable part.

    These angular momentum connections may be described as ‘felt’ connections, insofar as the torsional changes in their orbits are accompanied by the sorts of visceral sensations which sometimes cause motion sickness due to what is customarily called ‘inertia’. But how do these felt connections compare with their seen connections? The answer is as follows.

    10.9. Mach’s Principle and instantaneous action-at-a-distance

    Suppose we see, at a distance, the jolt that in the one body affects all the others in that instantaneous way. When a piece of the Shoemaker-Levy comet struck Jupiter in July 1994, was that event seen at the same instant that our seismographs might have recorded it here on Earth? Since, as we have said, a quantum interaction between two separate atoms is an event that happens at both of those atoms at once, it follows that any jolt sensed by the one atom is a jolt sensed instantly by the other atom, regardless of the distance between them [26]. So our answer to the question of whether the seen and the felt interactions are simultaneous has to be ‘yes’. (There is no contradiction in this because proper time instantaneity $t = 0$ and relativistic time-delay $t_R = s/c$ are complementary aspects of the same time-dilational equation: see Appendix 1, section A1.2.)

    These immediately felt connections which our bodies have with other bodies in general answer to what is sometimes called Mach’s Principle, which implies an instantaneous (proper time-instantaneous) overall action-at-a-distance connection between all bodies in the universe. This is the ‘universal’ frame of reference contemplated by Mach as the reference-zero for rotational motion – what astronomers, in Einstein’s words, call ‘the reference-frame of the fixed stars’. It is the reference-frame with respect to which, as Mach pointed out, all gyroscopes orientate themselves, and, of course, all free orbits and spins of bodies are ‘gyroscopes’ in their way. It is this same universal frame which serves as the reference zero of time dilation, described in Chapter 8, section 8.4. That is to say, its intrinsic time-standard is what, in POAMS, we have called Deep-Space Time (DST).

    As it turns out, then, inertia, the action-at-a-distance connection between bodies envisaged by Mach, is basically quantised in the same way as light – that is, into ‘cinematic’ time-sequences of instantaneous quantum connections or ‘stills’, which, in observational space and time exhibit, statistically, a wave-like probability-function with the constant distance-time progression-rate $c$.

    If the reasoning in this volume is correct, then it transpires that in the philosophical synthesis called Normal Realism, all the differently named phenomena, such as ‘electricity’, ‘magnetism’, ‘gravitation’, ‘inertia’, and so on, hitherto regarded as being of different ‘natures’, are all part and parcel of one and the same Nature. This Nature is that of a single overall substance. This substance is not matter, or mass, as customarily supposed, but angular momentum in all its multitudinous shapes and forms, of orbits, spins, swings, oscillations and the like. In angular momentum, matter, space and time are not different categories of phenomena. They are all inseparable parts of a single, integrated phenomenon combining space, matter, time and motion.

    CHAPTER 11

    Angular Momentum, the Universal Substance

    11.1. Angular momentum systems

    The last three chapters have emphasised the central role that angular momentum plays in Normal Realism. In this chapter, we examine some of the further characteristics of this universal phenomenon. Let us first consolidate our understanding of angular momentum. As already discussed, all angular momentum systems consist of at least two masses in a reciprocally balanced relation, like that of children on a playground see-saw. The only difference is that whereas the ends of the see-saw oscillate up and down, the masses at the ends of the moment arm of the angular momentum relation continue round and round. Just like the fulcrum on which the see-saw balances, in an angular momentum relation between two orbiting mass particles there is also a centre of balance (barycentre) somewhere between those particles, whose position depends on the difference in their masses. As we have seen, that centre is devoid of mass or any other physical properties in itself. In other words, with nothing there that can produce or reflect light, it fits the description of a void.

    This can be puzzling, because in the case of, say, the solar system, since the Sun is so massive compared with the planets, the barycentre of that system, although it is off-set from the centre of the Sun, remains within the Sun. To describe the barycentre as a ‘void’ in this case seems confusing, since it lies within the matter of the Sun. It is therefore not the sort of void which displaces the Sun’s matter, like a void in concrete, a metal casting or a bubble of air which displaces water. In this case, the barycentre is purely a locus, which just happens to be positioned within the body of the Sun. However, if the planet Jupiter, say, were very much larger than it is, then the barycentre between it and the Sun could be somewhere out in space between the two bodies. This balancing point between the two bodies would be the same regardless of what other bits of matter might be present. That barycentre, in itself, is still devoid of mass. So, in itself, the centre of an angular momentum system has no substance to displace other substances, and it certainly has no propensity to emit, absorb or reflect light. Far less does it possess any power to attract and incarcerate light, as proposed by some of the more outlandish theories of these so-called ‘singularities’.

    In summary, in the simplest case, an orbital angular momentum system consists of at least a pair of masses separated by a void. But what about a case where many masses are balanced around a common barycentre? Is that barycentre still a void? The answer is yes, because the principle of balance (known as the Law of Moments) holds for many particles as for just two. What is it, then, which draws the particles inwards towards the centre of a vortex? Is it a force of some special kind? No, it is simply that in the nature of angular momentum, any loss of potential energy in the system, howsoever caused, makes its particles orbit nearer and nearer towards the centre with ever-increasing kinetic energy and velocity. This, of course, is what produces the characteristic spiralling or vortical effect of a many-particle angular momentum system.

    So, again, the only force that may be involved is that which may be required to resist this orbiting motion. In a hurricane or tornado it is the friction exerted on the swirling air molecules, by the trees that are torn up, the houses that are damaged or destroyed, the vehicles lifted off the ground, and so on, and it is only when it has exhausted its energy against these various forms of resistance that the storm is abated. It is well known to astronomers that in spiral galaxies the stars have a tendency to coalesce in the densest regions and that up to certain distances from the centre these compactions rotate more in the manner of solid discs than of free particles, as the planets do in our solar system. This is because the closer together the stars become, the more orbitally attached they get to one another as they orbit the galaxy’s centre, in which case they tend to orbit that barycentre together, en masse, in almost a solid, disc-like way.

    It is to be stressed, once again, that in this account of the spiral motions of galaxies there is no question of any invisible super-mass, lying at the centre of the motion ‘dragging’ everything towards it by ‘gravitational force’. As we have seen, in Normal Realism, angular momentum alone is perfectly sufficient to explain the phenomenon with no assumptions regarding in vacuo forces of any kind, and certainly not those allegedly exerted by ‘singularities’

    With weather systems such as cyclones, hurricanes, tornados, etc., it is the increasing kinetic energy of particles as they converge towards the angular momentum barycentre that does all the damage. So, what is it that draws the stars towards the centre in the case of a galaxy? Where do they go as the galaxy spirals inwards? It can’t be down some cosmic ‘plughole’. ‘Wormholes into different universes’ and such like, play no role in POAMS, not least because there is no direct evidence of such theoretical entities, which makes them unempirical as well as simply nonsensical.

    We maintain that stars are drawn towards the centre of a spiral galaxy simply due to a loss of potential energy. The fact is that these galaxies lose energy in a continuous broad spectrum throughout the whole range of frequencies, from deepest infrared to farthest ultraviolet, X-rays and gamma. As a galaxy swirls inwards, unlike a whirlpool, it loses its energy more in the way an atom (qua quantum accumulator) does, by the release of its light-quanta into the receptive environment consisting of resonant interaction with other atoms like itself, both near and far. On an immensely large scale, a galaxy is continually losing such prodigious amounts of luminous energy that the farthest of them so far known is detectable here on Earth. Unlike a whirlpool, therefore, a spiral galaxy sheds its energy, not down some theoretical ‘plughole’ but from all over in the luminosity of its stars. Moreover, spiral galaxies also shed energy in huge jets of material which are known to be ejected from their centres by analogy with water-spouts and tornados which lose their energy by distribution into their environment. The more densely compacted the stars and other bits of matter become in approaching the centres of spiral galaxies, the more spectrographically bright and energy-shedding objects they are. All this, of course, is characteristic of angular momentum in all its forms.

    So then, in shedding energy, a galaxy naturally shrinks towards its barycentre in the manner of an atom losing its quanta. So what happens to all those stars that are drawn towards the barycentre? It must be that they end up in a great bulge that is so typical of spiral galaxies such as our Milky Way and its galactic neighbour, Andromeda. A most spectacular bulge of this sort is in the Sombrero galaxy, seen edge-on in Plate 5 below. Within this bulge, some of the centralised angular momentum decentralises and is re-distributed among the pairs of stars (binaries) like froth around a whirlpool or soap-suds around a sink. This is in the manner of a very large cluster of stars held together by nothing more than their individually paired angular momenta, without the coherence of a spiralling system. Indeed there are known to be such small collections, containing up to about a million stars, accompanying spiral galaxies but well outside them. It seems reasonable, therefore, to suppose that these ‘globular clusters’ of stars are spin-offs from the central bulges of the spiral galaxies, relieving them of their over-compaction of stars. At least this seems more feasible than the current conceptions of the stars disappearing beyond recall down cosmical conduits either into oblivion or to reappear in other parts of the universe – or other universes – at which point of speculation physics ceases to be physics and metaphysics takes over. It is staggering to contemplate that such a plunge of true science into such pseudo-science is still widely accepted during these early years of the 21st century.

    Now, whether or not the centres of these spiral galaxies are conceived as ‘Black Holes’, ‘wormholes’ or as simply the voids which naturally occur at the centres of all angular momentum systems, the consensus view among astronomers is that such central voids are characteristic of all spiral galaxies, including the bulging Sombrero galaxy. By that same consensus, there can be no information as to what the ‘insides’ of these voids might be. What does a ‘nothing’ consist of? What does it do? What powers does it have? These questions create conundrums, like the old film advert ‘Come and see the Invisible Man.’ The galactic void is therefore as much a void in the epistemological sense as in the physical sense – more of a null-and-void, one might say – than a void as ordinarily understood. From the point of view of conceptual economy, therefore, known as Ockham’s Razor, calling the void a ‘Black Hole’ is a flagrant and unnecessary misuse of language, an over-elaboration of the plain fact that the entity in question is simply a void. And a void is a void. Calling it a ‘Black Hole’ is to give it a quality on an ordinary colour scale of ‘black’, and to ascribe to it a power of ‘gravitational attraction’ gives it the property of mass, hence is another example of the category mistake, as described in Chapter 2, section 2.1.1. To say the very least, this is conceptually misleading. Normal Realism maintains that the so-called internal properties of this void will never be determined. This prediction of Normal Realism can be added to its prediction of the failure of any experiment at CERN to re-create the conditions of the ‘Big Bang’ [109].

    It can hardly be over-emphasised, then, that one of the strangest conceptions in modern physics has to be that of these angular momentum centres as autonomous entities which can exist apart from and independent of their surroundings, being able to pop up anywhere and roam around on their own – as if the centre of a hurricane or a whirlpool could jump out of the medium in which it appears and wander about seeking for things to consume, which, to speak plainly, must surely be one of the silliest ideas on record!

    In this shrinking and rotating process, towards the ends of their lives, galaxies, like angular momentum systems generally, lose their angular momentum by shedding energy and therefore, eventually, losing their coherence as galaxies. With their potential energy exhausted, they return to what they may have started out as, that is, loose collections of stars in their various stages of disintegration into interstellar gas and other space-junk. With further loss of potential energy into the environment – hence diminution of angular momentum – the particles in this junk orbit one another more and more closely together, eventually to re-form into another galaxy, and so on, ad infinitum. This is all in a steady state, overall-conserved angular momentum universe of indefinite extent, where some stars and galaxies die as others are born. (In Normal Realism the interpretation of the Hubble Redshift dictating an ‘expanding universe’ is a fallacy: see Chapter 12.)

    Now, whether or not this steady-state scenario is true is a moot point – as, of course, is every competing interpretation of the relevant phenomena. The crux of the matter, at this juncture is that in all these cases, from atoms to whirlpools, cyclones and spiral galaxies, it is in the angular momentum system as a whole that all the causal factors of the physical phenomena are to be found. The same applies to atoms as to galaxies and to all other angular momentum systems. The only difference is that in the case of atoms there is no space as such inside the atom. All the qualities and quantities of things in the objective physical scenario are on the outside of these atoms. This is where the true reality resides. The atoms, then, are the true substance of physical reality, literally the ‘standing under’ of everything, as the word was originally understood. Thus, whereas matter and space are combined within the definition of angular momentum, the void, on the most fundamental micro-physical level, is the ultimate non-substance which lies at the root of things and without which, as Democritus realised, there can be neither motion nor change. This same principle applies right down to those atoms which are the ultimate units of angular momentum, the terminal irreducibles of all physical analyses. Far from being the solid-throughout material particles of Democritus and classical physics, these atoms of Normal Realist conception, are, essentially, angular momentum configurations whose ultimate interiors are not matter but void. This completely inverts the Democritan hypothesis by putting the void on the inside of the atom and substance on the outside together with all its definitive physical dimensions and properties. It also solves the Problem of Knowledge described in Chapter 2, section 2.2.

    Now in the Democritan way of thinking there were two separate and distinct physical entities. The one was ‘matter’, or material substance, and the other was ‘space’. As Democritus conceived it, each of these two physical entities existed absolutely and self-sufficiently. In other words, if all the matter were somehow to disappear, the space would remain, and if all the space were to vanish, the matter would, somehow, still be there, albeit with all the atoms jammed together in the cosmic gridlock of an Eleatic stasis. Relics of this classical atomic materialism have remained at the root of our ideas of physics right up to this present time.

    11.2. End of the Old Order

    Signs of a sea-change, however, in this materialist mind-set began with the emergence of relativity theory. The modern originator of this change was, as previously discussed, not Einstein but Mach. Let us, then, consolidate our understanding of what we know about Machian relativity. For this purpose of consolidating so many, doubtless unfamiliar ideas, some repetition of these and previously presented arguments is necessary.

    For Mach, as we have said, the true substantial reality on which all physical science is based is not the underworld of Democritan atomic mechanisms, which he so vigorously rejected, but the immediate world of direct and instrumental observation – in a word, phenomena. Unfortunately, it seemed to Mach’s critics, as to those of his two-centuries-earlier predecessor, George Berkeley, that this relativism was no more than a form of Subjective Idealism (see Chapter 2, section 2.4 and Chapter 4, section 4.2), hence implying a loss of that solid and unchangeable ‘material substance’ on which they imagined commonsense science was based. Matter, space and time, for Mach’s materialist opponents, therefore, were absolutes of just the sort Democritus had conceived. This was anathema for Mach. So far as he was concerned, there can be no space other than as one of the three measure-categories of observational projection, or polar perspective (see Chapter 7 section 7.1.), the remaining two being, of course, mass and time, all three of which together are characteristic of physical phenomena. For Mach, as for commonsense, there is no possible way of describing how the physical world appears from everywhere all at once, as if observed by some imagined cosmic ubiquity. All physical dimensions for him were therefore, as we have seen, point-centred, whereas ordinary grid-space (Cartesian space), is the linear, point-to-point extrapolation in communication and physical interaction of these many individual perspectives. For Mach, of course, so far as scientific knowledge is concerned, polar spaces are epistemologically a priori, or primitive, and Cartesian are a posteriori, or secondary.

    Notwithstanding the epistemological primacy of the polar spaces, it is the Cartesian space, or grid-space, that is generally taken as the objective, or communal space of physical science. In this ‘absolute space’ as customarily conceived, the relativistic or observer-dependent nature of matter, space and time does not normally reveal itself. As is now known, it is only with motions which are uncommonly large that the relativistic nature of physical dimensions and properties is disclosed. This altogether intrusive discovery makes such an assault on our customary mind-set that even today, it has remained notoriously puzzling and, in some cases, highly contentious (see the reference to ‘Dissidence’ in Chapter 2, section 2.4).

    It is to that ordinary objective grid-space aspect of physical phenomena that we now turn our attention, to be discussed in those language-forms that are appropriate to that communal or ordinary Cartesian extrapolation.

    Bearing this in mind, let us recall that in common with Mach and in contrast to the Democritan paradigm, in Normal Realism there is only one substance in existence, and that is the indissoluble union of the measures length, time and mass which is angular momentum as directly observed in the orbital motions of all astronomical bodies. This angular momentum is also observationally ‘up front’, not hidden in any esoteric way. Furthermore, it is the same – that is to say, invariant – in all observational frames of reference. In angular momentum we have in itself, by definition, the observed spatial separations between bodies as well as their masses and motions. Had this been known at the time of Democritus (which, of course, it could not) it was all that was needed to solve the Eleatic problem of immobility without creating those paradoxes associated with the name of Zeno. Angular momentum is therefore not something which occurs ‘in space’, as classical science conceives it, it is space – and mass, and motion. That is to say, in Normal Realism, as for Mach, there is no space, nor time, nor mass, nor motion other than as defined, fundamentally, in the relations between bodies in measurable angular momentum phenomena.

    As the cohesive principle among freely orbiting bodies, unlike Newtonian ‘gravitation’, the law of conservation of angular momentum demands that the total angular momentum of an orbiting body must include that of any angular momentum the body may have, in addition to that of its orbit, such as, for example, the swing of a pendulum or the spin of an artificial satellite. This means (‘perish the thought’) that the so-called ‘gravitational constant’, $G$, has to be a variable for fast-spinning bodies (see Chapter 12, section 12.4 and Appendix 5). It promises a simple and straightforward solution of two so-far unexplained anomalies. One is NASA’s Pioneer anomaly and the other is that of the so-called ‘missing mass of the universe’. In Normal Realism, these are not two anomalies but one and the same anomaly; that is, they are both due to the neglect of spin in the orbital mechanics of Newton, which is used by the NASA scientists and astronomers to calculate orbital motion of space-probes and galaxies. However, recall that it is a plain fact that all bodies in the universe spin, from satellites and planets to spiral galaxies. Ignoring these prodigious, cumulative amounts of spin angular momentum, especially in the case of the multitudes of spiral galaxies, is bound to create anomalies. Another anomalous instance of the same kind is that of the Earth-colliding asteroid, TC3 which, in 2008, exploded in the air above the Nubian desert in northern Sudan, sprinkling the ground with its fragments. NASA’s calculations of the track taken by that rapidly tumbling asteroid, like those of their Pioneer space-probes, turned out to be significantly wide of the mark, as its bits landed further south than was anticipated. The ‘gravitational’ equations that had been used to predict the track of that fast-spinning asteroid were the same Newtonian kind as in the case of the Pioneer anomaly, which makes it at least feasible that this incident with the TC3 asteroid in the Nubian desert might prove to be just another instance of that same anomaly.

    In any case, putting spin into the orbital equation of motion solves both the Pioneer anomaly and the ‘missing mass’ anomaly at a stroke, dispensing with any need to postulate the existence of some strange and chronically undetectable ‘dark matter’, ‘dark energy’ or whatever. This will be discussed further in Chapter 12, section 12.4. In the present chapter, we now return to the question of what the fundamental ‘substance’ is and what it is not.

    11.3. The true non-substance, or ‘void’

    As already stated, the crucial difference between the Democritan concept of matter as the primary substance and that of angular momentum as the primary substance is that whereas in the former conception the ‘atom’ is a solid-throughout piece of matter, or mass, in the latter, the barycentre of the ultimate ‘atom’ (the quantum accumulator) is nothing at all, that is, essentially, a fundamental emptiness, that is, a hole or void, like the barycentre of a spinning bolas. This complete inversion of conventional atomism presents an intellectual challenge, and a very demanding one at that, which is to switch radically from thinking of ‘fundamental physical particles’ as solid and self-sufficient entities floating about freely in a Democritan void, to thinking of them instead as no more than, as we say, terminal irreducibles of the universal nexus of angular momentum.

    In POAMS, then, it is the directly observable and measurable phenomenon of angular momentum that is the primary universal substance (proto ousia, as Aristotle named it), and ‘matter’ (mass) is no more than just one of the three formal dimensional components of that measure, the other two being, as we have seen, length and time. So ‘mass’, in this context, is nothing like the homogeneous material ‘stuff’, or substance of classical, Democritan materialism. In Normal Realism, as in the standard orthodox theory of relativity, matter (mass) is a measure that cannot exist in isolation but only in the interrelations of objects within the context of some specific observation, hence is as relative to observational perspective as any other dimension, property or quality of phenomena. In this particular sense, like beauty, matter may be said to be ‘in the eye of the beholder’ – or the ‘observer’ in the specialised language of relativity. And so, of course, is space, being absolutely inseparable from that whole dimensional package on which empirical physics is built.

    The space, then, which Democritus interposed between bits of matter in order to break the Eleatic stasis, cannot truly be a void, in the special sense conceived by the Eleatics. For them, the ‘void’ was as an absolute nothing (no thing), a complete emptiness, not even space, a concept which it makes no sense even to think about. To speak, as we do today, of a nothing, or void, as a something positively existing – namely, a measurable hole, or gap – would therefore have been entirely paradoxical for those Greeks, which, of course, is why the thinkers like Zeno made such a big issue of it $^{[110]}$.

    Thus it seems from the linguistic or literal point of view, that right at its very beginning, our physical science, ‘got off on the wrong foot’. This was with the Eleatic argument that led to the consequence of the undifferentiated and solid-throughout Eleatic Unity from which, basically, our Western tradition of atomic materialism stems. When subjected to the method of Linguistic Analysis $^{[111]}$ it transpires that this Eleatic argument, which was so influential in its launching, by Democritus, of atomic physics, and prominent though it was to become, was just plain false. With the greatest respect for those early thinkers, it was a case of ‘Ask a silly question and you get a silly answer’.

    11.4. The fundamental holes or gaps

    In any normal use of language it is perfectly sensible to say that in, for example, a string or sequence of informational bits, as in ordinary discourse, writing, or a Morse-code message, there may be breaks or gaps between the symbols or periods during which it makes sense to say that nothing is conveyed. In that case, it would be strange to infer from this that the ‘nothing’, here, means that there is really no break, hence that the sequence in question is an unbroken ‘Eleatic’ continuum. In the normal way of speaking, to say that the sequence is interrupted by a period of ‘nothing’ makes perfect sense. Also perfectly understood in modern normal-language terms is that the length of that nothing is a measure of the period of interruption in the context of the message as a whole, which means that a ‘nothing’, or void, may actually have a time dimension – in which case it is definitely a something, that is to say, a something which has physical measure. In a music score, for instance, the lengths of the pauses between notes have definite time measure and significance without which the ‘music’ would be sheer cacophony. In the case of the broadcast message the ‘nothing’ or ‘break’ might stretch over seconds, minutes, weeks or whatever. So, also, in the case of the tornado or spiral galaxy, the ‘eye’ or ‘hole’, despite being a void, has definite dimensions and effects as measured by meteorologists and astronomers, on scales not only of size, but also of strength, direction, barometric pressure, duration and so on, which gives it an existence as an actual ‘hole’, but only in the context of its environment. Otherwise, to think of a void as having a reality and dimensions in and of itself, as a hole existing on its own with no reference to anything else is, obviously, pure nonsense. There simply cannot be dimensions or qualities to absolute nothing.

    In the primitive Eleatic argument, then, all that talk of nothing is self-negating. To say that since there is no nothing everything is jammed together, was a fallacy, whence so, automatically, was Democritus’ ‘atomistic’ answer to it, a fallacy which was bequeathed to the whole tradition of Western science that followed from it. It was, then, simply not the case, that, as the Eleatics assumed, a separation by nothing entails an undifferentiated jammed-together cosmic Unity. The fallacy they were embracing, especially Democritus, was to conflate the meanings of ‘space’ and ‘void.’ There is no way in which physical space, even a completely empty space, can truly be nothing. Space is decidedly a something, a specific something defined by the set of dimensions, three in all, of observational reality, along with the other physical dimensions, such as mass and time from which, in Normal Realism, space is inseparable [112].

    To summarise this point of vital linguistic significance concerning the development of our Western tradition of Science, the conflation of these terms ‘space’ and ‘void’ in our conventional physics language is a chronic confusion. The word void is normally used in ordinary language to signify something like a ‘hole’, ‘hollow’, ‘cavity’, etc. However, it is to be reiterated, that in no such normal sense of the word can a void be described as existing absolutely as a measurable thing-in-itself, like the ‘space’ of Democritan physics – or the imaginary ‘Black Hole’ of contemporary cosmology. So it needs to be stressed that the space of ordinary experience, from astronomy to microphysics, is not truly a void in the original Eleatic sense of a nothing. It is that triad of dimensions which is a dimensional component of the phenomenon of angular momentum, inseparable from its other dimensions, of mass and time. What is truly a void as the Eleatics read it is an absolute non-substance, such as, say, the disjunction between two consecutive events at the quantum level. As already discussed, trying to envisage what that disjunction is ‘in itself’ is a category confusion, like trying to describe what the cat and the mouse are doing between the stills of a Tom and Jerry cartoon, or the behaviour of an ‘electron’ in between two quantum levels in an energy-transacting atom. In these cases, there is nothing intrinsic to these disjunctive ‘quantum jumps’, or gaps, by which they can be described or measured. They are truly the voids, or essential dimensionless disjunctions which, ideally, should have been introduced by a contemporary of Democritus in order to circumvent the logical consequence of the Eleatic continuum, or stasis. This would have avoided the traditional ‘solution’ which has led us ‘up the garden path’ into the millennia of atomic materialism and all its scientific and most unwanted societal spin-offs which have characterised our Western society. (These societal implications will be reviewed in Chapter 13).

    So much, then, for the ‘nothings’ that divide, separate, or fundamentally discretise physical action in the ‘cinematic’ manner described. However, the same linguistic principle applies as much to the atoms themselves as to the quanta. As already discussed, the original concept of the atom as a solid-all-through piece of matter, or mass, induces us to think of it as something concrete that, with sufficient energy applied, can be cracked open like a nut to reveal its kernel. In contrast, from our anti-Democritan ‘inside-out’ point of view, just like quantum interstices, these ‘insides’ of atoms have no physical existence other than in terms of those physical phenomena of which they are the terminal irreducibles.

    This brings us to the switching point between what we have identified, analogously, as the language of the actors in the drama, and that of director or film-maker. Two such complementary languages need to be carefully distinguished, whereas in physics, right up to the present time, that distinction between the physical phenomena and their constituent micro-processes has been overlooked. This ignorance has created a bogus microphysics described in the same sort of language that is applied to such things as planets, beads and billiard balls. In this misapplied language the names our contemporary physics gives to these ‘particles’, therefore invite us to think of ‘electrons’, ‘protons’ and the like, and even ‘quarks’, as real, solid, self-sufficient entities functioning in nature like the cogs and gears of mechanical engineering [113]. In fact, however, far from these supposed entities being the ‘building-blocks’ of ordinary macro-physical phenomena, they are no more than the ultimate ciphers we finally encounter in our reductive end-analysis of all physical measures and which forestalls the consequence of an absolutely determinate Eleatic continuum. (Mathematically speaking, the ultimate units of any measure cannot be zero, because the sum of any number of zeroes is always zero, which means that there have to be ultimate limits of reduction on all physical measures at some level or other.) We maintain, then, that the only existence these ‘subatomic particles’ may have is within the context of macro-physical phenomena, being indescribable other than in terms of those surroundings. In other words, ‘subatomic particles’ and ‘atoms’ cannot sensibly be conceived as elements out of which those surroundings are constructed.

    11.5. The ‘Driving Forces of Nature’

    This, of course, turns classical atomism on its head so that, as literal nonentities, atomic particles, like the centres of cyclones and hurricanes, cannot be conceived as having any powers in themselves to ‘create’, ‘cause’ or ‘explain’ phenomena. ‘But what about nuclear fission and fusion?’ someone asks. ‘In fission, don’t the volatile and unstable nuclei of some atoms explode spontaneously, independently of their surroundings; and don’t these tiny explosions precipitate the much larger ones that are observed on the scale of atomic bombs?’ The answer is that it is impossible to think of an atom, however unstable and volatile, existing and acting on its own relative to nothing else. These ‘spontaneous triggering explosions’ of volatile particles can occur only in relational contexts where the potentials for providing these occurrences are statistically assured. For instance, a speck of dust may precipitate a rainstorm, but only in an atmosphere that is already saturated with water vapour, otherwise the dust particle in itself can do nothing. Similarly, the fission of a nucleus and its ‘knock-on effects’ on other nuclei can take place only in the presence of those other inter-resonant particles in statistical compactions that are critical for their stability or instability. The same goes for nuclear fusion, which requires that nuclei are ‘forced together’ in surroundings of great heat and pressure, as in the case where, in a star – such as our Sun – hydrogen nuclei are ‘compressed together’ to form the heavier nuclei of helium. In both these cases, of fission and fusion, it is in the inter-resonant relations with their surroundings, not in the particles themselves, that the potential, or premium, resides for the exponential effect that provides nuclear energy on a commercial or military scale. Besides, as we have seen, conservation of energy forbids that an atom can either lose or gain energy without some other atom somewhere else instantly gaining or losing it. The same applies to any other ‘subatomic particle’ interactions, however we might conceive them.

    Let us recall that experiments of the Thomas Young two-slit variety reveal that no such ‘particles’ manifest themselves in any definite way between the source and sink of an interaction [46]. They virtually disappear at their source and then reappear on the screen in patterns (fringes) as if, like waves, they had travelled through both slits at once – which, of course, is the origin of that chimera nonsensically called the ‘wave-particle’. However, as we have seen, there is no possible physical description of how, as autonomous ‘particles’, they ‘travel’ through those slits or how they ‘exist’ over that intervening distance between the source and sink. As we have also seen, the same applies to the particles in bubble-chambers, which leave no tracks in between the bubbles in the chamber, any more than they do between the source and screen in the two-slit experiment – or between a source and sink in the complete emptiness of space. This is the propensity of entities on the micro-phenomenal level to disappear at one place and reappear somewhere else, cinematically, as we say, like the ‘electron’ between one quantum level and another. Being completely discrete and consummate, their ‘transfer’ is immediate and proper time-instantaneous, which, in experiments of the two-slit variety makes them appear to seek their destinations as if with foreknowledge of the places and conditions of their reception [46]. This, of course, is simply because in the proper times of those quantum interactions, they are, so speak, ‘there as soon as they start out’ being, literally, in both places at once. This makes perfect sense in the quantum-still language whereas in the language we customarily use for describing the trajectories of satellites and bullets it creates chronic puzzlement.

    11.6. ‘Waves’ in vacuo

    Where, then, are these particles while, in the scenario language, they are traversing huge distances in space between a galactic source, say, and a terrestrial detector? The answer Normal Realism gives is, nowhere. As we have seen, in the vacuum those ‘fundamental particles’ simply don’t exist. It is the opposite way around, that the space – the vacuum – exists only as a projection from the actual on-the-spot appearances of those particles in statistical amounts in what might be called the

    ‘collapsing’ of the probability function. It is stressed here that this is not the ‘wave-function’ of quantum ‘wave-dynamics’ but a function of pure probabilities, stripped of any ‘wave-like’ connotations. The only straight answer extant physics can give to that same question of where the particle is and what it is doing in the intervening space is: ‘God knows!’ which is akin to what might be said of what is happening in the jump between two stills in a cinematographic film. (See Chapter 9, section 9.4.)

    From the standpoint of Normal Realism this customary language of fundamental ‘waves’ and ‘particles’ is as inappropriate in physics as the language that conflates the meanings of ‘space’ and ‘void’. However, so as not to suggest having to rewrite the whole of physics language – which at this present stage in our scientific history would be practically unthinkable – the customary textbook system of classifications and divisions of these ‘fundamental’ entities such as ‘light-waves’, ‘gravity-waves’, ‘photons’, ‘gravitons’, ‘electrons’, ‘protons’, ‘neutrons’ and so on, are so deeply ingrained in our language that they may have to remain more or less as they are for now, if only for purely pragmatical reasons. However, one hopes that these pragmatic considerations alone will not completely obscure the real issue here. This is not the matter of determining what may be à la mode in sophistic science-circles as the ‘most elegant’ theories; it is the very opposite, which is to find the plainest, most conceptually economical and logical – that is, the truest – scientific and philosophical interpretations of natural phenomena described by the simplest possible uses of ordinary language. Logical and literal truth have a notorious habit of asserting themselves, sooner or later, despite all the pragmatic political and doctrinal devices ranged against them. It is the reason why those truths discovered by Socrates, Copernicus, Bruno, Galileo, et al. were eventually substantiated, despite these innovators being persecuted and even executed for threatening the status quo with their uncomfortable truths. The only difference, nowadays, is that the religious dogmatism of the medieval Church has been taken over by contemporary Physics and Cosmology with their belief-system based on deistic ideas such as that of the ‘God Particle’ (Higgs Boson), the ‘Big Bang’, the ‘Moment of Creation’ and so on. This ‘Big Science’ has now, quite evidently, become the ‘New Church’, as witnessed at CERN, with the awesome Large Hadron Collider as its high altar and its priests being the media, conveying its unquestionable beliefs to a befuddled but worshipful laity.

    However, there is now increasing dissatisfaction with the current situation, expressing itself in many ways, including, not least, the increasing pressure towards replacing the current system with some kind of nonconformist ‘New Physics’. We maintain that what is required is not so much a ‘New Physics’ but a whole new and altogether fresh way of thinking about physical phenomena and reality – a whole resurgent Natural Philosophy, in effect.

    To what authority can the reader turn for a judgement on this nowadays, when our scientific authorities are in such disarray? What else but the authority of the reader himself – provided, of course, that he has sufficient confidence in his own free-range, rational and unclouded judgement? The truth must be there to be discovered and finding it must be the ultimate goal of commonsense science.

    In any event, it seems that the sleeping giant of commonsense is now stirring awake, looking around and gradually taking stock of the situation. What is certain is that in these early years of the new millennium, we are witnessing the impending demise of our grandiose, 20$^{th}$ century Physics and Cosmology, which future generations will surely regard as every bit as fanciful and bizarre as we now regard the ‘elephants-and-turtles’ cosmology of the ancient Hindus depicted on the cover of this book.

    In Chapter 13, we explore some of the more humanitarian and sociological implications of the new Natural Philosophy of Normal Realism. Meanwhile, in the next chapter, in keeping with the overall critical theme of this book, we continue with applying our method of Logical (Linguistic) Analysis to expose some of the major fallacies underlying our standard-model interpretations of physical phenomena that, over the last two centuries of physical theory, have led us into the current conceptual cul-de-sac in our efforts to gain a logically consistent and coherent understanding of nature.

    CHAPTER 12

    The ‘Expanding Universe’ and Other Fallacies

    As discussed in Chapter 3, in 1676 Olaus Römer discovered, for the first time in our history, that there exists a constant relationship, now labelled ‘$c$’, between space-measure and time-measure such that, as we now know, every 300,000,000 conventional metres of unmediated observational distance is equivalent to one conventional second of time. It was then discovered, by A. A. Michelson and E. W. Morley, in 1887 that, as Bondi and Pope concurred, this constant $c$ does not behave anything like a velocity, that it is no more nor less than just that: a constant for interconverting measures in metres and measures in seconds, in the same way that, now, $c^2$ is a constant for interconverting mass-measures in kilograms and energy-measures in joules. There is nothing mysterious or particularly significant or ‘magic’ about this constant, $c$. Its existence is simply due to pure accident in our arbitrary choices of units. For instance, by a different convention, that same constant is 186,000 miles to the second. If it had somehow happened that what we now call 186,000 miles – or, in metric units, 300,000 kilometres – had been chosen as one standard unit of distance, then $c$ would be equal to one and need never have appeared in any of our physics formulae and our customary three-dimensional ‘space’ would automatically have become a four-dimensional space-time, with all distances in any unmediated observational direction being measured in the same units of, for example, seconds. In that case, our usual talk about ‘light-velocity’ in physics would scarcely have arisen. (See also Chapters 6 and 7.) For non-mathematicians, the time-dilation formula of Special Relativity would, demonstrably, have been virtually child’s play [114].

    However, because this constant $c$ is a ratio of metres to seconds and because all velocities of motion are also ratios of metres to seconds, it has been assumed, throughout the subsequent history of physics, falsely, as it happens (see Chapter 6), that $c$ is a velocity, namely, the ‘velocity of light’.

    12.1. The Hubble Redshift

    Another example, in physics, of this same fallacy is to assume that because all receding light-sources exhibit a redshift, all light-sources that are redshifted are receding. On the basis of that fallacious assumption rests the whole dogmatic theory of the ‘expanding universe’ and its assumed ‘Big Bang’ beginnings.

    Edwin Hubble and Vesto Slipher were the first to report this discovery of what has been called the Cosmological Redshift – or, more familiarly perhaps, the Hubble Redshift. From this it has been assumed that the distant galaxies are receding from us and from one another at a speed of between 50 and 90 kilometres per second for every megaparsec of distance.

    Now this ‘expanding universe’ interpretation of the Hubble Redshift is by no means logically obligatory. Besides, neither Hubble nor anyone else has observed that expansion. What is observed is the redshift. To interpret this phenomenon as an ‘expansion of the universe’ is pure speculation. Indeed, so bizarre are the consequences of the ‘expansion’ interpretation, not least in the form of the ‘Big Bang’, that it might – or, as some would say, should – have been taken as a reductio ad absurdum of this interpretation. This implies that there must be at least one other, perfectly viable and sensible alternative explanation of the Hubble Redshift. For instance, one such alternative explanation of the Redshift is that it is a relativistic time dilational effect of increasing omnidirectional motion with distance, which has no bizarre ‘expanding universe’ and associated ‘Big Bang’ implications. This is the alternative that POAMS provides. As in the standard ‘expansion’ interpretation it also implies increasing velocities with distance but, significantly, in random not radial directions. For instance, it is standard textbook mechanics that in an angular momentum system, the nearer the orbiting body gets to the central mass of the system, the faster it goes. This can be seen with the planets and comets of our solar system, which travel faster, the nearer they get to their barycentre, near the centre of the Sun. The same occurs when the NASA space-probes approach massive planets like Saturn and Jupiter, in which cases, they travel faster than when they were launched from Earth. So where, in our universe as a whole, is there such a central mass if, indeed, it exists? It certainly isn’t our Sun or even our Milky Way. Nor can it be any other galaxy or finite group of galaxies, so it must be somewhere far off from any of these. Indeed, there is no logical contradiction in assuming that this ‘barycentre’ may be distributed all over, so that the speeds of the galaxies increase in proportion to the distance relative to the observer, more or less symmetrically on account of the accumulated mass of objects along the observational line of sight. This is consistent with the basic concept of relativity according to which all masses and other physical measures are relative to and centred upon the observer, as opposed to absolute and universal, as in the ‘Big Bang’ theory.

    It is the Normal Realist view then, as opposed to a current cosmological view of a closed universe, that the universe as such is in a constant, steady state of indefinite extent. That is to say, from any observational point of view, there are expanding spheres of volume encompassing all the stars, galaxies and other bits of matter within a range up to the limit at which the (random) velocities of the galaxies become asymptotic to $c$. In this case their masses become theoretically infinite creating, in effect, an observational ‘horizon’ of more and more tightly-packed objects. This is the same limit that is set by the recessional hypothesis. The quantitative results, therefore, of this alternative random-directional assumption prove to be indistinguishable from those of the recessional interpretation [115]. For instance, there is absolutely no way to distinguish, by pure observation, a redshift due to time dilation from one due to recession. In both cases, the spectral lines for all frequencies are shifted altogether en bloc – that is to say, without the dispersion that is entailed by just about every other theory – such as, for example, the ‘tired light’ theory or the theory of ‘gravitational bending’. This leaves only the criterion of plausibility by which to judge the two assumptions as to which is the more credible. Is it to assume simply that the velocities of galaxies increase randomly but exponentially with distance or is it to assume that the whole universe started out from the explosion of something less than the size of a pinhead, some fourteen billion years ago? Surely, from any commonsense point of view, this latter explanation is by far the least plausible.

    In any case, the fact remains, throughout, that referring to the ‘Big Bang’ theory as ‘The Big Bang’ – by use of the definite article ‘the’ – is a fallacy. However, this fashion of referring to ‘The Big Bang’ has become practically standard in contemporary textbooks of physics and astronomy and, particularly, in the popular media. Seldom is it truthfully called ‘the Big Bang theory or hypothesis’. But since there is at least one demonstrable alternative to that theory, to speak in terms of ‘The Big Bang’ signifies a dogma. With its textbooks virtually excluding any mention of a possible alternative, this ‘Big Bang’ assumption might justifiably be described as ‘the dogma of our age’.

    12.2. The alleged ‘expanding universe’

    Regarding what was said in the earlier chapters about language, it is plain that our interpretations of what there is, or exists, in nature have to make literal sense, otherwise all language does is to mislead us, creating chimeras, monsters of pure imagination. Take, for instance, the word ‘universe’. This properly signifies the logical class of ‘everything there is’, all space, all time, all light, all dark, all objects, all dimensions, properties, qualities, etc., with no existential residue whatsoever. As the Oxford English Dictionary adds, it signifies ‘All the objects that are the subjects of consideration’. What, then, are we to make of the notion of an ‘expanding universe’? What is it supposed to be ‘expanding’ into since, by definition, there is nothing outside it, not even empty space? At what speed can it be ‘expanding’, since all reference points to which that ‘speed’ may be judged are inside the very thing that is supposed to be expanding? With reference to what can we say that it is ‘tiny’ at one stage and ‘large’ at another? The description is meaningless. Moreover, with all the various alleged fragments flying about with different speeds relatively to one another, by what clock is the rate of expansion to be timed? By what clock is its age at any stage to be judged? This supposed ‘age’ of thirteen and three-quarter billion years is, again, pure nonsense.

    How, then, can we possibly say that ‘the universe is expanding’? From whichever way we look at that statement it makes no sense. Yet physicists and cosmologists barefacedly talk about this ‘expansion’, ‘inflation’ and so on, as if these were statements of incontrovertible fact. On analysis, however, they are forms of statements like ‘Why is a mouse when it spins?’ or Lewis Carroll’s ‘The slithy toves did gyre and gimble in the wabe’ which, of course, were written for purposes of entertainment. Like so many other spin-offs of the ‘expanding universe’ hypothesis, it sounds exciting and is no doubt entertaining, yet is completely devoid of true meaning. The same goes, as we shall now see, for the allied notion of the ‘Big Bang’. Let us take another look at that strange conception and study some of its ramifications.

    12.3. The alleged ‘Big Bang’

    Since the idea of the ‘expanding universe’ is such plain nonsense, what on earth are we to make of the idea that ‘at the beginning of time’ the universe was one size, which was extremely tiny, and that as time went on it grew larger and larger until it became the immensity it is now? Again, with reference to what could it have been ‘tiny’ at one time and ‘large’ at another? These questions are as fair and forthright as any innocent child might ask, often to the embarrassment of pretentious adults. Like those childish questions, these equally honest questions connected with the dogmas of the ‘Big Bang’ and the ‘expanding universe’, cannot be answerable by their priestly advocates without twisting ordinary language into knots.

    For example, what can it possibly mean that, as it is claimed, some fourteen billion years ago everything that there now is, including space and time, was locked up within some infinitesimally small ‘primeval atom’ waiting, in a state of non-being, for it to explode into being? And how is that time-scale to be measured, since all those hypothetical fragments of the explosion would be travelling at different speeds hence different durations relative to one another? Over that alleged interval from then until now the accumulated relative differences between them due to time dilation would be something of the same order of magnitude as ‘the age of the universe’ itself. In any case, what meaning can possibly be ascribed to the phrase ‘age of the universe’ when there is no universal proper time or relative-time standard, by which that ‘age’ can be measured?

    We should be thankful, then, that to interpret the Hubble Redshift as a recessional motion on the part of the galaxies is by no means logically obligatory; that, as already mentioned, there is at least one other, much simpler and far more plausible interpretation of the Redshift than that of an expansion of the whole universe.

    12.4. The alleged ‘dark matter’

    A long-standing problem in modern physics is what seems to be the existence of an entirely hidden and inscrutable amount of matter, quaintly called ‘dark matter’. This chimera has been created in answer to the problem of what is known as the ‘missing mass anomaly’. The anomaly is that calculations of the amount of matter, or mass, that there should be in the universe of stars, galaxies and other known forms of material is much smaller than there actually appears to be $^{[116]}$. In other words, something seems to be ‘missing’, something which is mass but with none of the ordinary properties of mass, or matter, such as visibility, detectability and ponderability. The puzzle is that the amount of this ‘dark matter’ required to balance the equations is so large that if it existed, then with all that amount of mass between us and distant objects we should not be able to see into space to anything like the extent we do. In fact, however, the awesome clarity of our modern telescopic photographs of galaxies upon galaxies of stars as far as our very best instruments can reach, betokens that between us and those objects there is either nothing at all, or else that if there is anything, then it is nothing like matter as we normally understand it. For example, it would need to be profoundly invisible and absolutely transparent, being of such an altogether unknown composition as not to emit, reflect or absorb light, nor to have any tangible property of matter as we know it other than its measure in kilograms.

    But how can some portion of the same substance (in this case, mass) be observable and the rest not, and by what means can it be ‘hidden’? How can it be both ‘there’ and ‘missing’? How can it be ‘dark’ in the darkness of space itself? The assumption, like all the rest, is plainly nonsensical, yet, just like the ‘God Particle’ and ‘gravity waves’, much heavily funded research is dedicated to detecting this alleged non-substance. However, for all that exhaustive and expensive attention over the decades there has been – hardly surprisingly – not even the tiniest bit of direct evidence as to the existence of this speculative quasi-substance.

    There is, however, another approach to this problem according to which there is no such ‘missing mass’, hence no metaphysical ‘dark matter’ or ‘dark’ anything. This is simply to declare that it is our calculations of the amount of matter which there ought to be are wrong. Let’s start with the truism that there can be no anomalies in nature as such but only in our theories about nature. Where, then, among our accepted theories may we hope to find this anomaly? The answer is quite obvious. It lies in the 18$^{th}$ century Newtonian theory of ‘gravitation’ on which the calculations of that ‘missing mass’ are based. In the so-called ‘Pioneer anomaly’ (see Appendix 6) Newtonian ‘gravitation’ fails to take account of the spin angular momentum of space-probes. By the same token, Newtonian ‘gravitation’ takes no account of the prodigious amounts of spin angular momentum that bodies such as the spiral galaxies and just about all other astronomical bodies possess. However, conservation of angular momentum rules that the total angular momentum of an orbiting body has to include that of both orbital and spin, and therein lies the anomaly. The ‘gravitational’ calculations on which both the ‘missing mass’ and ‘Pioneer’ anomalies are based typically ignore the amount of spin angular momentum that bodies have on the astronomical scale. Since the total angular momentum of a freely moving body includes that of orbital plus spin, then it is plain that introducing spin angular momentum affects the orbital angular momentum, and the less there is of orbital angular momentum, the nearer the body orbits the central body. In the case of

    NASA’s spinning space-probes in solar orbit this means that the probe orbits nearer to the Sun than if it were not spinning, which is precisely the ‘anomaly’ that the scientists at NASA’s Jet Propulsion Laboratory (JPL) have discovered. In the astronomical case, the reason for the so-called ‘missing mass’ is the same as for the ‘Pioneer anomaly’, namely, that when the Newtonian formula for orbital motion is corrected to include spin, the so-called ‘gravitational constant’, $G$ becomes a variable, as may be seen in the following section.

    12.5. The alleged constancy of the ‘gravitational constant’ $G$

    As can be seen in Appendix 5 of this book, the so-called ‘gravitational constant’, $G$, is constant for orbiting bodies that are theoretically without spin. However, as soon as that spin angular momentum is included, $G$ ceases to be a constant and becomes a variable. This POAMS discovery of varying $G$ is supported not only by the evidence of the seemingly anomalous behaviour of NASA’s space-probes but also by Mordecai Milgrom’s MOND theory, which is that $G$ varies for different parts of the universe [117]. The essential difference, however, between Milgrom’s MOND theory and that of the Normal Realist approach is that while the MOND theory is purely speculative and ad hoc, assuming a different value of $G$ for different parts of the universe, ignoring spin, in POAMS these changes in $G$ are endemic in the very definition of angular momentum, hence are logically based and predictive of the effect on spinning bodies universally.

    Why, then, over the long years, has this variability of $G$ not been detected? It is because the context in which $G$ was measured was that of astronomy, in which the proportion of the spin to orbital angular momentum of bodies such as the planets and satellites is altogether miniscule. It is only on the level of microphysics that this proportion of spin to orbit becomes really significant. For example, as is demonstrated in Appendix 5, so large is the proportion of spin to orbit of the so-called electron that the force required to remove that ‘particle’ from its orbit – to ‘ionise’ it, in conventional terms – is so strong that it seems to be a force altogether different in nature from that of ‘gravitation’, hence the invention of a whole new, artificial and specialised category of language to describe ‘electrical’ phenomena. For this so-called ‘Coulomb force’ required to separate the spinning electron from its nuclear proton, the value of $G$ is many orders of magnitude greater than for spinless bodies [118], otherwise, those two forces, the ‘gravitational’ force and the ‘Coulomb force’ are of the same kind. That is, they are both, basically, the forces which have to be exerted in order to prevent particles from following their natural force-free orbits.

    12.5.1. ‘Gravity’ and ‘antigravity’

    The magnitude of the electron’s spin angular momentum is huge in comparison to that of its orbital angular momentum. In POAMS, the electron’s enormous proportion of spin to its orbital angular momentum is held responsible for the comparative tightness of its ‘orbit’ within the atom. Without that spin, the electron would orbit like any other spinless body, but at a radial distance determined by the standard value of $G$ in the orbital equation which is truly immense (see Appendix 5).

    In the case of an Earth satellite, the directional effect of the spin is the same in kind as that of the electron, described in Appendices 4 and 5. In particular, if a satellite spins in the same direction as its orbital motion, then it will orbit closer to the Earth than if it were not spinning. If it spins in the opposite direction to its orbital motion, then it will orbit further out than if it were not spinning. A gyroscope resting on the Earth’s surface at the equator should therefore weigh somewhat more when spinning in the same direction as that of the Earth, and less when spinning in the opposite direction. However, for an ordinary-sized gyroscope to spin fast enough to register a significant change in weight, it would have to spin way beyond its disintegration speed, which is why we do not normally see spinning bodies lose or gain weight to any significant extent. In some quarters of physics, experiments have been conducted, for example, by the team of Hayasaka and Takeuchi of Tokai University, Japan, aimed at measuring the amount of levitation or loss of weight of a spinning body. However, at their maximum achievable spin speed of 216 revolutions per second for a gyroscope weighing 176 grams, the effect POAMS predicts is far too small to be properly measured, and their claims to have discovered and measured this effect – a loss of eleven thousandths of a gram – are dubious. For instance, no systematic account was taken by these researchers of the geographical location of the experiment or the orientation of the spin vector of the spinning body with respect to that of the Earth. Moreover, attempts by other researchers to replicate these experiments – as, for instance by the Standards Institute in Paris – have failed to obtain anything like the same results. In general, as we have said, the speeds required to make a reliable measurable difference to weight involve the risk of dangerous disintegration. Reports of spin-speeds achieved at around 30,000 rpm for compact discs (CDs) have included descriptions of these discs exploding. At a Russian laboratory, comparable spin-speeds were reached with very thick glass discs, where it was reported that the discs disintegrated so violently that for more than a week afterwards the operators were picking out tiny shards of glass from their skin and hair. (Happily, these fragments were too small to do any real damage.) It is to be hoped, however, that this problem may sometime be solved, creating a means of decisively testing the POAMS prediction of the ‘levitation’ and ‘depression’ of spinning bodies. As one commentator wryly remarked ‘perhaps we should ask the UFO pilots how they do it’.

    12.6. The alleged discovery of ‘antimatter’

    In 2010, there were reports in the media (the BBC and The Daily Telegraph for example) of claims by physicists to have discovered – or, rather, created – antimatter, that is, stable collections of antiparticles. By an ‘antiparticle’ is meant a fundamental particle which has the same characteristics as ordinary matter but possessed of opposite charge. For instance, the antiparticle of the negatively charged ‘electron’ is a ‘positron’. Every fundamental particle is supposed to have its antiparticle by what is thought to be the principle of ‘symmetry’ in nature. In separating-off these small collections of these antiparticles and keeping them apart from other particles for sufficiently long periods, the physicists claim to have established the existence of antimatter, the alleged ‘mirror-image’ of ordinary matter.

    In the standard model of particle physics, it is said that when a pair of particles of opposite charge are brought together, they annihilate each other, with the creation of great outbursts of energy in the form of gamma radiation. By that same token, if there had happened to be as much antimatter in the universe as ordinary matter, then the two forms of matter would long since have destroyed each other, with an altogether prodigious release of energy. So, the ‘Big Bang’ theorists argue, if there had been as many antiparticles as particles produced at that moment of creation, then there should simply have been no universe. For modern physics and cosmology this raises an obvious question: ‘Why, then is there a universe at all?’ Why is there now, as it seems, just the one kind of matter and practically none of the other? This is known as the problem of matter-antimatter asymmetry, which is deemed to be a major problem in particle physics nowadays.

    But is this a serious problem, a problem worth spending more and more huge amounts of time and money on? Or is it, perhaps, a Scheinprobleme, like so many others in physics nowadays? Is it is simply a case of, as Berkeley put it: ‘We kick up a dust and complain we cannot see.’ From the standpoint of Normal Realism, it certainly seems so. For instance, why do these opposite charges annihilate each other? By virtue of what mechanism is this supposed to happen? What is it about ‘charge’ that enables it to do this? Besides, what is ‘charge’, anyway? In traditional physics this remains unexplained. Not so in Normal Realism, however, where the explanation is perfectly plain.

    In Normal Realism, as we have seen (see Chapter 9) there is no such thing as ‘charge’, the esoteric ‘something-we-know-not-what’ that is traditionally ascribed to ‘elementary particles’ such as ‘electrons’ and ‘protons’. Instead, that ‘charge’, in static-energy units of coulombs, is cashed out entirely in kinetic energy units of joules, as the spin kinetic energy attributed by Uhlenbeck and Goudsmit to those micro-physical particles. Conceived in this way, the situation is quite commonplace. For instance, it is well known, from ordinary everyday experience, that when a pair of same-sized wheels spinning in opposite directions come firmly into contact, friction between them brings them to a halt, resulting in the production of heat. This presents no mystery. A prime example, well known and understood by any competent motorist, is that of the clutch used in motor vehicles to connect and disconnect the engine to and from the transmission, where the clutch-plate and the flywheel are put into contact to kill the motion between them.

    Nor is this, by any means, the only example of that perfectly natural phenomenon. In cricket, tennis, engineering, astronomy and commonsense in general it is well understood that in collections of differently spinning and non-spinning objects, contacts between those spinning objects themselves and other surfaces sends them off on all kinds of different tracks, spirals, whorls, the screaming ricochets of bullets etc., where spins aid and annihilate each other in well understood ways. Since this presents no mystery in ordinary experience, then why should it be a mystery in particle physics where, for instance, the bubble tracks in liquid hydrogen typically have these features, as shown in Figure 4 below?

    How, then, does Normal Realism address this mystery of the alleged matter-antimatter asymmetry in the universe? Well, in the first place, if there never was any ‘Big Bang’, then there could be no question of which spin rotation ‘came first’. Whichever rotation we find to be prevalent right now has to be the one that was

    Typical view of particle tracks in the bubble chamber at CERN

    always there, any occurrence of the opposite rotation being purely accidental and transient. That is to say, regardless of how it might be said to have come about, it could not have survived for more than a moment. Nothing changes in this shift in conception from ‘charge’ to spin. The same energy is produced from the mutual annihilation of the opposite spins as, allegedly, from the opposite ‘charges’. The only difference is that all this is much more intuitively understandable in terms of the quantised spins of particles than in terms of the purely abstract and inscrutable ‘charges’. Logically, then, this vexatious ‘problem’ of the matter-antimatter asymmetry turns out to be unreal, being uniquely a product of the ‘Big Bang’ theory and which does not arise in any other context. Like the theoretical ‘Black Holes’, ‘dark matter’ and so on, this theoretical ‘antimatter’ can therefore safely be consigned to the category of media-hype and Science Fiction. However, insofar as it may be true that particles of opposite spin have been discovered, and that throwing these particles together may provide a new source of energy, that is surely of positive significance, both practically and in advancing our understanding of nature. Experimental research into this phenomenon is therefore, no doubt, worthy of serious attention and funding but not, we believe, in its association with the abortive ‘Big Bang’ dogma.

    12.7. Causality at the quantum level

    In all this talk of the intrinsic spin of elementary particles, such as ‘electrons’ and so on, we must not lose sight of the fact that these ‘fundamental particles’ (howsoever we may prefer to think of them) have no powers in themselves to do anything, any more than the centre of a whirlpool, a cyclone or galaxy has the power to act autonomously, in itself, apart from the surrounding medium. Any notion, then, of the World as we know it, with life and everything being the evolutionary result of ‘fortuitous combinations’ among these imagined randomly distributed and mechanically moving ‘particles’, has to be a non-starter, for what can be possibly be ‘fortuitous’ or otherwise in a world of sheer mindless randomness? In the customary (Democritan) way of thinking, causality is that of one individual thing autonomously striking, pushing or pulling another either in direct contact or by means of some intervening invisible medium. This presupposes that the motions of physical particles are governed by nothing but the classically conceived laws of mechanics applied to chance encounters among bits of matter sprinkled around at random. In this classical conception, the positions and motions of those particles are supposed to be absolutely determinate in accordance with these mechanical laws. This leads to the assumption, commonly attributed to Laplace [119], that an absolute ‘God’s-eye-view’ knowledge of the positions and motions of all particles in the universe at any instant implies absolute determinacy, according to which the end-state of all the various particle interactions is mechanically assured from the beginning of everything – whatever we might suppose that ‘beginning’ to be. As the poet and mathematician, Omar Khayyam expressed it: ‘Yea, the first morning of Creation wrote what the last day of reckoning shall read’ [120].

    However, on the quantum level, causality is nothing like that. For instance, all fundamental particle interactions on that level are indeterminate or random in accordance with Werner Heisenberg’s Indeterminacy Principle – or Uncertainty Principle as it is sometimes called [121]. This states that no interaction between particles can, in one and the same instance, manifest both their positions in that event and their directions thenceforward. This means that future interactions with other particles are calculable, at best, no more than probabilistically. However, this by no means implies that these interactions are entirely capricious because, paradoxical though it might seem, probabilities are strictly law-governed. The two laws which govern the probabilities of particle interactions are, (i), the Energy Conservation Law, that no particle in a closed system may move in any way which would disconserve the overall energy of the system as a whole; and, (ii), the Second Law of Thermodynamics, according to which no exchange of energy between particles may take place in any way which does not increase the randomness of future such interactions. It is for this reason that the Second Law is often referred to as the Law of Entropy, or the Law of Randomness, according to which all order, everywhere, decays inevitably towards disorder. (See Chapter 5, section 5.3.1.)

    According to these two laws, in none of their interactions with one another can any particles act autonomously, far less in prospect of any future ‘advantage’ to be gained by this or that particular knock, pull or push. This demands that within traditional physical rules, the natural phenomenon of life, with its characteristically prospective, goal-seeking and other anticipatory activities, is either an illusion or else a complete miracle – to assume which, however tacitly, is scarcely scientific.

    Consequently, traditional materialistic physics fails to cope with a whole range of natural phenomena connected with just about everything that has to do with life in all its huge variety of forms. But, of course, this is scarcely surprising, since the existence of this whole sub-category of natural phenomena was arbitrarily dismissed by Democritus in the first place. Logically, then, ‘miracles’ aside, the facts signify that the classical laws of mechanics, although mitigated to some extent by the concept of randomness and probability, are altogether inadequate for explaining natural phenomena, not least because they must arbitrarily exclude a whole range of these plainly exhibited phenomena. Surely nothing signifies more than this that something is seriously wrong with the science which, even today, remains based on the Democritan, materialist approach to physics.

    What is missing from this kind of physics, so far as Normal Realism is concerned, is any recognition of the overall interconnectedness and interdependence of things which is manifest throughout nature. In Normal Realism, and especially in POAMS, this interconnectedness is supplied by an angular momentum nexus consisting, on the fundamental analytical level, of intransitive proper time-instantaneous actions-at-a-distance in quantum ‘blips’.
