As already discussed, in Normal Realism, all the traditional in vacuo ‘fields’ and ‘field-forces’ (Democritan ‘pulls’ and ‘pushes’) are replaced by this single overall nexus of angular momentum. This proves sufficient in itself to explain the correlations of the motions and interrelations between all physical particles ordinarily attributed to ‘gravitation’, the speed of which (‘gravitation’) some astronomers, such as the late Van Flandern judge to be ‘superluminal’ (i.e., many times, if not infinitely, greater than $c$)$^{[122]}$. The proper time instantaneity of angular momentum makes it a conserved quantity, within which all particles are automatically and immediately paired and balanced. What the law of conservation of angular momentum forbids, as we have seen, is any ‘single-ended interaction’, that is to say, any interaction between particles which is not immediately and instantly consummated in accordance with Newton’s Third Law of equal and reciprocal action/reaction. This reciprocity of angular momentum balance obviously has to be instantaneous regardless of any intervening distance.

    Let us recall, here, in relation to the alleged ‘photon’, that this immediate consummation of action is unthinkable in the mind-set of traditional physicists. In the ‘flipside’ holistic paradigm, however, a quantum of energy cannot be released from a source without an assured billet somewhere for its absorption since, in the proper time of the interaction, the source-event and the sink-event are not two events but one-and-the-same event. As already discussed, this is why ‘particles’ on the quantum level are regarded in some experiments as behaving in ways that, from the customary point of view, suggest some sort of ‘telepathic’ foreknowledge of their destinations [46].

    Quantum interaction is therefore essentially law-governed, not by the classical mechanical and deterministic, Democritan, laws but by holistic laws like that of statistical probability – closely related, in modern science, to the rules of information theory [43]. As we have seen, the Law of the Conservation of Energy forbids that any particle may act capriciously in its own right, outside that law. Moreover, the Second Law of Thermodynamics, or Law of Entropy, ensures that no particle interactions may take place other than within the jurisdiction of that law. This makes profound sense. If the particle events were not random, then there would be no such thing as Charles’ Law ensuring a regular distribution of pressure on the walls of a container such as a steam boiler or cylinder [123]. If it were not for these statistical laws governing random events, then the designers of engines would not know whether the highly pressurised molecules of steam or fuel in a cylinder would push the piston out or suck it in – or perhaps blow out some part of its walls. Without these statistical laws ensuring a state of randomness among mindless particles, there could be no certainty as to whether an engine would run forward or backward, or whether a kettle of water set on a gas-burner would boil or freeze. In that case, not only would engineering as we know it be impossible but so, also, would every physical process that leads to and sustains life itself. For life to be possible, there has to be a strictly defined sequence of causality.

    Now the point to be stressed, here, is that all these laws, of entropy, energy conservation, the conservation of angular momentum and so on are, essentially, holistic laws. They all state, in effect, that the whole determines the parts and not the parts the whole. If the ultimate parts determined the whole there would be no assurance of anything. Paradoxical though it might seem, therefore, without the assurance of basic randomness (entropy) there would be complete chaos – not even assured randomness – with everything interacting autonomously and capriciously, with no predictable outcome whatsoever, a situation which is completely unthinkable.

    Typical of the scientific legacy of Democritus is the view of causality as directed always from earlier to later. Time’s ‘arrow’, it is said, points inexorably forwards from the present to the future, with nothing in the future capable of reversing that arrow at any stage. This view partially conflicts with the fact, exemplified throughout the organic kingdom, that in nature there are actions of things, such as animals and plants, which are prospective, that is to say, anticipatory of and directive towards future states. Nor is this phenomenon of goal-directing or cybernetic (literally steering) behaviour confined to living organisms. As we have seen, the statistical laws which govern purely inorganic processes are also directed towards the fulfilment of future states, in a way which it is anathema even to contemplate in classical Democritan mechanistic terms.

    Take the example of a railway steam engine. As already discussed, the steam molecules in the boiler and the cylinders have to act in obedience to statistical laws, such as those of energy conservation and entropy. Much as it may appear that those molecules move at random – which, indeed, they do – none of those molecules can move in any way other than towards those future states, or end-states which are assured by those statistical laws. This means, in fact, that predictable future states do actually influence present states, which confounds the standard expectations of Democritan physics. In other words, mechanistic physics collapses, not only in the face of the phenomenon of future-directed, or cybernetic, activity as manifest in living matter but also in the face of the statistical Law of Entropy as a determiner of future end-states, such as, for instance, a decrease in order among mindless, or undirected, moving particles.

    However, not all end-states have to conform entirely to the Law of Entropy. A pebble has no capacity other than to warm in sunshine and then cool down afterwards, precisely and predictably in accordance with this law. On the other hand, the same sunshine falling on a leaf generates photosynthesis, which turns solar energy ‘uphill’ from inorganic into organic material. This process is called negentropic, because it acts counter to the Law of Entropy as, in essence, do all the living processes that abound in nature. However, some scientists say that the concept of negentropy is spurious, that the whole process of life and so on is no more than a temporary ‘backflow’, or ‘eddy’, in the universal flow towards disorder, hence is powered by the Law of Entropy in the same way as everything else. According to this view, the system involving just the Sun and the organic end-product – the tree, the fruit and the animals feeding off it, including human society – is a process every stage of which is accounted for by the Law of Entropy. It is thus supposed that this law, in itself, makes no allowance for capricious breaches in the universal flow towards disorder at any stage in the process, even for a moment. To imagine that entropy can explain those anomalies, such as life and intentionality in general, is therefore no more than a leap of faith on the part of those who feel it obligatory to defend at all costs the standard Democritan concept of mechanical causality. This defence is often maintained even at the expense of objective reason by unstatedly postulating, as we have said, another ‘miracle’ in the form of a ‘fortuitous accident’ in a primordial setting where ‘fortuity’ can have no possible meaning.

    For the sake of preserving the classical conventions, these conservators of tradition have to remain in denial of certain factual phenomena, no matter how commonly observed. But that, surely, is not the way of true empirical science. It is certainly not the way of Normal Realism, in which the actual phenomenon of goal-directed activity in general is recognised for what it so plainly is, an integral and undeniable part of nature in the round, as so profusely manifest on just about every square metre on this planet.

    12.8. The fallacy of universal entropy

    Something more needs to be said at this stage about causality in relation to evolution. The laws of randomness governing sequences of events on the ultimate quantum level provide, for modern physics, what Democritus secured for classical physics: that is, the release of physical action from the gridlock of the Eleatic stasis. Although this Democritan solution succeeded in releasing motion and change from stagnant immobility it did so only at the cost of making a complete mystery of a whole subset of natural phenomena which are associated with those processes classified as ‘organic’ – that is to say, life-processes generally. This, of course, is because the ‘atoms’ of Democritus were conceived as purely mechanical and aimless in their actions and interactions; that is, entirely random in the sense of not being committed to any future condition or end-state. In modern physics, by contrast, these random events are end-directed, in the manner previously discussed. This is because randomness is essentially governed by the Law of Entropy, which ensures the retroactive causal effects of end-states on initial states (which Aristotle called entelechies, or retrograde causalities $^{[124]}$). That is to say, the Law of Entropy directs that all purely random physical processes are future-directed towards an end-state of maximum probability.

    However, probability theorists know that in modern physics there is not just one single probabilistic end-state, that of maximum probability (see Chapter 5, section 5.3.1.) In fact, probabilities form a sliding scale of measures from nought to one, nought being virtual impossibility and one being virtual certainty. All natural phenomena, from the clattering of pebbles to the activities of animals and human beings, fit somewhere onto that sliding scale, with other things such as virus particles, the DNA molecule, the microbes around deep-sea hydrothermal vents and so on, all placed somewhere in between the two extremities (i.e., between animal and mineral, or organic and inorganic) [125]. Moreover, in modern physics, the inverse of probability – that is, improbability – defines the measure of information. In this way, probability-statistics provides entelechies, not of just the one sort, i.e., that of maximum probability, or entropy; it provides entelechies in the whole range of probabilities from nought to one, with no particular measure naturally singled out. In this way, Normal Realism deals with the whole scale of probabilities (entelechies) as they come, without arbitrarily excluding those known as ‘living’ in the way that classical ‘atomism’ does.

    Now information, intelligence and life are all in the same logical category. A defining attribute of that whole category is that of negentropy, or creation of order, as in writing a novel or in living growth and development. The way this works is that nature supplies information, not only of the here-and-now but also of the probabilities or improbabilities of future events. The hound chasing the hare is continually assessing those probabilities from the information supplied to its senses by both its sight of the hare and of the terrain in which the chase takes place. Meantime, the hare, with its natural sideways and rear-view compass of vision is no less continually reading the same sort of information but with the aim of making some move that the dog will not predict, so wrong-footing and perhaps hamstringing the animal, as countrymen (poachers, especially) well know.

    This is the same with all life-forms that are in competition for survival. Some species evolve strategies of defence and offence which favour them in that struggle, and other species cooperate with one another towards that same end. Since there is no way in which Democritan physics can explain this, it is left to separate disciplines, in the residual group of subjects such as biology, zoology, psychology, and suchlike, to fill the gap. Removing that categorical division, as Normal Realism does, allows the same sorts of explanations to be applied to living as to non-living or purely mechanical processes, leaving these hitherto disparate disciplines seamlessly joined. In this way, the phenomena which are the concern of physics and the life sciences respectively all conform to a broadened form of the Law of Entropy which presents, not just the one, single-valued classical trend towards maximum entropy but a whole sliding scale of probabilities from entropy to negentropy. On the one end of that scale is the kettle of water which freezes when placed in an arctic environment. Towards the opposite end of that same scale is the Eskimo who builds an igloo as protection against that same possibility of freezing and makes a fire to boil the kettle and cook his food.

    These improbabilistic or negentropic processes among life-forms are as common among animals as entropic ones are among machines. Far from precipitating everything, therefore, into universal disorder, as classical science supposed in what it dramatically described as ‘the heat death of the universe’, the Law of Entropy, stripped of its unjustifiable bias towards pure randomness, permits that later states can affect earlier ones in various ways, hence that the life sciences have equal status with those dealing with inanimate matter and may pursue their interests together.

    The future, then, is by no means mechanically determined in the way people like Laplace and Boltzmann – and Omar Khayyam – supposed but is calculable, to some considerable extent, in statistical terms of probabilities. The future is therefore neither completely determinate nor completely indeterminate. This means that it is never a complete blank, as it would have to be without these statistical laws of connection or continuity between the future and the present. Indeed, the complete lack of any such connection is quite inconceivable. The future therefore has to exist in some definitive but indeterminate sense, as directive of present events [126]. This is in terms of sets of probabilities and possibilities, which is what makes it calculable as either advantageous or disadvantageous to the organism according to its ability to make such calculations and act accordingly.

    This is sufficient reason in itself to dispense with any need for explanations of life, etc., in terms of some mysterious, tacitly deistic plan drawn up in a heavenly ‘drawing-office’ by a great cosmical ‘draughtsman’ and then consigned to the universal laws of mechanical determinism to put that plan into effect. Quite obviously, any such ‘scientific’ explanation of the existence of life can scarcely be distinguished from that of religion.

    Yet, that is the only explanation of life that classical science can come up with, which makes it an irony that so many people have seen this sort of ‘science’ as ousting religion [127]. More to the point, however, is that this incipiently deistic (as opposed to theistic) science has now been superseded. Rather than putting design and intention at the beginning of the world, modern science puts them into the future rather than the past (as well, of course, into the freely intentional, volitional activities of organisms which are witnessed on a daily basis). This is because an absolutely deterministic, bumpety-bump kind of mechanics, as assumed by the likes of Boltzmann and Laplace, has been replaced by a mechanics in which holistic laws such as conservation of angular momentum and energy, and the Law of Entropy, are ultimately statistical in the manner conceived by Shannon and Weaver [128] and others.

    The same applies, logically, to explanations as to what it is that ‘causes’ later events to affect earlier ones in instances of statistical improbability (entelechies) of the sort that are characteristic of living organisms. Given those statistical laws, explanations of such processes in terms of specific and detailed accounts of mechanical collisions, pulls, pushes and so on among particles of mindless matter, are as logically redundant as they are practically impossible.

    Consider, once again, the phenomenon of a steam train travelling from A to B. What powers the train is the energy of the steam molecules in obedience to the statistical Second Law of Thermodynamics. In that case, the phenomenon is explicable entirely in terms of entropy at the maximum-probability end of the scale. But if that is what powers the train, then what drives the train is something different. This, of course, is the engine driver. This part of the phenomenon can be accounted for in terms of a different measure on that same probabilities scale, but this time more towards the zero end of the scale than towards the one – that is, more towards the informational, or negentropic end than the purely mechanical, or entropic, end. Then there are the designers and builders of the locomotive, the railway engineers, the organisers of the railway timetable, the tickets and general informational infrastructure of the railway, which directs just where and when the train will travel, in the same sort of way that the heat of the boiler directs the purely mechanical motion of the train. There are also the financial investors and directors who provide the funds for the building of the engines and rolling stock, the wayleaves that have to be obtained from landowners for the laying of the track, and so on. All this massive intricacy of interlacing plans and intentions can scarcely be credited to no more than

    ‘fortuitous’ combinations of random events. These cases are examples of entelechies in complex interweavings where future possibilities direct present ones, impossible to explain in traditional terms of mechanical collisions among atoms and molecules under the rule of ‘universal entropy’. At various other points on the scale between the two extremes of order and disorder lie the differences between ‘organic’ and ‘inorganic’, ‘living’ and ‘non-living’, ‘intelligent’ and ‘non-intelligent’, ‘conscious’ and ‘unconscious’ – or ‘mind’ and ‘matter’. On empirical grounds, to include them all under the single heading of ‘physical’ or ‘mechanical’ is, quite obviously, absurd.

    Like the phenomenon of the steam train, other physical processes involve all sorts of different and complex measures of probability on the scale of nought to one. But nowhere on that scale is there anything like that single hard-line division, or break, between living and non-living matter which is presupposed by the mechanistic tradition of physical science. All one detects is a perfectly seamless connection between organic and inorganic, between living and non-living, between beings with intention and beings that are automatons. There simply is no such thing in nature as that imagined dividing-line between the two artificial categories of ‘mind’ and ‘matter’. Removal of that voodoo chalk line by broadening the Second Law of Thermodynamics to include the whole scale of probabilities, leaves a continuum of those phenomena which are customarily marked-off as ‘physical’, ‘geological’, ‘biological’, ‘anthropological’, ‘sociological’, ‘psychological’ and so forth.

    The universe conceived in accordance with Normal Realism is therefore very different from that of the Democritan/Cartesian conception which persists as the basis of standard mechanistic physics and of our Western culture that has evolved around it. Typical of this classical-science based, Western way of thinking is that of the universe as a Great Machine, whose functioning is determined by the laws of (basically Democritan) physics. In that imaginary universe there is no place for anything of the sort that is essential to life, far less to humanity as such – that is to say, intention, responsibility, morality, sentiment, love, art, religion, honour, humour, etc. Such social and personal considerations were either ignored by Democritan physics or else dismissed as the abstract province of ‘mind’. In Democritan science ‘mind’ has no rightful place in the materialistic scheme of things other than as an unquiet ‘Ghost in the Machine’, as the philosopher Gilbert Ryle named it [129]. By complete contrast, in Normal Realism, with its basis in information and communication, far from being a ‘Ghost in the Machine’, ‘mind’, in the sense of informational recognition and communication, is the most basic, most undeniable fact of nature. Indeed, without mind there would be neither science to start with, nor – quite obviously – any possible talk of nature as we know it. To deny the existence of mind in the natural scheme of things is therefore analogous to a tree-surgeon chopping-off the branch on which he sits.

    In our modern science, the classical rule of determinacy has been replaced, as we have seen, by the Second Law of Thermodynamics. The logical consequence of this, if followed through consistently, is for the universe to succumb, eventually, to a state of maximum entropy. But in that case, how could that universe have ever started in the first place, if not by the miraculous intervention by some imagined deity or demon which (or who) introduced into an unspeakable nothing a build-up of maximum order and left it to drain away towards a state of complete disorder, commonly called the ‘heat death of the universe’? In this scenario, any deity has to be, at best, a universal ‘Machine Designer’ who, having wound-up the universe like a great clock and then set it to run, leaves it all, from then on, to run down on its own towards some mechanically predetermined end. To assume that life somehow emerges through pure chance is therefore to believe in miracles, hence to eject theism ostentatiously through the front door and then sneak it in, surreptitiously, through the back door as deism.

    However, so far as growth and decay are concerned, to assume that this Law of Entropy is universal is just another confusion of language. This is not least because in relativity there is no ‘universe’ of that sort that can be defined, nor any overall instant in which any such ‘beginning’ and ‘end’ can be sensibly described. This raises a point of subtle linguistic significance. Logically speaking, to say that the Law of Entropy applies everywhere, i.e., ‘universally’, is not the same as saying that it applies everywhere all at the same time. Things change, develop and decay, universally, that’s true, but there is no way in which, while some of its parts may change, ‘the universe as a whole’ has to change in any similar, all-over way. This makes it meaningless to enquire as to how, as a ‘Whole’, it all ‘starts’ and ‘ends’ when there is no ‘Whole’, of the classical God’s-eye-view sort, that it makes sense to speak of.

    By default, then, the ‘universe’, however we may choose to conceive it, has to remain in an overall-conserved, non-local steady state, with entropy, or decay in one locality compensated by negentropy, or growth, in another $^{[130]}$.

    12.9. The ‘Creation’ versus ‘Evolution’ fiasco

    As we see, then, without any meaningful explanation as to how an overall-conserved and balanced system of angular momentum can, as a whole, ‘start’ and ‘end’, the idea of a ‘Universal Creation’ or the ‘Birth of the Cosmos’ is as meaningless as that of an all-over cosmic heat death. Also, any notion of ‘continuous creation’ is a non-starter (with apologies to Bondi, Gold and Hoyle $^{[131]}$). Conserved quantities remain conserved, which means, by definition, that they can be neither created nor destroyed. Without any valid reasoning to the contrary, therefore, it has to be accepted that if the law of conservation of angular momentum is true, then there cannot be any cosmic beginning or end.

    Aside from all the tortuous theories of cosmic beginnings and ends, such as the ‘Big Bang’, etc., the fact remains, throughout, that as entropy applies in one place, it is balanced by negentropy in another. This is what is generally observed in nature, not only in the phenomenon of life and death on this planet but also in what we know of the lives and deaths of stars and galaxies: as one star or galaxy dies, another, somewhere else, is born and develops. In that same way, a whole human society or animal species may evolve into pre-eminence in one epoch only to fall into extinction in another as, eminently, in the case of the dinosaurs or the rise and fall of the Roman Empire. To accept this principle of life and death (evolution and decay) as a universal fact of nature is one thing. To extend that same principle to everything, everywhere all at once – that is, ‘universally’ – especially when, as we have seen, there is no overall instant or ‘Unity’ that can be defined – is something else entirely. It is, to say the very least, no more than unwarranted, fanciful assumption.

    Logically speaking, then, accepting that life and death is a universal phenomenon is not the same as accepting that the universe itself is subject to life and death. It is like saying that because all white things, universally, are white, the universe itself is also white $^{[132]}$. This makes the ‘Evolution-versus-Creation’ controversy a dismal mistake, a running sore in our social history. Does life evolve or was it created? Quite evidently, it evolves; the evidence is incontrovertible. From where, then, did life evolve? Did it begin, like everything else, in the ‘Big Bang’ as supporters of that hypothesis would have us believe? Or was there a moment in a lifeless universe when, among some set of completely random events, a fortuitous accident took place, leading to that negentropic process of evolution we call life? But in a completely random situation, what can possibly be meant by ‘fortuity’? That would be tacitly to assume that there is some miraculous spiritual agency hanging around, poised to stamp ‘fortuitous’ or ‘significant’ on any such accident, should it ever occur. But, again, to believe in such an agency is scarcely distinguishable from outright religion hence is hypocritical in its declared antagonism towards the same. (Apologies to the well-known ‘atheist’, Professor Richard Dawkins.)

    It remains true, then, that life may evolve in one place while in another it is created. Hence, to accept that life both evolves and is created but not all at the same time everywhere involves no contradiction whatsoever. Evolution and Creation are not contradictory. To put this in a practical context: a farmer who ploughs a field and plants seeds creates, in a deliberate pre-planned, negentropic act, the conditions for next year’s harvest. In similar fashion, a mother who selects and purchases the ingredients for her child’s birthday cake deliberately creates the conditions for that cake to materialise in time for the party. Without that sort of deliberate planning activity, on the part of the farmer and the mother, neither the crop nor the cake would ever have appeared. And in the same way that when the mother entrusts her cake mixture to the eminently random heat and chemical processes in the oven to evolve into the cake that she has intended, so the farmer consigns his seeds to the equally random processes of the elements, trusting them to evolve into his anticipated crop. In none of these sorts of natural instances, do ‘creation’ and ‘evolution’ come anywhere near being in logical conflict. From the holistic standpoint of Normal Realism, then, any notion that ‘Life as a Whole’ can emerge, monolithically from chaos is nonsense. So is any idea that life can be created, either locally or universally, from purely ‘fortuitous’ interactions among essentially blind and intentionless ‘material particles’, without any built-in pre-existing premium or potentiality for it to do so, is to assume something every bit as miraculous as that the whole universe was produced in a single overall magical act of Creation. In all the possible factual examples that can be produced, concepts of ‘creation’, ‘evolution’, ‘randomness’, ‘fortuity’, ‘intention’ and so on can – and quite evidently, do – co-exist in nature. The only conflict occurs is when any one of these concepts is dogmatically universalised, that is, extended in a wholly presumptuous ‘God’s-eye-view’ way, to encompass Everything all together.

    That is by no means the sort of ‘holism’ that Normal Realism espouses. Like all forms of phenomenalism, Normal Realism is empirical. It recognises what is perfectly obvious, that some things are created while others are destroyed, all in that overall balance of things we customarily call Nature. And if we describe the angular momentum nexus as ‘universally’ conserved, then it cannot be by presuming some overall ‘God’s-eye-view’ of the whole, as in classical physics, but by employing the word ‘universal’ in the normal sense according to which the conservation law applies in all instances but not everywhere, all over, at the same time. This is to employ language in the correct commonsense way, as advised by Austin, Ryle and Wittgenstein, et al., rather than in over-specialised usage.

    The more settled a concept system becomes, of course, the more it establishes itself as the way things are, as if written in the rocks by nature itself, and in contrast to which any other concept system that may be different is regarded as foreign or alien. Nevertheless, it cannot be doubted that every such concept-template or language system is finite, hence is always revisable in principle in the light of new knowledge. Essential to the efficacy of any language system, therefore, is the realisation that no such system is ever sufficient unto itself, that it always depends upon something beyond itself, as a kind of perfection, or truth, outside and beyond it but towards which (ideally) it aspires. This ideal towards which language evolves may be regarded as the unadulterated ‘language of nature itself’, which the Greeks called logos. This was regarded as superhuman, simply in the secular sense of being a truth which transcends that of human language and towards which our finite language, if it remains healthy enough, evolves. Inevitably, perhaps, this transcendental character of logos has come to be personified. For instance, in the Gospel of St. John, it is personified as the Christ of the Christian religion $^{[133]}$. Regardless, however, of whether or not that logos is personified, deified or regarded in some other religious light, the fact remains that, on the secular level, it is a standard of correct informational interpretation of phenomena towards which our language – our physics language, in particular – naturally aspires. Its existence as an end-aim of human language is signified by the fact that errors in our interpretations of natural phenomena can be detected and logically rectified, a process of elimination which inevitably leads towards a goal of perfect correspondence with natural reality.

    This (secular) logos, whose nature is fundamentally the same as that of the language we use for communication and social awareness, is, no more nor less, what philosophers like Socrates, Plato, Aristotle, et al. called truth, as opposed to mere opinion. The younger contemporary of Socrates, the sophist Protagoras, dismissed any idea of truth, his teaching being that there was ‘no such thing as truth, only opinion.’ To this, one only had to ask: ‘Is that view true or is it merely an opinion?’ If it is no more than opinion, then it does not have to be accepted; and if it is true, then truth exits and the challenge is false. The fact that truth exits is therefore undeniable.

    This, of course, is regardless of whether we know that truth or not. What we do know for certain is that there is truth to be uncovered, if only in a gradual, probing (i.e., empirical) way. What, then, is truth? What can it be but what remains when we get rid of error, which is, of course, much easier to determine than the truth. This is why there have been ‘truth-chasers’ and intellectual adventurers throughout history, such as Socrates and Galileo to name but two who refused to succumb to the sloth of general sophistry.

    12.10. The true Unknown

    A recognition of the finitude of human perception and language also implies, of course, the recognition of a residual class of phenomena that has to exist, albeit in a form that is presently unknown to us. In saying, then, that in Normal Realism all reality is directly accessible and ‘up front’, by no means denies that there are things in nature that are presently unknown and even, in some cases, that we may never know, either individually or corporately. This is the category of ‘the so-far-unknown’, of things that lie beyond that hill, around that bend or farther out than that galaxy; things inside that rock or living cell. These things which are not directly known may nevertheless be reasonable subjects of commonsense conjecture, or theory. Indeed, without such theories no true science could ever survive, let alone exist.

    These perfectly necessary theories are about what might conceivably be studied and examined as all part of the norm of scientific and philosophical research. What is not necessary to this research are theories of the metaphysical sort which postulate the existence of things whose defining properties and qualities are predicated in terms which bear no direct relation to the language of ordinary perception and experience. Theories such as that of the ‘Big Bang’, ‘Black Holes’, etc. fall into that category. So do theories of the invisible and intangible ‘field-forces’ of ‘gravitation’, ‘wave-particles’, ‘photons’, ‘wormholes into other universes’, ‘strings’ and ‘superstrings’, ‘tachyons’, ‘antimatter’, ‘the æther’, and the inscrutable miscalled ‘colours’ and other quasi attributes of the purely theoretical and entirely mystical ‘quarks’. These are just a few examples of the theoretical chimeras which language creates if unchecked but which it cannot logically sustain. These are the false unknowns, the realm not of the unknown but of the logically unknowable.

    Unfortunately, this false realm of theoretical vanity affords a playground for intellectuals who are keen to display their mental dexterity by claiming knowledge of things that defeat all attempts at logical comprehension. That there are such people and that they have admirers and followers is simply a fact of life. A prime example of this presumptuous intellectualism is that of the religious authorities of the Middle Ages, who sought to coerce the populace into religious conformity by means of mystification and superstition, reinforced by their Inquisitions and instruments of ‘correction’ and terror. Such compulsions towards conformity could not, of course, be tolerated nowadays – or could they?

    12.11. The role of commonsense

    Perhaps the final test of what truly counts as a realistic explanation of physical phenomena is to think of what might be accepted as such in an ordinary court of law. How would it be, say, for a murderer to claim innocence on the grounds of mechanical determinism? (‘It wasn’t my fault, my atoms did it.’) Or for a defence lawyer to propose that when his client committed the crime it had taken place in just one of many ‘parallel universes’, while in the majority of his other lives his client might be innocent and of good character? How else would these claims be reported in the press but as judiciary ‘howlers’?

    So why should it be any different with the glut of fantastic ideas that are presented to us nowadays in the popular-science media, ideas to which the general public would never, soberly, under oath and directed by a competent judge, assign the slightest credibility? What possible ‘evidence’ could be presented in support of them that would make any sense to a jury in terms of common experience? It is to these ordinary, intelligent and critical thinkers, the sort who are called upon to apply forensic reasoning in the solving of crimes and to serve on juries in the best traditions of democratic justice, that Normal Realism presents its case.

    Throughout this book, we have shown how Normal Realism seeks the simplest possible realistic explanation of physical phenomena in the tradition of Ockham’s Razor. This includes replacing the misguided concept of ‘light-speed’ with that of a ‘dimensional constant’ c and the equally non-empirical explanation of orbital motion in terms of an in vacuo ‘gravitational force’ with conservation of angular momentum as presented in POAMS. We believe that the more complicated a theory is regarding natural phenomena, the less likely it is to be a true description. It is human nature for people to sometimes make theories needlessly complicated in order to enhance their reputation and career. Nature, on the other hand, has no such hidden agenda and we believe that, at its heart, it is ultimately simple to explain. For example, the equivalence of mass and energy was heralded as a great discovery of Special Relativity, but is this result so surprising? As shown in Appendix 1, our Normal Realist approach derives the standard time dilation formula of Special Relativity using simply Pythagoras’s Theorem applied to three time measures. Since the dimensional constant $c$ converts distance measures into time measures, and since Pythagoras’s Theorem is used, a factor of $c^2$ appears in the time dilation formula. This formula, in turn, can be used to give a straightforward derivation of the equivalence of mass and energy expressed as $e = mc^2$, the details of which are provided in Appendix 3. It follows that the equivalence of mass and energy can be explained simply in terms of the nature of time.

    In the past, it was religious superstition that was to be challenged by commonsense. For example, Socrates appealed to his Greek contemporaries to abandon their traditional faith in their mythical ‘gods’ and rely, instead, on their own commonsense-logical perceptions and judgements to interpret the World around them. This seriously upset the sophistic status quo to the extent that Socrates was eventually executed for his pains. What is certain, nowadays, is that modern Theoretical Physics and Cosmology have already gone far beyond the possibility of any truly commonsense comprehension – some physicists even boast about it [134]. This is to an extent that can scarcely be matched by any other mythology, past or present, even that of the Hindu cosmology in which the Earth was assumed to be a disc supported by four elephants standing on the back of a turtle swimming in an infinite sea, as depicted on the cover of this book. Compared to the present dogma of the ‘Big Bang’, Hindu cosmology, with its fabulously bejewelled temples built for the worship of the Turtle God, seems almost sensible. Similarly, on the scale of sheer magnificence and expense, surely the Large Hadron Collider (LHC) at CERN beats all! Who would dare to stand before it and declare it to be, in their own honestly considered view, simply a piece of useless junk, with no more prospect of achieving one of its declared aims of recreating the Creation than if it were designed to discover the ultimate particle of ectoplasm? One thinks of how, with advancing knowledge, those ancient temples collapsed into ruins. One also thinks of the bits of expensive hardware which were designed to detect ‘gravity waves’, ‘gravitons’ and so on, but which now litter the long grass at the backs of magnificent science laboratories. This reflects the point made by Margaret Thatcher in her speech to the Royal Society, on September 27th, 1988 where she stated:

    ‘… no nation has unlimited funds, and it will have even less if it wastes them. A commitment to basic science cannot mean a blank cheque for everyone with — if I may put it colloquially — a bee in his bonnet. That would spread the honey too thinly.’

    One wonders, also, how long it will be before the sleeping giant of commonsense will wake to the realisation of the intellectual and financial extravagances that are now being perpetrated in the name of Modern Physics and Cosmology.

    CHAPTER 13

    Humanitarian Implications

    13.1. The conflict between atomism and religion in Western Science

    All human societies, past and present, have been based on what might broadly be termed the ‘science’ of their day. The Cynics, Stoics, Skeptics, Epicureans, etc., of the Hellenistic period of Greek philosophy (323-146 BC) and the Romantics of 18$^{th}$ century European culture, were partakers, in one way or another, of the atomistic legacy of Democritus and Leucippus. All these social and ethical philosophies were concerned with human virtue in the face of what they conceived as those mindless atomic mechanisms which they regarded as, unquestionably, the basis of all nature. In our modern Western culture, that Democritan/Cartesian schism between things mental and things material remains more or less unchanged. It has bequeathed to our Western society a chronic problem, which is that its moral philosophy and its materialistic science simply do not gel. The reason, according to the main thread of that materialistic science, is that none of those value systems that are so essential to the humanities exists in the world of the mechanically determinate motions of material atoms in the emptiness of space that Democritus envisioned. A purely mechanical, deterministic philosophy removes all responsibility for human action, and where there is no responsibility, there can be no question of morality; and without morality what can religion be but, at best, a mess of fragmented superstitions and dogmas. So, in view of its atomistic, or materialist underpinnings, when our Western society is described as ‘Christian’, that is altogether equivocal. It signifies a condition analogous to what psychiatrists call ‘split personality disorder’ with ‘atheistic’ Science on the one hand and Religion on the other, with ‘never the twain shall meet’.

    13.2. The ‘God of the Scientists’

    It seems inevitable, then, that any society based on a philosophy of atomic materialism without any coherent view as to how that society and its individuals relate together within the wider natural scheme of things, even though it may become materially replete is, at root, morally and ethically moribund. Such has been the case with those above-mentioned communities in the past, whose designs for society were based on that same Democritan atomism. In all these cases, the problem was that of how to fit human values into a world of blind, aimless and uncaring atoms. Every one of those communities tacitly espoused a policy of romantic desperation which regarded all human value-systems as pure ephemera, as fleeting and insignificant as the lives of mayflies. What else, they felt, could humanity be but some kind of tragic accident of circumstance, in which all our human values are no more than what Democritus regarded as mere ‘conventions’. These human ‘conventions’ were thought of as confined to the time-span of human mortality, fading into oblivion at either end. In no way could these human values be regarded as cosmic verities. So far as these thinkers were concerned, all notions of universal Good and Evil, Justice and the like, without which no society can function, were no more than epiphenomena.

    What alternative, then, has there been for our Western culture, based on that same Democritan ethic, but to follow in the wake of those post-Democritan policies, either of despair or dogged belief in some figment of the imagination – or, of course, of glorious anarchistic freedom? (‘God is dead’, declared Nietzsche, ‘so everything is possible’ (allowable)). As for Christian religion, how is anyone supposed to pray to, or in any other way communicate with, a mindless Machine? The only deity that can be conceived in those mechanistic terms is what has been called ‘the God of the Scientists’, at best a great and purely amoral Machine Designer, whose works may be admired and worshipped for their own sake, regardless of that deity’s complete indifference towards humanity as such. This deterministic ‘God of the Scientists’ as the traditional object of belief was well expressed in Einstein’s reported notoriously deistic statement, ‘God does not play dice.’

    Typically, our modern society hovers uneasily between these two stools of rational science and irrational belief. It has bequeathed to us the same policy of despair as that on which those earlier societies were founded. Suicides and drug misuses abound among young people who either cannot discern any moral compass-direction for their lives or else are prepared to sacrifice themselves for ideals that are purely irrational and emotional. They simply cannot face what they are conditioned, mistakenly, to think of as ‘brute reality’. Nowhere can they find any rational consensus as to what we are doing here or why. With this nihilistic conditioning, we can scarcely imagine, nowadays, any prospect of a society in which those two sectors, Science and Humanities, might be merged in such a way as completely to support each other in social harmony.

    Nevertheless, a physical science founded on direct visual and other sensory-instrumental information – that is to say, natural phenomena – instead of atomic mechanisms does not have the same dire implications for humanity as does the mechanistic science of Democritus. As we say, for religious people, it makes more sense, surely, to worship and pray to an information-transacting deity than to some cosmic automaton. Also, to contemplate a world of nature which is structured along the same logical lines as those we experience directly in terms of our language and thought should surely be less hostile to religion than something to which our human language is completely alien – namely the theoretical language of ‘fermions’, ‘bosons’, ‘quarks’ ‘God particles’ ... etc. Indeed, it might truly be said that, in contrast to this jargonistic language, in our very best and most efficient uses of finite language we partake, directly, in the infinite, universal language of nature which the Greeks called logos.

    Now, whether or to what extent our ideas of truth, justice, beauty and our humane values in general have any similar connection with that same logos, is a moot point. However, it is difficult to think that in any other society there might be, here or out in space, that in any system of language whatsoever, logical truth could be radically different from what we know of it here on Earth. To argue that in the nature of things there can exist totally different kinds of basic logical truth seems entirely bizarre – especially if that view is claimed to be true. The same goes for basic fairness and justice, as these have been defined in successful societies, as witnessed by the democratic institutions of Golden-Age Greece, the British Justice system and the American Constitution. For all their various cultural differences and shortcomings (for instance the fact of the Greek system’s dependence on slave labour), these societies are all objectively centred on the same basic theme of fairness and justice for all. And could there possibly be any society, anywhere, which cannot distinguish between order and chaos, regarding the one as beautiful and the other as ugly – in different lexicons, of course? That seems hardly likely. Nor does there appear to be any contradiction in supposing that what we experience directly as conscious awareness is, to some extent or another, an integral characteristic of the life-principle in general. It seems no more likely that oneself is the one and only manifestation of consciousness than that the only manifestation of life in the universe is as we experience it here on this tiny planet. Moreover, the tendency to personify and even deify this logos, under its various names, has been with mankind since time immemorial. Is that, perhaps, yet another cosmic principle among such highly developed organisms? On what logical grounds can any of us confidently exclude that possibility?

    Aside from the issue of theology and religion, the hinge-pin of this revolutionary information-based alternative to atomic materialism is the conceptual flipover from our peculiarly mechanistic traditional way of thinking of light as a spatial intermediary between the physical object and the observer, to thinking of it as the direct informational root-source of all our knowledge of nature. The wider implications for physics, of that switch, as we have seen, are profound. For instance, not only does it greatly simplify the mathematics of Einstein’s Special Relativity, but also, in the relativistic philosophy of Einstein’s tutor and critic, Mach, it provides a firm grounding for that otherwise philosophically rootless theory of Einstein’s. Moreover, the notoriously inhibiting ‘EPR’ conflict between Einstein and Bohr over whether action-at-a-distance is instantaneous or time-delayed finally dissolves when it is clearly demonstrated that quantum instantaneity and relativistic time-delay, far from being antagonistic to each other are actually complementary, as inseparable components of the same action. This normalised, or logicalised, way of thinking also offers the prospect of curbing the worst excesses of our presently fevered Theoretical Physics and Cosmology by an injection of ordinary humane commonsense. This prescription for relieving modern language of its stultifying weight of sheer jargon in favour of ordinary logic would surely end the Intellectual Apartheid that nowadays pertains between commonsense and our Modern Physics, now grandiloquently named ‘Big Science’.

    As for our Western culture in general, this flipover from a mechanics-based to an information-based approach to natural and social philosophy should do much towards curing our society’s present dysfunctional split between what are regarded as ‘Idealists with feet planted firmly in mid-air’ and the ‘Materialists with heads planted firmly in the ground.’ Without some such radical revision in our customary ways of thinking, our ideas of a properly functioning society could scarcely be ever global.

    CHAPTER 14

    Conclusion

    14.1. Prospects of reception of the ‘Flipside Paradigm’

    Undoubtedly, what militates most against the arguments put forward in this dissertation is our modern Educational divide between Arts and Science – between Philosophy and Physics in particular. No longer do we have that combination of the two which used to be called Natural Philosophy. Instead, the names ‘Natural’ and ‘Philosophy’ in that title have separated-out into Science and Arts to become the two distinct and mutually hostile academic disciplines of Physics and Philosophy as listed in most of the Educational curricula today.

    But why the hostility? The reason is that by the 19$^{th}$ century, Natural Philosophy had become an almost completely scholastic preoccupation with centuries-old and seemingly insoluble dilemmas over such things as the Problem of Knowledge, or Problem of Perception, Idealism versus Realism, and so on. Impatient with this ‘purist’, or ‘scholastic’ preoccupation with the subject, which they regarded as effete, the more practically-minded and ‘hard-nosed’ among those philosophers separated from the others to carry on in a go-it-alone manner under the banner of what became known as Physics. This became a distinct discipline in its own right, with its own departmental administration and eventually its own separate buildings, with there being no comparable department – far less any comparable dedicated building – of Natural Philosophy as such. Hence, these days students are, in effect, conditioned into thinking of Physics as one thing and Philosophy as something entirely different.

    As a mature-age entrant to university the present co-author, Pope, had not been particularly concerned with this academic distinction. He had simply a driving curiosity about nature in all its academically unclassified manifestations. He was what he describes as a natural ‘truth-chaser’, a free-range thinker – or, had he known it at the time – what was once called a ‘Natural Philosopher’ or uncommitted, non-partisan philosopher of Nature. By the time he went to university he had already formulated the basis of his philosophical approach to nature-in-the-round, and after graduating he saw it as an opportunity to develop these ideas under academic supervision with the help, as he supposed, of experts in the subject. This, as it transpired, was not to be. For the sake of academic propriety his natural binocular view of reality had to be cancelled in favour of a choice of, as it were, which eye to look through. If he wished for any academic support for his ideas then he would have to decide which of those two monocular approaches he would take. It had to be the one or the other, never both.

    When he presented this binocular cross-disciplinary thesis for examination in pursuit of a post-graduate Master’s degree in Philosophy of Science at university, Pope was told that the ideas he had developed extramurally, prior to university – indeed, the very reason why he had agreed to go to university in the first place – would have to be dropped if he wished to become a card-carrying academic. ‘Philosophers,’ he was informed, ‘should not even suggest to Physicists what they should or should not do’. He was told, quite categorically, that if he persisted against this advice his thesis would surely fail – which it did, inevitably, as forewarned [135].

    From this and other events that followed, it became plain that in modern Higher Education, the departments of Physics and Philosophy have to be kept strictly apart. At a Physics conference, in North York University, Toronto, on being told that the next speaker, Pope, was a Philosopher, one of the delegates ostentatiously stood up and walked out. He was later reported as saying to a fellow conference-member and friend, ‘As soon as he said he was a Philosopher, I got up and left’ [136]. (He didn’t wait to hear that this ‘Philosopher’ had, mostly, an Engineering background.) At Swansea University, during the highpoint of the interdepartmental tension between Physics and Philosophy, two members of the respective departments, were found fighting in the foyer of the University Library – such was the academic separation between Physics and Philosophy, throughout the 1960s.

    Nevertheless, the author of this Normal Realist thesis persisted in his dogged attempt at reversing the academic severance of Physics from Philosophy, so as to recover the integrity, as he saw it, of what was, originally, Natural Philosophy. This enterprise has continued and been developed more or less extramurally over the almost forty years since the thesis was first presented to a university in 1972-73. Since 1982, this has been developed further, as a university research project in collaboration with the second co-author of this book, Dr. Anthony Osborne, of the Mathematics Department of Keele University, UK. It is now more than sixty years since all this began in a short extramural correspondence between the young, non-academic telephone lineman, Pope and Professor Albert Einstein [137].

    Allied to this problem of the Educational apartheid is, no doubt, the intrinsic difficulty of coping with the suggestion of a complete change of mind-set towards a whole new and unfamiliar way of thinking, even though this has been suggested by more and more physicists, including, not least, the aforementioned NASA spokesman, John Anderson. To adopt this Normal Realist paradigm flip-over, if only for serious consideration as a contender for this anticipated ‘New Physics’, and maintain it free of interference from the pressure of habitual precepts and conventional language, seems to be the most difficult part of the mental challenge it presents (see Chapter 9, section 9.4.). This is, in some ways, more of a psychological or, perhaps, a psycho-social problem than a logical one. Any prospect of overturning more than two thousand years of scientific habit will no doubt be impossible for some – perhaps even a majority – of good people to contemplate. Yet that challenge remains. It is doubtless unthinkable that this proposed logical revolution, initiated by Berkeley and modernised for this 21$^{st}$ century by Pope’s Neo-Machian Normal Realism, might become global in the foreseeable future. However, that in itself does not dispose of the intrinsic validity of the argument. For anyone who is not entirely mired-in with our present social and political conventions, this logical alternative is there for the taking. Like mountains, it is simply there to be explored.

    This suggested enterprise, however, cannot be dismissed as entirely trivial. For instance, let us recall what Russell wrote in his History of Western Philosophy:

    ‘The philosophy appropriate to quantum theory…has not yet been adequately developed. I suspect that it will demand even more radical departures from the traditional doctrine of space and time than those demanded by the theory of relativity.’

    Also let us remind ourselves of what was said by John Anderson, Spokesman for NASA’s Jet Propulsion Laboratory (JPL):

    ‘In the unlikely event that there is new physics, one does not want to miss it because one had the wrong mind-set.’

    The present ‘mind-set’, in the almost seventy years following what Russell says about quantum physics is, in fact, the sense of desperation among physicists faced with what senior science writer Robert Roy Britt describes as the

    ‘…nagging suspicion that … a constant and consistent presence (we have) come to understand and rely on, (isn’t) what it seemed. That’s how scientists feel when they ponder gravity these days.’

    So it seems that both our contemporary Quantum Theory and theory of gravity are in chronic disarray. This, Britt says,

    ‘… leaves open staggering possibilities that would force wholesale reprinting of all physics books.’

    In this book, these worries have been conscientiously addressed, not by the usual cobbling together of ad hoc ideas for piecemeal solutions to these particular problems but by partaking of a legacy provided by an altogether different tradition of lateral thinking that has been developed over more than three centuries. This is the illustrious history of the much-misunderstood English Empiricism, or observational relativism, initiated by George Berkeley and carried through into this present century in the Neo-Machian form of Normal Realism. It is an alternative mind-set which, perhaps, has been, as it were, waiting in the wings to come on stage to play its part in the next Act in the drama of Progressive Science.

    What this book presents is a systematic unified and logically connected approach to scientific understanding with the provenance of centuries of well-thought-out philosophy behind it. This is not so much a ‘new physics’ as a whole new unified way of thinking about nature conceived in the grand classical manner of Natural Philosophy. If this logical reconciliation of those ideas which have led to the conflict between contemporary Quantum Theory and Relativity is not the answer to the call for a ‘New Physics’, then the reader must ask himself, ‘What is?’ And if this philosophical proposal should prove to be the answer, then, surely, to echo the sentiments of NASA’s John Anderson, we should not miss it because we are over-attached to the wrong mind-set.

    However, it gives pause for thought that even in response to calls from Anderson and his colleagues for suggested ideas on the subject of the anomalous trajectories of the NASA space probes, all attempts to apprise them of the existence of this proposed simple solution of those anomalies described in this book (see Appendix 6) have met with a monolithic lack of response. This is despite automatic acknowledgements of receipt of the messages. Moreover, some earlier references to it listed on the relevant websites have since disappeared.

    APPENDIX 1

    Time Dilation

    A1.1. The Pythagorean time equation

    Let the intrinsic spectral period of a particular atom be denoted by $t$, and let that period relative to an observer be denoted by $t_{\mathrm{R}}$. The distance in metres between that atom and the observer is then denoted by $s$.

    As we already know, in Normal Realism, this distance $s$ in metres, divided by the constant $c$ is a time in seconds, where $c$ is a dimensional constant, not a 'velocity'. Since these measures, $t$, $t_{\mathrm{R}}$ and $s/c$ in the diagram are all in the same units (seconds), they can be represented dimensionally in the orthogonal (rectangular) way [138].

    Unlike the traditional scalar graph of uniform motion, in the diagram on the right the length $t_{\mathrm{R}}$, of the atom's motion vector is, by Pythagoras' Theorem, the geometrical resultant of the two components, $t$ and $s/c$, viz.:

    $$ (t_{\mathrm{R}})^2 = (s/c)^2 + t^2. \tag{1} $$

    This simple formula gives precisely the same value of time stretching ('dilation') as that given by Einstein's formula for that same relativistic time dilation. This can be seen if we substitute for the distance $s$ in (1) the product $vt_{\mathrm{R}}$, where $v$ is the relative, or observed, speed and then simplify by ordinary rules of algebra. This produces the standard equation which appears in Special Relativity:

    $$ t_{\mathrm{R}} = t/\sqrt{[1 - (v^2/c^2)]}. \tag{2} $$

    It is to be noted that since the squaring of the ‘velocity’ term in the equation makes it uniformly positive it makes no difference as to the direction of the motion. This means that when one body travels away from another, as in the ‘twins’ example (Chapter 7, section 7.4.), the time dilation is route-independent, being the same on the return leg of the journey as on the outward leg.

    A1.2. Proper time and relative time: the EPR non-paradox.

    Note that in the above Pythagorean time equation (1), when the proper time (intrinsic time) $t$, of an interaction over the distance $s$ is zero (i.e., instantaneous), the relative or observational time, $t_{\mathrm{R}}$, is the delayed time $s/c$. In other words, the finite ‘speed $c$’ and the infinite ‘speed’ of the interaction over the distance $s$ are no more than complementary aspects – the ‘heads and tails’, as it were – of the same distant interaction.

    APPENDIX 2

    The Spectral Bar Code of Balmer and Rydberg

    Hydrogen Absorption Spectrum

    Hydrogen Emission Spectrum

    Plate 6: The Hydrogen Colour Spectrum

    In the picture above we see the distribution of discrete lines in the visible range of the spectrum of atomic hydrogen. This is, in effect, the identificatory ‘bar code’ of that element.

    The formula for the distribution of lines in that spectral bar code was first produced by Johann Balmer. It was based on the method of pure trial and error, and is as follows:

    $$f = c R (1 - 1 / n ^ {2}), \tag {1}$$

    where $f$ is frequency and $n$ is the sequence of integers starting with $n = 2$. The coefficient $R$ is known as the Rydberg frequency constant and represents the natural limiting frequency. When multiplied by Planck's constant, $h$, $cR$ gives a measure of energy. The approximate value of $hcR$ is $2.179907344 \times 10^{-18}$ joule.

    This formula was later expanded by Johannes Rydberg to include other hydrogen-like spectra of atomic number $N$, where $N$ is an integer. The Balmer-Rydberg formula for the spectrum thus becomes:

    $$ f = N^2 c R \left(1 - 1/n^2\right). $$

    In this formula, a whole sequence of intermediate values of integers $n$ gives:

    $$ f = N^2 c R \left[ \left(1/n_1^2\right) - \left(1/n_2^2\right) \right], \tag{2} $$

    where $n_1$ is the ‘fixed’ and $n_2$ the ‘running’ term. With $n_1 = 2$ and $n_2 = 3, 4, 5 \ldots$ etc., the formula gives precisely the series of spectral frequencies for hydrogen-like atoms. With $N = 1$, $n_1 = 2$, the formula gives the Lyman series of the hydrogen spectrum. For $N = 1$ and $n_1 = 1$, the formula produces the further series of Paschen, Brackett, Wunt, and so on.

    APPENDIX 3

    The Spectral Bar Code of Normal Realism

    It can be shown that the formulae (1) and (2) for atomic frequencies produced by Balmer and Rydberg by methods of trial and error given in Appendix 2 can be deduced directly and logically from the relativistic time dilation formula given in Appendix 1. That formula is:

    $$ t_{\mathrm{R}} = t / \sqrt{[1 - (v^2/c^2)]}. \tag{1} $$

    Assuming that $t_{\mathrm{R}}$ and $t$ can take only discrete values, it follows that $t_{\mathrm{R}}/t = n/N$ for certain integers (whole numbers) $n$ and $N$. This means that we can rewrite (1) as the following:

    $$ n^2/N^2 = 1 / [1 - (v^2/c^2)]. \tag{2} $$

    Now the potential energy, $e$, is given by $e = mv^2$, where $m$ is a mass and $v$ is its relative speed, so that (2) becomes:

    $$ n^2/N^2 = 1 / [1 - e/(mc^2)], \tag{3} $$

    and so, rearranging this formula, we have

    $$ e = mc^2(1 - N^2/n^2). \tag{4} $$

    Dividing the energy, $e$, by Planck’s constant, $h$, expresses frequency $f$ and $mc^2/h$ is the natural limit of frequency, $cR$, where $R$ is the Rydberg frequency constant. Hence, dividing (4) by $h$ and letting $N = 1$ produces Balmer’s equation (1) in Appendix 2. Including intermediate values for the integers $n$ then gives the more general Balmer-Rydberg equation (2) of Appendix 2.

    Note that for $n$ at theoretical infinity in (4) the equation expresses what appears to be the standard mass-energy relation

    $$ e = mc^2, \tag{5} $$

    which is the usual well-known formula for interconverting mass and energy. However the way in which this formula (5) is derived is radically different from the usual way of standard orthodox Relativity. This is not only because it is deduced from an entirely different, more primitive source, namely, the theorem of Pythagoras, along an altogether unconventional route of pure syllogistic. It is also because, whereas in the conventional formula the range of values is continuous, the values in our equation (5) are quantised in the discrete fractional amounts shown on the right-hand side of equation (4).

    Comparing (4) with Balmer’s equation, and knowing the values of the constants used in these equations, enables us to deduce the value of $m$ in (4) as $N \times 2.425473 \times 10^{-35}$ kg. This is the mass equivalent of the so-called ‘photon’ (in our nomenclature, a light quantum or photon), which is the maximum amount of mass an atom (quantum accumulator) may shed or absorb in the form of light-energy in its interactions with other atoms. For intermediate values of $n$, with the ordinal atomic number $N = 1$, we have the various normal spectral barcodes given by the Balmer and other formulae. However, it follows that for atoms of very large ordinal number $N$, such as radium ($N = 88$) and plutonium ($N = 94$) their inter-atomic exchanges of mass-energy, commonly known as ‘radiation’, become increasingly volatile – or ‘dangerous’, so far as life is concerned. Needless to say, in these higher-order atoms, with large numbers of electrons, the simplicity of the above formulae is replaced by much more complicated formulae, so that these simple formulae hold only for hydrogen-like ions, that is, atoms denuded of all but one of their electrons.

    Changing the value of $N$ signifies a whole new range of inter-atomic mass (energy) exchanges which are many orders of magnitude greater than for hydrogen-like ions, in the range we call ‘nuclear’. This is where whole atoms fall apart (in the process known as fission) because the transferred masses (nucleons), cannot fit comfortably into stable arrangements, which means that some or more nucleons have to be violently rejected (emitted) as subatomic particles of one denomination or another. To discuss these nuclear processes in detail, however, falls outside the remit of this present book. For present purposes, let it be noted simply that all these phenomena, regardless of how complex they appear, analyse out, ultimately, into numbers of irreducible units of angular momentum $\hbar$ and their interactions with one another in those quantum action-units $h$ in the way that has been described in this book.

    APPENDIX 4

    The Orbital Dynamics of Normal Realism

    For simplicity, in what follows we shall consider angular momentum only for circular motion of a satellite of mass $m$ about a central body of mass $M$. In this case, the (orbital) angular momentum, $L$, of the satellite is given by $L = mvr$, where $v$ is the speed of the satellite and $r$ is the radial distance between the satellite and centre-of-mass of the central body. It follows by conservation of angular momentum alone that in a natural orbit [5],

    $$ v^2 = GM/r, \tag{1} $$

    where $G$ is the usual empirical factor commonly called ‘Newton’s Gravitational Constant’ which, like $c$, is a product of the arbitrary choice of conventional units of measure. Combining this equation with the expression for $L$ gives

    $$ L = mvr = GmM/v. \tag{2} $$

    Plainly, in this simple formula, given that the measures $L$, $m$, $M$, $G$ and hence $v$ are known, the orbital radius $r$ is given by $r = L/(mv)$. Angular momentum, then, is sufficient in itself to explain basic orbital motion with no need of ‘gravitation’ or any other similar in vacuo force. There is also a spin angular momentum associated with any spinning body. In POAMS, the spin angular momentum of a spinning satellite has to be taken into account in the equations of motion (see below and Appendix 5).

    Combining equation (1) with the time dilation formula, (2), of Appendix 1 gives the time dilation associated with natural circular motion:

    $$ t_R = t/\sqrt{[1 - (GM/(c^2r))]}, \tag{3} $$

    where $t$ is the proper time recorded by the satellite and $t_R$ is the delayed, observational time. This is the result referred to at beginning of Section 8.4 in Chapter 8.

    Now something needs to be said here about angular momentum as a vector quantity. A vector is simply a measure with both magnitude and direction. For example, ten miles per hour in a north-easterly direction is a vector, which makes it a velocity, as opposed to just a speed. Vectors can be added or subtracted according to a geometrical law. The magnitude of the sum of two vectors is the sum or difference of their magnitudes if they point, respectively, in the same or opposite directions. For example, a ship sailing due west against a tide flowing due east is slowed to a speed which is the magnitude of the difference between the velocity of the ship and the velocity of the tide. Similarly, the speed of a plane flying west with a following westward tailwind is the magnitude of the sum of the velocity of the plane and the velocity of the tailwind. However, if two vectors point in different directions, then their magnitudes are not simply added or subtracted. The angular composition of those vectors varies between the two angular extremes of $0^\circ$ and $180^\circ$.

    Angular momentum is such a vector quantity. Orbital and spin angular momenta therefore add and subtract in the same way as velocities. The total angular momentum of an orbiting particle is the sum of its orbital and spin angular momentum vectors. In POAMS, the angular momentum which determines the orbit radius is the total spin-plus-orbital angular momentum. It follows that we should not expect an orbiting body that is spinning, in whatever direction, to follow the same orbit as a non-spinning one. Since the total angular momentum must be conserved, any change in the spin angular momentum directly affects the (spin-less) orbital angular momentum. This means that a space-probe spinning in the same plane as its solar orbit, orbits either nearer to and faster around the Sun or further away and slower according to the magnitude and direction of its spin, when compared to a non-spinning probe. Explicitly, when the spin and orbital angular momentum vectors of the probe are in the same direction, the probe orbits closer to the barycentre, compared to the case when there is no spin. By that same token, when the orbital and spin angular momentum vectors of the probe are opposed, the probe orbits farther from the barycentre, compared to the case when there is no spin.

    APPENDIX 5

    Varying ‘G’

    In Appendix 4 we have the standard equation for simple circular orbital angular momentum:

    $$ L = m v r = G m M / v. \tag{1} $$

    Now recall that angular momentum is a conserved quantity. That being the case, it follows logically, as stated in Appendix 4, that for a given total angular momentum, the inclusion of spin angular momentum affects the orbital angular momentum. This fact can be incorporated in Eqn. (1) by allowing $G$ to vary. For example, let the angular momentum $L$ in (1) be equal to $\hbar$, the angular momentum of the electron in the standard Bohr model of the hydrogen atom [139]. Thus we have

    $$ G = \hbar v / (m M), \tag{2} $$

    where $v$ is the ‘orbital speed’ of the electron and $m$ and $M$ are, respectively, the masses associated with the electron and proton. Taking account of the orbital kinetic energy of the electron, including spin effects [95], gives the standard value of $v$ as

    $$ v = 2.1876907 \times 10^6 \, \mathrm{m \, s^{-1}}. $$

    Then equation (2) gives

    $$ G = 1.514 \times 10^{29} \, \mathrm{N \, m^2 \, kg^{-2}}. $$

    It follows from (1) that the orbit radius of this spinning electron is

    $$ r = L / m v = \hbar / m v = 5.2917706 \times 10^{-11} \, \mathrm{m}. $$

    The values of $r$ and $v$, respectively, are precisely the values for the ‘radius’ and ‘orbital speed’ of the electron in the Bohr atom.

    Clearly, then, the proportion of spin to orbital angular momentum for those micro-physical bodies conventionally called ‘electrons’ and ‘protons’ is extremely large. This is why the ‘orbit radii’ of these micro-physical particles are so much smaller and tighter than those of planets and satellites, for which the proportion of spin to orbital angular momentum is extremely small. It is also, of course, why the forces (real, measurable forces) required to change those ‘electronic’ orbits are so huge in comparison with those of so-called ‘gravitation’.

    None of these deductions in Normal Realism involves any mention of the in vacuo ‘field-forces’ of current convention. They are conducted in terms of definitions and empirical values entirely. Note also, that if it is assumed that the electron has no spin, then with G at its standard value of 6.673 × 10⁻¹¹ m³ kg⁻¹ s⁻², the orbit radius of the electron would be truly enormous (i.e., 1.2 × 10²⁹ metres). This is the distance from the barycentre at which the electron would orbit without its intrinsic spin. This would be with a tangential velocity of 9.64 × 10⁻³⁴ metres per second, which is virtually stationary [140].

    Now if the direction of the electron’s spin were reversed (i.e., vectorially opposite to that of its orbit) then assuming that the magnitudes of the spin and orbital angular momenta were the same, the two directions and magnitudes (vectors) would cancel, in which case there would be no angular momentum to separate the electron and proton, which would then put them into direct contact with each other. This, of course, represents the extreme situation where the two magnitudes are equal. However, this leads us to expect that between these two extremes of maximum and minimum directional effect, vectorial (i.e., angular) considerations must apply in accordance with the standard law of composition of vectors. By that same token, the same effects of vectorial orientation must apply to the orbit radii of comparatively large bodies such as planets and satellites, namely, that with various amounts of spin angular momentum and degrees of direction, the orbits of those bodies will be larger or smaller, together with appropriate changes in the value of G. This may be interpreted, in ordinary parlance, as the ‘elevation’ or ‘depression’ (repulsion or attraction) of those bodies in accordance with the value of G as compared with its standard textbook value. For instance, adding spin angular momentum to a particle, say, on the Earth’s equator, depending on its direction of travel, will increase or decrease its natural orbital speed relative to the Earth’s surface, making it weigh either more or less, depending on the direction of its motion. This also explains why, as spinning bodies, NASA space-probes veer ‘anomalously’ off their courses, which are customarily calculated in terms of the standard ‘gravitational constant’, G, which, traditionally, is the same for all bodies, whether spinning or non-spinning (see Appendix 6) [141].

    APPENDIX 6

    The Perplexing ‘Pioneer’ and ‘Missing Mass’ Anomalies: the Need for a New Physics

    In the online free encyclopedia, Wikipedia, in the section, ‘Unsolved Problems in Physics’, we read the question:

    ‘What causes the apparent residual Sunward acceleration of the Pioneer spacecraft?’

    ‘The Pioneer anomaly or Pioneer effect is the observed deviation from expectations of the trajectories of various unmanned spacecraft visiting the outer solar system, notably, Pioneers 10 and 11. Both spacecraft are escaping from the solar system, and are slowing down under the influence of the Sun’s gravity. Upon very close examination, however, they are slowing down slightly more than expected from influence of all known sources. The effect can be modelled as a slight additional acceleration towards the Sun.’

    ‘At present, there is no universally accepted explanation for this phenomenon; while it is possible that the explanation will be mundane – such as thrust from gas leakage – the possibility of unknown physics is also being considered.’

    John Anderson, Spokesman for NASA’s Jet Propulsion Laboratory (JPL)) writes as follows:

    ‘In the unlikely event that there is new physics, one does not want to miss it because one had the wrong mind-set.’ (Our underlining)

    Let us then explore the theoretical possibility of a ‘new physics’. What form might it take? POAMS proposes a very simple answer. All NASA’s probes spin. They have to in order to maintain the orientations of their antennae with regard to Earth. But since the speeds of these spinning probes are not nearly large enough to involve Relativity theory, it is the Newtonian theory which is used to track them. Typical of Newton’s theory, of course, is that it assumes an imaginary in vacuo force which Newton named ‘gravitation’ as being responsible for the orbital trajectories of bodies. Also typical of the Newtonian theory is that these intangible in vacuo gravitational forces act solely on a body’s mass, hence are supposed to be the same for orbiting bodies that spin as for bodies which do not.

    This poses a problem. All orbiting bodies have orbital angular momentum and according to the law of conservation of angular momentum, the total angular momentum of an orbiting body has to include any form of angular momentum, including its spin. This is the concept of angular momentum that POAMS embraces (see Appendices 4 and 5). However, this is eminently what Newtonian orbital dynamics does not do and this, in itself, is a rather conspicuous anomaly.
